To collide, or not to collide, that is the question - a survey
Florian Oschmann

Abstract




Introduction

A simple question is not always easy to answer. In this work, the leading question we want to investigate is the following:

Imagine you put a small ball in a glass of water, wait a little, does the ball eventually touch the ground?

Even a child would probably answer this with “yes, of course”, but to solve this problem is much harder for a mathematician. Our aim in here is to give a detailed overview over known results for such collision problems, and spin them further from incompressible fluids such as water over compressible ones such as air to the case of non-Newtonian fluids, an example of which is given by blood, paint, or ketchup. A complete solution of whether or not the ball touches the bottom of the glass is, unfortunately, out of reach at least for compressible fluids. We will rather show that
if
both the compressible fluid and the solid inside possess some specific properties,
then
collision happens. Additionally, for the very specific case of two-dimensional Stokes flow, we will also show some converse of this.

Before going into deeper details, let us specify the equations under consideration. Two of the main models in fluid mechanics are the incompressible and compressible Navier-Stokes
1
1

1

after Claude-Louis Navier (1785–1836) and George Gabriel Stokes (1819–1903)
equations governing the motion of a fluid in some domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
d\in\{2,3\}
, the “skeleton” of those is given by the system of equations

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\rho+\div(\rho\mathbf{u})=0&\text{(mass conservation)},\\
\partial_{t}(\rho\mathbf{u})+\div(\rho\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{u})-\div\mathbb{S}+\nabla p=\rho\mathbf{f}&\text{(momentum conservation)},\end{cases}

supplemented with suitable initial and boundary conditions. Here,
\rho
and
\mathbf{u}
denote the fluid’s density and velocity, respectively,
p
is the fluid’s pressure,
\mathbb{S}
the (viscous) stress tensor, and
\mathbf{f}
a given external force density. For the compressible case
\rho\neq\text{constant}
, the pressure commonly depends on the density, whereas for the incompressible case
\rho\equiv\text{constant}
, it can be seen as a Lagrange multiplier to the divergence-free condition
\div\mathbf{u}=0
. Inserting in the fluid some solid obstacle
\mathcal{S}
, the fluid’s and solid’s velocity are clearly not independent, and the above system of equations needs to be extended accordingly:

•

The solid has its own translational and rotational velocity. Therefore, the fluid shall follow this motion on the common boundary
\partial\mathcal{S}
.

•

By Newton’s laws of motion, the movement of the solid is governed by the force the fluid imposes to it, thus leading to additional equations for the conservation of linear and angular momentum of the solid.

The above issues are covered by the additional equations for the fluid-solid-interaction, namely

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\mathbf{u}|_{\partial\mathcal{S}}=\dot{\mathbf{G}}(t)+\omega(t)\times(x-\mathbf{G}(t))&\text{(fluid's = solid's velocity)},\\
m\ddot{\mathbf{G}}(t)=-\int_{\partial\mathcal{S}}(\mathbb{S}-p\mathbb{I})\mathbf{n}\ \mathrm{d}\sigma+\int_{\mathcal{S}}\rho_{\mathcal{S}}\mathbf{f}\ \mathrm{d}x&\text{(linear momentum)},\\
\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}(\mathbb{J}\omega)=-\int_{\partial\mathcal{S}}(x-\mathbf{G})\times(\mathbb{S}-p\mathbb{I})\mathbf{n}\ \mathrm{d}\sigma+\int_{\mathcal{S}}(x-\mathbf{G})\times\rho_{\mathcal{S}}\mathbf{f}\ \mathrm{d}x&\text{(angular momentum)}.\end{cases}

Here, the notation “
\times
” shall be read as
\omega(t)(x-\mathbf{G}(t))^{\perp}
if
d=2
, where
(x_{1},x_{2})^{\perp}=(-x_{2},x_{1})
, and similarly for the equation of angular momentum. In the above, we denoted by
\rho_{\mathcal{S}}>0
the solid’s density,
\mathbf{G}
is the center of mass of the body
\mathcal{S}
,
\omega
is its rotational velocity,
m>0
is the object’s mass given by

\displaystyle m=\int_{\mathcal{S}}\rho_{\mathcal{S}}\ \mathrm{d}x,

and
\mathbb{J}
is the inertial tensor (moment of inertia) given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{J}=\int_{\mathcal{S}}\rho_{\mathcal{S}}\big(|x-\mathbf{G}|^{2}\mathbb{I}-(x-\mathbf{G})\otimes(x-\mathbf{G})\big)\ \mathrm{d}x.

In order to understand what is behind these equations and where they come from, let us make three remarks on them:

1.

Strictly speaking, the solid
\mathcal{S}
shall be seen as a time-dependent set-valued map

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}:(0,T)\times\mathcal{S}_{0}\to 2^{\mathbb{R}^{d}}

for some fixed reference particle
\mathcal{S}_{0}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
. The assumption of a
rigid
solid particle then implies

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{S}&=\mathcal{S}(t)=\mathbf{G}(t)+\mathbb{O}(t)(\mathcal{S}_{0}-\mathbf{G}(0))\\
&=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:x=\mathbf{G}(t)+\mathbb{O}(t)(y-\mathbf{G}(0)),\ y\in\mathcal{S}_{0}\}\end{split}

(S)

for some rotation (orthogonal matrix)
\mathbb{O}(t)\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
with
\mathbb{O}(0)=\mathbb{O}(t)\mathbb{O}(t)^{T}=\mathbb{I}
. In turn, differentiating (
S
) with respect to time, the velocity of the particle
\mathcal{S}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}_{s}(t,x)=\dot{x}(t,x)=\dot{\mathbf{G}}(t)+\mathbb{Q}(t)(x-\mathbf{G}(t))\ \text{for all}\ x\in\mathcal{S},

where the matrix
\mathbb{Q}
is skew-symmetric and related to
\mathbb{O}
via

\displaystyle\dot{\mathbb{O}}\mathbb{O}^{T}=\mathbb{Q}.

The skew-symmetry follows from

\displaystyle 0=\frac{\ \mathrm{d}}{\ \mathrm{d}t}\mathbb{I}=\frac{\ \mathrm{d}}{\ \mathrm{d}t}(\mathbb{O}\mathbb{O}^{T})=\dot{\mathbb{O}}\mathbb{O}^{T}+\mathbb{O}\dot{\mathbb{O}}^{T}=\dot{\mathbb{O}}\mathbb{O}^{T}+(\dot{\mathbb{O}}\mathbb{O}^{T})^{T}=\mathbb{Q}+\mathbb{Q}^{T}.

Note especially that this implies the existence of some function
\omega(t)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d-3}
such that for any
x\in\mathcal{S}

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}_{\mathcal{S}}(t,x)

\displaystyle=\dot{\mathbf{G}}(t)+\omega(t)(x-\mathbf{G}(t))^{\perp}\ \text{if}\ d=2,

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}_{\mathcal{S}}(t,x)

\displaystyle=\dot{\mathbf{G}}(t)+\omega(t)\times(x-\mathbf{G}(t))\ \text{if}\ d=3,

which represents precisely the compatibility condition of the fluid’s velocity on
\partial\mathcal{S}
.

2.

Newton’s second law states that acceleration of a body is due to the forces exerted to it. The solid’s momentum is simply given by

\displaystyle m\dot{\mathbf{G}}=\dot{\mathbf{G}}\int_{\mathcal{S}}\rho_{\mathcal{S}}\ \mathrm{d}x=\int_{\mathcal{S}}\rho_{\mathcal{S}}\mathbf{u}\ \mathrm{d}x

as
\int_{\mathcal{S}}x-\mathbf{G}\ \mathrm{d}x=0
by the definition of
\mathbf{G}
. Hence, the force on
\mathcal{S}
is

\displaystyle m\ddot{\mathbf{G}}

\displaystyle=\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}(m\dot{\mathbf{G}})=\frac{\rm d}{{\rm d}t}\int_{\mathcal{S}}\rho_{\mathcal{S}}\mathbf{u}\ \mathrm{d}x=\int_{\mathcal{S}}\partial_{t}(\rho_{\mathcal{S}}\mathbf{u})+\div(\rho_{\mathcal{S}}\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{u})\ \mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{S}}\rho_{\mathcal{S}}\mathbf{f}\ \mathrm{d}x-\int_{\mathcal{S}}-\div(\mathbb{S}-p\mathbb{I})\ \mathrm{d}x=\int_{\mathcal{S}}\rho_{\mathcal{S}}\mathbf{f}\ \mathrm{d}x-\int_{\partial\mathcal{S}}(\mathbb{S}-p\mathbb{I})\mathbf{n}\ \mathrm{d}\sigma,

which is conservation of linear momentum. Similar arguments yield the formula for the angular momentum.

3.

The moment of inertia
\mathbb{J}
is a symmetric positive definite matrix (a positive scalar if
d=2
) in the sense that for each
\omega\in\mathbb{R}^{2d-3}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{J}\omega\cdot\omega=\int_{\mathcal{S}}\rho_{\mathcal{S}}\big(|x-\mathbf{G}|^{2}|\omega|^{2}-|(x-\mathbf{G})\cdot\omega|^{2}\big)\ \mathrm{d}x=\int_{\mathcal{S}}\rho_{\mathcal{S}}|\omega\times(x-\mathbf{G})|^{2}\ \mathrm{d}x\geq 0.

In the literature, this is sometimes used as the definition of
\mathbb{J}
in the sense that for any
a,b\in\mathbb{R}^{2d-3}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{J}a\cdot b=\int_{\mathcal{S}}\rho_{\mathcal{S}}[a\times(x-\mathbf{G})]\cdot[b\times(x-\mathbf{G})]\ \mathrm{d}x.

In three spatial dimensions, the eigenvalues of this tensor are called
principal moments of inertia
, the corresponding eigenvectors are the
principal axes
. Moreover, as the solid
\mathcal{S}(t)
as well as its center of mass
\mathbf{G}(t)
are both time-dependent, one shall think that the moment of inertia
\mathbb{J}
is as well. Using the change of variables
z=\mathbb{O}^{T}(x-\mathbf{G})
, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{J}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{S}_{0}-\mathbf{G}(0)}\rho_{\mathcal{S}}\big(|z|^{2}\mathbb{I}-(\mathbb{O}z)\otimes(\mathbb{O}z)\big)\ \mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{S}_{0}-\mathbf{G}(0)}\rho_{\mathcal{S}}\big(|z|^{2}\mathbb{I}-z\otimes z\big)\ \mathrm{d}z+\int_{\mathcal{S}_{0}-\mathbf{G}(0)}\rho_{\mathcal{S}}\big(z\otimes z-(\mathbb{O}z)\otimes(\mathbb{O}z)\big)\ \mathrm{d}z.

Indeed the last integral, being the only time-dependent term, tells that
\mathbb{J}
is almost time-independent in the sense that the principal axes of the body point in different directions due to the underlying rotation. If the body does not rotate, meaning
\mathbb{O}(t)=\mathbb{I}
for all
t
, the matrix
\mathbb{J}
is indeed constant in time. The same holds true if
\mathcal{S}_{0}
is symmetric to the underlying rotation, meaning
\mathcal{S}_{0}-\mathbf{G}(0)=\mathbb{O}(\mathcal{S}_{0}-\mathbf{G}(0))
: indeed,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}_{0}-\mathbf{G}(0)}\big(z\otimes z-(\mathbb{O}z)\otimes(\mathbb{O}z)\big)\ \mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{S}_{0}-\mathbf{G}(0)}z\otimes z\ \mathrm{d}z-\int_{\mathbb{O}^{-1}(\mathcal{S}_{0}-\mathbf{G}(0))}(\mathbb{O}z)\otimes(\mathbb{O}z)\ \mathrm{d}(\mathbb{O}z)=0.

In particular, for
\mathcal{S}
being a ball,
\mathbb{J}
does not change for
any
rotation
2
2

2

In this special case,
\mathbb{J}
is a constant scalar multiple of the identity:
\mathbb{J}=\frac{2}{5}mr^{2}\mathbb{I}
, where
r>0
is the radius of the ball
\mathcal{S}
.
.

Assuming the solid
\mathcal{S}
is in free fall under the force of gravity over a horizontal plate, one shall expect that the solid touches the plate in finite time, as it is observed in physics.
We will show, however, that
this is not always the case
. In fact, it strongly depends on the shape of the body
\mathcal{S}
near the contact zone, as well as the boundary conditions imposed on
\partial\mathcal{S}
and
\partial\Omega
. Roughly speaking, at least for Newtonian fluids with a linear stress tensor, the main outcomes of the following chapters are:

•

If the body
\mathcal{S}
is a ball, and if we impose no-slip boundary conditions on the fluid’s velocity, then the solid stays away from the boundary of its container for all finite times.

•

If both the wall and the solid are “suitably rough” (in a sense to be specified), then collision occurs in finite time.

The term “suitably rough” here means different shapes of
\mathcal{S}
besides a ball, as well as different boundary conditions on the fluid’s velocity. We will be precise on this in the corresponding chapters.

The physical intuitions behind the seemingly paradoxical no-collision for no-slip is the following: a ball has a critical shape in the sense that it is smooth and its lower tip is “too flat”, and the no-slip boundary conditions on the fluid velocity makes the fluid to stick on
\mathcal{S}
. The fluid has now to squeeze solely through a long channel between the ball and the plate, while at the same time resting on all boundaries, hence creating a huge drag force on the solid preventing it from touching. On the other hand, if the solid is shaped like a parabola, it “cuts” through the fluid and thus can collide (in other words, the channel is not as long as for a ball). For slip boundary conditions, the fluid does not completely has to squeeze through the channel but can partially escape “to the side”, thus the drag force is lowered and collision can occur.

Chapter 1
Basic function spaces, notations, auxiliary results

To begin, we recall briefly the function spaces used in the sequel. Let
d\in\{2,3\}
,
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a domain, and
1\leq p,q\leq\infty
. We refer to
[
13
]
for a detailed overview.

•

Lebesgue and Sobolev spaces
will be denoted in the usual way as
L^{p}(\Omega)
and
W^{k,p}(\Omega)
, respectively. We will also denote them for vector- and matrix-valued functions as in the scalar case, that is,
L^{p}(\Omega)
instead of
L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
. The Sobolev space of trace-zero functions will be denoted by
W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
.

•

Lebesgue-Bochner spaces
: For
p,q
finite, the Lebesgue-Bochner spaces are defined as

\displaystyle L^{p}(0,T;L^{q}(\Omega))=\Big\{f:(0,T)\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d}:f(t,\cdot)\in L^{q}(\Omega),\ \|f\|_{L^{p}(0,T;L^{q}(\Omega))}<\infty\Big\}

with corresponding norm

\displaystyle\|f\|_{L^{p}(0,T;L^{q}(\Omega))}=\left(\int_{0}^{T}\|f(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}^{q}\ \mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}=\left(\int_{0}^{T}\left(\int_{\Omega}|f(t,x)|^{p}\ \mathrm{d}x\right)^{\frac{q}{p}}\ \mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}.

Similarly, we define
L^{p}(0,T;W^{k,q}(\Omega))
as

\displaystyle L^{p}(0,T;W^{k,q}(\Omega))=\Big\{f:(0,T)\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d}:f(t,\cdot)\in W^{k,q}(\Omega),\ \|f\|_{L^{p}(0,T;W^{k,q}(\Omega))}<\infty\Big\}

with corresponding norm

\displaystyle\|f\|_{L^{p}(0,T;W^{k,q}(\Omega))}=\sum_{l=0}^{k}\|\nabla^{l}f\|_{L^{p}(0,T;L^{q}(\Omega))}.

The definition of the spaces for
p=\infty
or
q=\infty
is then as usual with the help of the essential supremum.

•

Frobenius inner product:
For each
\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, we set
\mathbb{A}:\mathbb{B}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}A_{ij}B_{ij}
. Further, we define the Frobenius norm by
|\mathbb{A}|^{2}=\mathbb{A}:\mathbb{A}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}|A_{ij}|^{2}
.

•

Korn’s inequality:
There exists a constant
C>0
such that for each
\mathbf{u}\in W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
,

\displaystyle\|\nabla\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq C\|\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{u})\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)},

(1.1)

where
\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{u})=\frac{1}{2}(\nabla\mathbf{u}+\nabla^{T}\mathbf{u})
is the symmetrized gradient.

•

Poincaré’s inequality:
For a bounded domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, there exists a constant
C>0
depending just on
\Omega
and
p
such that for any
f\in W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
,

\displaystyle\|f\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq C\|\nabla f\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}.

(1.2)

Moreover, if
0\in\Omega
, the constant
C
scales like
C(r\Omega)=rC(\Omega)
for any
r>0
.

•

Hardy’s inequality:
Let
\Omega\subsetneq\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a convex open set. Then, there exists a constant
C=C(p)>0
such that for any
\mathbf{u}\in W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
,

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\bigg|\frac{\mathbf{u}(x)}{\dist(x,\partial\Omega)}\bigg|^{p}\ \mathrm{d}x\leq C\int_{\Omega}|\nabla\mathbf{u}|^{p}\ \mathrm{d}x.

(1.3)

•

Sobolev embedding:
Let
d,k\geq 1
and
p\in[1,\infty]
. For
k<d/p
, denote

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p^{\ast}}=\frac{1}{p}-\frac{k}{d}.

Then the embedding
W^{k,p}\hookrightarrow L^{q}
is compact for any
1\leq q<p^{\ast}
and continuous for
q=p^{\ast}
, and there exists a constant
C>0
depending only on
\Omega
,
d
,
k
,
q
, and
p
such that for any
f\in W^{k,p}(\Omega)
, we have

\displaystyle\|f\|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}\leq C\|f\|_{W^{k,p}(\Omega)}.

(1.4)

Furthermore, if
k=d/p
, then the embedding
W^{k,p}\hookrightarrow L^{q}
is continuous for any
1\leq q<\infty
, and (
1.4
) holds. If
k>d/p
, then (
1.4
) holds with
L^{q}(\Omega)
replaced by
C^{k-1-[d/p]}(\Omega)
, where
[d/p]
denotes the entire part of
d/p
.

•

Grönwall’s inequality:
Let
f:[0,T]\to[0,\infty)
be integrable and assume there are constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
with

\displaystyle f(t)\leq C_{1}\int_{0}^{t}f(s)\ \mathrm{d}s+C_{2}.

Then, it holds

\displaystyle f(t)\leq C_{2}\left(1+C_{1}te^{C_{1}t}\right)

(1.5)

for almost every
t\in[0,T]
. In particular, there exists a constant
C=C(C_{1},C_{2},T)>0
such that
f(t)\leq C
for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
.

•

Young’s inequality:
For any
a,b\geq 0
, and
\delta>0
, and any
1<p,q<\infty
with
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
, we have

\displaystyle ab\leq\delta a^{p}+\frac{(p\delta)^{1-q}}{q}b^{q}.

(1.6)

Mostly, we will use the short form of this: for each
\delta>0
, all
a,b\geq 0
, and all
1<p,q<\infty
with
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
there exists a constant
C=C(\delta,p)>0
such that
ab\leq\delta a^{p}+Cb^{q}
.

•

Hölder’s inequality:
Let
N\geq 1
,
i\in\{1,\dots,N\}
,
p_{i},p\in[1,\infty]
, and
f_{i}\in L^{p_{i}}(\Omega)
with
\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}^{-1}=p^{-1}
. Then
f=\prod_{i=1}^{N}f_{i}\in L^{p}(\Omega)
with

\displaystyle\|f\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq\prod_{i=1}^{N}\|f_{i}\|_{L^{p_{i}}(\Omega)}.

(1.7)

•

Riesz transform (see
[
12
]
):
Let
j\in\{1,\dots,d\}
. There exists a bounded linear operator
R_{j}:L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and a constant
C=C(d)>0
such that for any
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we have

\displaystyle R_{j}f=\partial_{j}(-\Delta)^{-\frac{1}{2}}f,

\displaystyle\|R_{j}f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},

where
(-\Delta)^{-\frac{1}{2}}
is the Fourier multiplier with symbol
-1/|\xi|
. In particular, for any
i,j\in\{1,\dots,d\}
and any
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
\Delta f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we have

\displaystyle R_{i}R_{j}\Delta f=-\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial x_{i}\partial x_{j}},

\displaystyle\bigg\|\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial x_{i}\partial x_{j}}\bigg\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\|\Delta f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

(1.8)

To lean the notation, we will write
a\lesssim b
if there is a generic constant
C>0
which is independent of
a,b
, and the parameters of interest such that
a\leq Cb
. The constant might change its value from line to line. The solid
\mathcal{S}(t)\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is assumed to be a simply connected compact set, the motion of which is continuous in time. The domain occupied by the fluid is denoted by
\mathcal{F}(t)=\Omega\setminus\mathcal{S}(t)
.

Chapter 2
General setting: collision for parabolic shapes

In this chapter, we will focus on the three-dimensional case
d=3
, and just later on go to the two-dimensional setting, since it contains several issues that do not occur here. We will first focus on no-slip boundary conditions and introduce the “roughness” by a parameter
\alpha>0
modelling the shape of the solid. All the outcomes given in this and the next chapter are taken from
[
34
]
and
[
40
]
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
be a bounded domain and
\mathcal{S}(t)
be a rigid body with center of mass at
\mathbf{G}(t)
moving inside a viscous fluid, where the fluid domain is
\mathcal{F}(t)=\Omega\setminus\mathcal{S}(t)
. The equations of motion are given by model-precise variants of the basic Navier-Stokes system

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\rho+\div(\rho\mathbf{u})=0&\mbox{ in }\mathcal{F}(t),\\
\partial_{t}(\rho\mathbf{u})+\div(\rho\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{u})-\div\mathbb{S}+\nabla p=\rho\mathbf{f}&\mbox{ in }\mathcal{F}(t),\\
\mathbf{u}=\dot{\mathbf{G}}(t)+\omega(t)\times{(x-\mathbf{G}(t))}&\mbox{ on }{\partial\mathcal{S}(t)},\\
\mathbf{u}=0&\mbox{ on }{\partial\Omega},\end{cases}

(2.1)

together with some initial conditions and the necessary compatibility conditions between the time derivatives of linear and angular momentum. Here,
\mathbf{u}
and
\rho
are the fluid’s velocity and density, and
\dot{\mathbf{G}}(t)
and
\omega(t)
are the translational and rotational velocities of the rigid body, respectively. Moreover, we assume the force
\mathbf{f}\in L^{\infty}((0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{3})
to be given.

We remark that, depending on the model at hand, one needs to add equations (inequalities) for heat, energy, and entropy, respectively, and accordingly make assumptions on the form of the pressure
p
and the stress tensor
\mathbb{S}
. Moreover, we will not state explicitly the dependence of the pressure
p
on the density and the temperature; we will just require that the pressure behaves “nicely” in order to get reasonable bounds on the density and the temperature. As we will focus on collision, which just needs the momentum equation, we will state some precise systems containing all the necessary (in)equalities in Chapter
4
.

The stress tensor
\mathbb{S}
will depend on the symmetrized velocity gradient
\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{u})=\frac{1}{2}(\nabla\mathbf{u}+\nabla^{T}\mathbf{u})
. The precise assumptions on
\mathbb{S}
are stated in
(S1)
–
(S3)
below. Further, we assume that the solid is homogeneous with constant mass density
\rho_{\mathcal{S}}>0
. The mass and centre of mass of the rigid body are given by

m=\rho_{\mathcal{S}}|\mathcal{S}(0)|,\quad\mathbf{G}(t)=\frac{1}{m}\int_{\mathcal{S}(t)}\rho_{\mathcal{S}}x\ \mathrm{d}x.

We will also assume that the solid’s mass is independent of time, that is,
m=\rho_{\mathcal{S}}|\mathcal{S}(t)|
for any
t\geq 0
, leading to the density-independent expression
\mathbf{G}(t)=|\mathcal{S}(t)|^{-1}\int_{\mathcal{S}(t)}x\ \mathrm{d}x
.

2.1
Heuristics ensuring collision

The collision result, for bodies of class
C^{1,\alpha}
and in its easiest form, relies essentially on three main points:

•

uniform bounds: The velocity and, if under consideration, density and temperature shall obey bounds that are independent of the solid’s distance
h
to the boundary of its container. Such estimates usually follow easily from the energy (respectively entropy) and Grönwall’s inequality (
1.5
), and thus can essentially be considered as “given”.

•

appropriate test function for the momentum equation: The question whether collision occurs or not is answered by testing the momentum equation against a “well constructed” test function
\mathbf{w}_{h}
and estimating all occurring terms. This function being the same for both compressible and incompressible systems, and being additionally divergence-free, the only term that changes its form in the momentum equation is the diffusive one containing the stress tensor
\mathbb{S}
.

•

body orientation: This is indeed one of the main points when considering solids of
C^{1,\alpha}
regularity, since one needs to ensure that the equation describing the lower tip of the body keeps its form during the free fall; in particular, rotations of the body (except rotations around the
x_{3}
-axis) must be excluded.

The rest of the proof is easily explained: once the test function
\mathbf{w}_{h}
is constructed, one estimates its norm and the norm of its derivatives in different
L^{q}
-spaces such that these norms are uniformly bounded independent of
h
. This requirement will give conditions on
q
in terms of
\alpha
. Testing the momentum equation with
\mathbf{w}_{h}
, using the regularity of the velocity, density, and temperature, and estimating all occurring terms will then lead to restrictions on
\alpha
. Provided these restrictions hold, the final inequality takes the form

\displaystyle T\leq C(1+T),

where
T>0
is the maximal existence time before collision. Under some energy and mass assumptions, one then ensures
C<1
such that
T<\infty
, meaning collision occurs in finite time.

Let us remark that collision may also occur if
\mathbf{w}_{h}
is
not
uniformly bounded with respect to
h
. This behavior shows up since, at least in the incompressible case and for Newtonian fluids, the distance
h
is related to the drag force
\mathcal{D}_{h}
via an ODE of the form
\ddot{h}+\dot{h}\mathcal{D}_{h}=f
. For bodies of
C^{1,\alpha}
shape, one can show that
\mathcal{D}_{h}\sim h^{-\beta}
for some
\beta=\beta(\alpha)<1
, thus collision can occur in finite time. In contrast, for a sphere
\mathcal{D}_{h}\sim h^{-1}
, hence collision is forbidden. The question of the drag forces for
compressible
fluids is rather different, and it is not clear whether one can derive a similar ODE for
h
, and even how
\mathcal{D}_{h}
depends on the fluid’s density. The author thinks that also the construction of the test function
\mathbf{w}_{h}
has to be changed according to the non-constant fluid’s density. Moreover, it requires a much more detailed analysis of the involved terms. For these reasons, when talking about
compressible
fluids, we will just consider cases where
\mathbf{w}_{h}
is uniformly bounded with respect to
h
. A wider class of obstacles with unbounded
\mathbf{w}_{h}
in the
incompressible
setting will be given in and after Chapter
6
.

2.2
The stress tensor and uniform bounds

Like mentioned in the introduction, the crucial part in analyzing collisions is to investigate the form of the stress tensor
\mathbb{S}
. We will make the following assumptions:

(S1)

Continuity:
\mathbb{S}
is a continuous mapping from
(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\rm sym}^{3\times 3}
to
\mathbb{R}_{\rm sym}^{3\times 3}
, and depends continuously on the temperature
\vartheta\in(0,\infty)
and the symmetric gradient
\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{u})=\frac{1}{2}(\nabla\mathbf{u}+\nabla^{T}\mathbf{u})\in\mathbb{R}_{\rm sym}^{3\times 3}
.

(S2)

Monotonicity: For any
\mathbb{M},\mathbb{N}\in\mathbb{R}_{\rm sym}^{3\times 3}
, we have
[\mathbb{S}(\cdot,\mathbb{M})-\mathbb{S}(\cdot,\mathbb{N})]:(\mathbb{M}-\mathbb{N})\geq 0
.

(S3)

Growth: There are absolute constants
\delta\geq 0
and
0<c_{0}\leq c_{1}<\infty
such that for some
p>1
, for all
\vartheta>0
, and all
\mathbb{M}\in\mathbb{R}_{\rm sym}^{3\times 3}
, we have
c_{0}|\mathbb{M}|^{p}-\delta\leq\mathbb{S}(\vartheta,\mathbb{M}):\mathbb{M}\leq c_{1}|\mathbb{M}|^{p}
.

We note that two main models fall into this setting: classical power-law fluids like
\mathbb{S}=|\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{u})|^{p-2}\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{u})
, and so-called activated Euler fluids with
\mathbb{S}=\max\{|\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{u})|-\delta_{0},0\}|\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{u})|^{-1}\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{u})
for some
\delta_{0}>0
. Moreover, we emphasize that in view of
(S3)
, the temperature plays essentially no role for our discussion. One can think of temperature dependent viscosity coefficients that are uniformly bounded. Another example of viscosities
growing
with the temperature will be given in Chapter
5
. Note moreover that condition
(S3)
implies by duality
\mathbb{S}\in L^{p^{\prime}}((0,T)\times\Omega)
since

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|\mathbb{S}\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}((0,T)\times\Omega)}&=\sup_{\|\mathbb{M}\|_{L^{p}((0,T)\times\Omega)}\leq 1}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\mathbb{S}:\mathbb{M}\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}t\\
&\leq c_{1}\sup_{\|\mathbb{M}\|_{L^{p}((0,T)\times\Omega)}\leq 1}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}|\mathbb{M}|^{p}\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}t\leq c_{1}.\end{split}

(2.2)

Remark 2.1
.

As the proof of Theorem
2.2
below will show, we are able to catch stress tensors of different growth for small and large values of
|\mathbb{M}|
; in fact, we might also consider

\displaystyle c_{0}|\mathbb{M}|^{p}+c_{2}|\mathbb{M}|^{q}-\delta\leq\mathbb{S}(\vartheta,\mathbb{M}):\mathbb{M}\leq c_{1}|\mathbb{M}|^{p}+c_{3}|\mathbb{M}|^{q}.

(2.3)

The conditions required in Theorem
2.2
below then have to be modified in an obvious way. Note that the advantage in allowing for different growth is that one may also take into account other fluid models such as the so-called Carreau-Yasuda law, where the stress tensor is given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{M})=\mu(1+|\mathbb{M}|^{2})^{\frac{p}{2}-1}\mathbb{M}+\lambda\,{\rm trace}(\mathbb{M})\mathbb{I},\quad\mu>0,\,\lambda\geq 0,

giving rise to a growth with
q=2
in (
2.3
).

To start analyzing the collision behavior, one first needs uniform bounds on the velocity, density, and temperature. With a slight abuse of notation, we extend
\rho
and
\mathbf{u}
by

\displaystyle\rho=\begin{cases}\rho&\text{in }\mathcal{F}(t),\\
\rho_{\mathcal{S}}&\text{in }\mathcal{S}(t),\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{u}&\text{in }\mathcal{F}(t),\\
\dot{\mathbf{G}}(t)+\omega(t)\times(x-\mathbf{G}(t))&\text{in }\mathcal{S}(t).\end{cases}

(2.4)

For the time being, we will
assume that the following bounds hold
, and give for some specific models the proofs in Chapter
4
:

\displaystyle\gamma>\frac{3}{2},\quad\|\rho\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{\gamma}(\mathcal{F}(\cdot)))}^{\gamma}+\|\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{p}(0,T;W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega))}^{p}+\|\rho|\mathbf{u}|^{2}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{1}(\Omega))}\lesssim E_{0}+1.

(2.5)

Here,
E_{0}
is the initial energy of the system given by

\displaystyle E_{0}=\int_{\mathcal{F}(0)}\frac{|\mathbf{m}_{0}|^{2}}{2\rho_{0}}+P(\rho_{0},\vartheta_{0})\ \mathrm{d}x+\frac{m}{2}|\mathbf{V}_{0}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{J}(0)\omega_{0}\cdot\omega_{0},

(2.6)

where
\mathbf{m}_{0}=(\rho\mathbf{u})(0)
is the fluid’s initial momentum,
\mathbf{V}_{0}=\dot{\mathbf{G}}(0)
and
\omega_{0}=\omega(0)
are the initial translational and rotational speed of
\mathcal{S}
, respectively, and
P(\rho,\vartheta)
is a pressure potential associated to the original pressure
p(\rho,\vartheta)
. For instance, if the temperature is constant such that
p(\rho,\vartheta)=p(\rho)
, the potential
P(\rho)
satisfies

\displaystyle\rho P^{\prime}(\rho)-P(\rho)=p(\rho).

Further, in sense of density, we assume that

\displaystyle p(\rho,\cdot)\sim\rho^{\gamma}\ \text{ for }\ \rho\geq\underline{\rho}>0,\quad\gamma>\frac{3}{2}.

(2.7)

We will be more precise on this in the models stated in Chapter
4
, together with available existence results of weak solutions to the problem under consideration.

In the sequel, we consider a solid of class
C^{1,\alpha}
moving vertically over a flat horizontal surface under the influence of gravity. More precisely, we make the following assumptions (see Figure
2.1
for the main notations):

(A1)

The source term is provided by the gravitational force
{\mathbf{f}}=-g{\mathbf{e}}_{3}
and
g>0
.

(A2)

The solid moves along and is symmetric to the vertical axis
\{x_{1}=x_{2}=0\}
.

(A3)

The only possible collision point is at
x=0\in\partial\Omega
, and the solid’s motion is a vertical translation.

(A4)

Near
r=0
,
\partial\Omega
is flat and horizontal, where
r=\sqrt{x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}}
.

(A5)

Near
r=0
, the lower part of
\partial\mathcal{S}(t)
is given by

\displaystyle x_{3}={h(t)+r^{1+\alpha}},\ r\leq 2r_{0}\mbox{ for some small enough }r_{0}>0.

(A6)

The collision just happens near the flat boundary of
\Omega
:

\inf_{t>0}\dist\left(\mathcal{S}(t),\partial\Omega\setminus[-2r_{0},2r_{0}]^{2}\times\{0\}\right)\geq d_{0}>0.

Let us also assume that the position of the solid is characterized by its height
h(t)
, in the sense that
{\mathbf{G}}(t)={\mathbf{G}}(0)+(h(t)-h(0)){\mathbf{e}}_{3}
, and
\mathcal{S}(t)=\mathcal{S}(0)+(h(t)-h(0)){\mathbf{e}}_{3}
. Note especially that this means that the solid rotates at most around the
x_{3}
-axis. In turn, if
\mathcal{S}(0)-\mathbf{G}(0)=\mathbb{O}_{3}(\mathcal{S}(0)-\mathbf{G}(0))
, then
\mathcal{S}(t)-\mathbf{G}(t)=\mathbb{O}_{3}(\mathcal{S}(t)-\mathbf{G}(t))
for all
t\geq 0
and all rotations
\mathbb{O}_{3}\in SO(3)
around the
x_{3}
-axis, that is
1
1

1

As for the moment of inertia
\mathbb{J}
, we saw before that this symmetry forces
\mathbb{J}
to be constant in time.
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{O}_{3}=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\phi&-\sin\phi&0\\
\sin\phi&\cos\phi&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix},\quad\phi\in[0,2\pi).

By rotational invariance of the Navier-Stokes equations, as long as the solution is unique (as, for instance, in 2D), this assumption can be verified rigorously, see
[
22
]
.

r
x_{3}
\partial\Omega
-g\mathbf{e}_{3}
\mathcal{S}
\mathcal{F}
h
x_{3}=h+r^{1+\alpha}
2r_{0}
-r_{0}

Figure 2.1:
The body
\mathcal{S}
and fluid
\mathcal{F}
in the container
\Omega
.

Our main result regarding collision now reads as follows:

Theorem 2.2
.

Let
\gamma>\frac{3}{2}
,
p\geq 2
,
0<\alpha\leq 1
, and
\Omega,\ \mathcal{S}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
be bounded domains of class
C^{1,\alpha}
.
Let
(\rho,\vartheta,\mathbf{u},{\mathbf{G}})
be a weak solution to a model-precise version of (
2.1
) enjoying the bounds (
2.5
), let
\mathbb{S}
comply with
(S1)
–
(S3)
, and assume that
(A1)
–
(A6)
are fulfilled. If the solid’s mass is large enough, and its initial vertical and rotational velocities are small enough, then the solid touches
\partial\Omega
in finite time provided

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\alpha<\min\bigg\{\frac{3-p}{2p-1},\frac{3(4p\gamma-3p-6\gamma)}{p\gamma+3p+6\gamma}\bigg\}\quad\text{with}\\
&\frac{3}{2}<\gamma\leq 3,\ \frac{6\gamma}{4\gamma-3}<p<3,\quad\text{or}\quad\gamma>3,\ 2\leq p<3.\end{split}

(2.8)

Remark 2.3
.

The terms “large enough” and “small enough” should be interpreted in such a way that inequality (
3.14
) below is satisfied. Specifically, there is a constant
C_{0}>0
which is independent of
m
and
T
such that collision occurs provided

\displaystyle C_{0}\max\{m^{-1/2},m^{-3/2}\}\bigg(1+E_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{\gamma}+\frac{1}{p}}\bigg)<1.

One shall also compare these assumptions with the ones given in Section
8.4
.

Remark 2.4
.

Let us mention a few facts about the constraints in (
2.8
):

(i)

The two expressions inside the minimum stem, as one shall expect, from estimating the diffusive and convective part, respectively.

(ii)

The restriction
p<3
is due to the diffusive part, see the estimate of
I_{4}
in Section
3.2
. Moreover, the requirement
p\geq 2
stems from the convective term, since we need to estimate the square of the velocity in time. Thus, our result as stated above is just valid for shear-thickening fluids. Omitting convection, Theorem
2.2
still holds provided

\displaystyle\gamma>\frac{3}{2},\ \frac{\gamma}{\gamma-1}<p<3,\ \alpha<\min\bigg\{\frac{3-p}{2p-1},\frac{9(p\gamma-p-\gamma)}{2p\gamma+3p+3\gamma}\bigg\},

(2.9)

hence also allowing for shear-thinning fluids if
\gamma>2
.

(iii)

The first condition on
p
and
\gamma
in (
2.8
) can be equivalently stated as
\frac{3p}{4p-6}<\gamma\leq 3
,
2<p<3
.

(iv)

The first fraction inside the minimum in (
2.8
) wins precisely if
\gamma\geq\frac{3p}{5p-9}
, and in (
2.9
) if
\gamma\geq\frac{3p}{4p-6}
. This seems to be optimal in the sense that for
p=2
,
\alpha=\frac{1}{3}
is a “borderline value” for the incompressible case, which would (loosely speaking) correspond to
\gamma=\infty
(see
[
23
, Section 3.1]
for details).

The proof of Theorem
2.2
will be carried out in the next chapter. Specifically, we will construct a special function associated to the solid. Testing the momentum equation against this function and estimating carefully all occurring terms will finally yield the result.

Remark 2.5
.

Another way how to interpret collision is to use so-called streamlines. These are the solutions to the ODE

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\mathbf{X}(t,x)=\mathbf{u}(t,\mathbf{X}(t,x)),\quad t>0,\quad\mathbf{X}(0,x)=x.

If no collision occurs, the solutions to this ODE are well-defined, in particular, streamlines cannot concentrate. For instance, this happens if
\mathbf{u}
is Lipschitz continuous in the second variable such that the solutions are even unique. If, however, the solid collides with the boundary of its container, then all the streamlines have the same value at the collision point and thus are ill-defined, and the Lipschitz-norm (or some even weaker
W^{1,q}
-norm) of the velocity
\mathbf{u}
blows up as
t
reaches the collision time. We will see how one can prove no-collision results based on this observation in Chapters
7
and
8
.

Chapter 3
Proof of Theorem
2.2

The aim of this chapter is to define an appropriate test function for the momentum equation that will ensure collision in finite time. Let
(\rho,\vartheta,\mathbf{u},{\mathbf{G}})
be a weak solution of (
2.1
) satisfying the assumptions
(A1)
–
(A6)
in the time interval
(0,T_{*})
before collision. From now on we denote
\mathcal{S}_{h}=\mathcal{S}_{h}(t)=\mathcal{S}(0)+(h(t)-h(0)){\mathbf{e}}_{3}
and
\mathcal{F}_{h}=\mathcal{F}_{h}(t)=\Omega\setminus\mathcal{S}_{h}(t)
. As mentioned before, the assumption
(A2)
on
\mathcal{S}(t)
especially means that the body rotates at most around the
x_{3}
-axis.

Collision can occur if and only if
\lim_{t\to T_{*}}h(t)=0
. Note further that
\dist(\mathcal{S}_{h}(t),\partial\Omega)=\min\{h(t),d_{0}\}
by assumptions
(A2)
and
(A6)
.

3.1
Test function

To construct our desired function, we will make use of cylindrical coordinates
(r,\theta,x_{3})
with the standard basis
(\mathbf{e}_{r},\mathbf{e}_{\theta},\mathbf{e}_{3})
.
We use the same function as in
[
23
]
(see also
[
22
,
25
]
), which is constructed as a function
{\mathbf{w}}_{h}
associated with the solid particle
\mathcal{S}_{h}
frozen at distance
h
. This function will be defined for
h\in(0,\sup_{t\in[0,T_{*})}h(t))
. Note that when
h\to 0
, a cusp arises in
\mathcal{F}_{h}
, which is contained in a cylindrical domain beneath
\mathcal{S}
given by

\displaystyle\Omega_{h,r_{0}}=\{x\in\mathcal{F}_{h}:0\leq r<r_{0},\ 0\leq x_{3}\leq h+r^{1+\alpha},\ r^{2}=x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}\}.

(3.1)

For the sequel, we fix
h
as a positive constant and define
\psi(r):=h+r^{1+\alpha}
. Note that the common boundary
\partial\Omega_{h,r_{0}}\cap\partial\mathcal{S}_{h}
is precisely given by the set
\{0\leq r\leq r_{0},\ x_{3}=\psi(r)\}
.

Let us derive how an appropriate test function inside
\Omega_{h,r_{0}}
might look like. In order to get rid of the pressure term, we seek for a function
\mathbf{w}_{h}
which is divergence-free. Additionally, it shall be rigid on
\mathcal{S}_{h}
, and comply with its motion. Thus, our test function shall satisfy

\displaystyle\mathbf{w}_{h}|_{\mathcal{S}_{h}}=\mathbf{e}_{3},\quad\mathbf{w}_{h}|_{\partial\Omega}=0,\quad\div\mathbf{w}_{h}=0.

An easy function satisfying all this is given by
\mathbf{w}_{h}=\nabla\times(\phi_{h}\mathbf{e}_{\theta})
for some function
\phi_{h}(r,x_{3})
to be determined. The solenoidality of
\mathbf{w}_{h}
is thus obvious. In cylindrical coordinates, we write
\mathbf{w}_{h}
as

\displaystyle\mathbf{w}_{h}=-\partial_{3}\phi_{h}\mathbf{e}_{r}+\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}(r\phi_{h})\mathbf{e}_{3}.

(3.2)

The boundary conditions on
\mathbf{w}_{h}
translate for
\phi_{h}
into

\displaystyle\partial_{3}\phi_{h}(r,0)=0,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}(r\phi_{h})(r,0)=0,

\displaystyle\partial_{3}\phi_{h}(r,\psi(r))=0,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}(r\phi_{h})(r,\psi(r))=1.

Further, considering the energy

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}=\int_{\mathcal{F}_{h}}|\nabla\mathbf{w}_{h}|^{2}\ \mathrm{d}x

and anticipating that most of it stems from the vertical motion, that is, from the derivative in
x_{3}
-direction, we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\sim\int_{\mathcal{F}_{h}}|\partial_{3}^{2}\phi_{h}|^{2}\ \mathrm{d}x.

The Euler-Lagrange equation for the functional
\mathcal{E}
thus reads
\partial_{3}^{4}\phi_{h}(r,x_{3})=0
, meaning
\phi_{h}(r,x_{3})=a(r)x_{3}^{3}+b(r)x_{3}^{2}+c(r)x_{3}+d(r)
for some functions
a,b,c,d
to find. A simple calculation now leads to the general form

\displaystyle\phi_{h}(r,x_{3})=-\frac{3}{2}\bigg(\frac{\kappa_{1}}{r}-r\bigg)\bigg(\frac{x_{3}}{\psi(r)}\bigg)^{2}+\bigg(\frac{\kappa_{1}}{r}-r\bigg)\bigg(\frac{x_{3}}{\psi(r)}\bigg)^{3}+\frac{\kappa_{2}}{r},\quad\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\in\mathbb{R}.

In order to get a smooth function
\phi_{h}
for all values of
r
and
x_{3}
, we choose
\kappa_{1}=\kappa_{2}=0
to infer

\displaystyle\phi_{h}(r,x_{3})=\frac{r}{2}\Phi\bigg(\frac{x_{3}}{\psi(r)}\bigg),\quad\Phi(t)=t^{2}(3-2t).

Thus, inside
\Omega_{h,r_{0}}
, the so constructed function will take advantage of the precise form of the solid. Extending
\phi_{h}
in a proper way to the whole of
\Omega
, we thus can define a proper test function
\mathbf{w}_{h}
.

To achieve this, we use a similar extension as in
[
22
]
: define smooth functions
\chi,\eta
satisfying

\displaystyle\chi=1\text{ on }(-r_{0},r_{0})^{2}\times(0,r_{0}),

\displaystyle\chi=0\text{ on }\Omega\setminus\big((-2r_{0},2r_{0})^{2}\times(0,2r_{0})\big)

(3.3)

\displaystyle\eta=1\text{ on }\mathcal{N}_{d_{0}/2},

\displaystyle\eta=0\text{ on }\Omega\setminus\mathcal{N}_{d_{0}},

(3.4)

where
d_{0}>0
is as in
(A6)
, and
\mathcal{N}_{\delta}
is a
\delta
-neighborhood of
\mathcal{S}(0)
. With a slight abuse of the notations above, set

\displaystyle\phi_{h}(r,x_{3})=\frac{r}{2}\begin{cases}1&\text{on }\mathcal{S}_{h},\\
(1-\chi(r,x_{3}))\eta(r,x_{3}-h+h(0))+\chi(r,x_{3})\Phi\left(\frac{x_{3}}{\psi(r)}\right)&\text{on }\Omega\setminus\mathcal{S}_{h},\end{cases}

(3.5)

and
\mathbf{w}_{h}=\nabla\times(\phi_{h}\mathbf{e}_{\theta})
.
Observe that the function
\mathbf{w}_{h}
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathbf{w}_{h}|_{\partial\mathcal{S}_{h}}=\mathbf{e}_{3},\quad\mathbf{w}_{h}|_{\partial\Omega}=0,\quad\div\mathbf{w}_{h}=0.

Indeed, the divergence-free condition is obvious from the definition of
\mathbf{w}_{h}
. Further, since
\phi_{h}=r/2
on
\mathcal{S}_{h}
, we have
\mathbf{w}_{h}=\mathbf{e}_{3}
there. Moreover, by definition of
\chi
and
\eta
, we have
\phi_{h}=0
on
\partial\Omega\setminus\big((-2r_{0},2r_{0})^{2}\times\{0\}\big)
as long as
r_{0}
and
h
are so small that
h+r_{0}^{1+\alpha}\leq d_{0}<r_{0}
. Lastly,
\phi_{h}=0
on
\partial\Omega\cap\big((-r_{0},r_{0})^{2}\times\{0\}\big)
by definition of
\chi
and
\Phi(0)=0
, and in the annulus
\big((-2r_{0},2r_{0})^{2}\setminus(-r_{0},r_{0})^{2}\big)\times\{0\}
we use also
\eta(r,h(0))=0
for
r>\mathfrak{r}_{0}
for some
\mathfrak{r}_{0}\in(d_{0},r_{0})
to finally conclude
\mathbf{w}_{h}|_{\partial\Omega}=0
, provided
h
is sufficiently close to zero.

We summarize further properties in the following Lemma:

Lemma 3.1
.

It holds
\mathbf{w}_{h}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
and

\displaystyle\|\partial_{h}\mathbf{w}_{h}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega\setminus\Omega_{h,r_{0}})}+\|\mathbf{w}_{h}\|_{W^{1,\infty}(\Omega\setminus\Omega_{h,r_{0}})}\lesssim 1.

(3.6)

Moreover,

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{w}_{h}\|_{L^{q}(\Omega_{h,r_{0}})}

\displaystyle\lesssim 1\text{ for any }q<1+\frac{3}{\alpha},

\displaystyle\|\partial_{h}\mathbf{w}_{h}\|_{L^{q}(\Omega_{h,r_{0}})}+\|\nabla\mathbf{w}_{h}\|_{L^{q}(\Omega_{h,r_{0}})}

\displaystyle\lesssim 1\text{ for any }q<\frac{3+\alpha}{1+2\alpha}.

Proof.

We know from the definition of
\mathbf{w}_{h}
in (
3.2
) that
\mathbf{w}_{h}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
. Moreover,
\mathbf{w}_{h}
is bounded outside the bounded region
\Omega_{h,r_{0}}
, so the first inequality (
3.6
) is obvious.

Due to the property (
3.3
) of
\chi
, the function
\phi_{h}
(see (
3.5
)) in
\Omega_{h,r_{0}}
becomes

\phi_{h}(r,x_{3})=\frac{r}{2}\Phi\bigg(\frac{x_{3}}{\psi(r)}\bigg)\quad\mbox{in}\quad\Omega_{h,r_{0}}.

By definition (
3.2
) of
\mathbf{w}_{h}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{w}_{h}=-\frac{r}{2}\Phi^{\prime}\bigg(\frac{x_{3}}{\psi}\bigg)\frac{1}{\psi}\mathbf{e}_{r}+\Phi\bigg(\frac{x_{3}}{\psi}\bigg)\mathbf{e}_{3}-\frac{r}{2}\Phi^{\prime}\bigg(\frac{x_{3}}{\psi}\bigg)\frac{x_{3}\partial_{r}\psi}{\psi^{2}}\mathbf{e}_{3}\quad\mbox{in}\quad\Omega_{h,r_{0}}.

Further,
x_{3}\leq\psi
in
\Omega_{h,r_{0}}
. Hence,

\displaystyle|\Phi|+|\Phi^{\prime}|+|\Phi^{\prime\prime}|\lesssim 1,

(3.7)

leading to

\displaystyle|\mathbf{w}_{h}|\lesssim 1+\frac{r}{\psi}(1+\partial_{r}\psi).

Due to legibility, we will not write the argument of
\Phi
in the sequel. Then, we obtain successively

\displaystyle|\partial_{r}\mathbf{w}_{h}|

\displaystyle\lesssim\Phi^{\prime}\frac{1}{\psi}+\frac{r}{2\psi}\Phi^{\prime\prime}\frac{x_{3}\partial_{r}\psi}{\psi^{2}}+\frac{r}{2}\Phi^{\prime}\frac{\partial_{r}\psi}{\psi^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+\Phi^{\prime}\frac{x_{3}\partial_{r}\psi}{\psi^{2}}+\frac{r}{2}\Phi^{\prime\prime}\cdot\bigg(\frac{x_{3}\partial_{r}\psi}{\psi^{2}}\bigg)^{2}+\frac{r}{2}\Phi^{\prime}\frac{x_{3}\partial_{r}^{2}\psi}{\psi^{2}}+r\Phi^{\prime}\frac{x_{3}(\partial_{r}\psi)^{2}}{\psi^{3}},

\displaystyle|\partial_{3}\mathbf{w}_{h}|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{r}{2}\Phi^{\prime\prime}\frac{1}{\psi^{2}}+\Phi^{\prime}\frac{1}{\psi}+\frac{r}{2}\Phi^{\prime\prime}\frac{x_{3}\partial_{r}\psi}{\psi^{3}}+\frac{r}{2}\Phi^{\prime}\frac{\partial_{r}\psi}{\psi^{2}},

\displaystyle|\partial_{h}\mathbf{w}_{h}|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{r}{2}\Phi^{\prime\prime}\frac{x_{3}}{\psi^{3}}+\frac{r}{2}\Phi^{\prime}\frac{1}{\psi^{2}}+\Phi^{\prime}\frac{x_{3}}{\psi^{2}}+\frac{r\partial_{r}\psi}{2}\Phi^{\prime\prime}\cdot\bigg(\frac{x_{3}}{\psi^{2}}\bigg)^{2}+\Phi^{\prime}\frac{x_{3}r\partial_{r}\psi}{\psi^{3}}.

Using again
x_{3}\leq\psi
and the bounds (
3.7
), we have

\displaystyle|\nabla\mathbf{w}_{h}|

\displaystyle\lesssim|\partial_{r}\mathbf{w}_{h}|+|\partial_{3}\mathbf{w}_{h}|+\bigg|\frac{\mathbf{w}_{h}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{r}}{r}\bigg|\lesssim\frac{1}{\psi}+\frac{r}{\psi^{2}}+\frac{r\partial_{r}\psi}{\psi^{2}}+\frac{\partial_{r}\psi}{\psi}+\frac{r(\partial_{r}\psi)^{2}}{\psi^{2}}+\frac{r\partial_{r}^{2}\psi}{\psi},

\displaystyle|\partial_{h}\mathbf{w}_{h}|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\psi}+\frac{r}{\psi^{2}}+\frac{r\partial_{r}\psi}{\psi^{2}}.

Note that these bounds hold independently of the specific form of
\psi
. In our setting,
\psi(r)=h+r^{1+\alpha}
. Thus, the proof of the remaining estimates on
\mathbf{w}_{h}
,
\nabla\mathbf{w}_{h}
and
\partial_{h}\mathbf{w}_{h}
are based on the following result: we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{r_{0}}\frac{r^{q}}{(h+r^{1+\alpha})^{s}}\ \mathrm{d}r\leq\int_{0}^{r_{0}}r^{q-s(1+\alpha)}\ \mathrm{d}r\lesssim 1\ \forall\ (\alpha,q,s)\in(0,\infty)^{3}\ \mbox{ satisfying }\ q+1>s(1+\alpha);

(3.8)

see also Lemma
6.2
for a generalization to other values of
\alpha,q,s
. Since we don’t need this refinement here, we give the details later on.

Using the estimate (
3.8
), we get

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{h,r_{0}}}|\mathbf{w}_{h}|^{q}\ \mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\lesssim 1+\int_{0}^{r_{0}}\int_{0}^{\psi}\frac{r^{q+1}}{\psi^{q}}+\frac{r^{(1+\alpha)q+1}}{\psi^{q}}\ \mathrm{d}x_{3}\ \mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\lesssim 1+\int_{0}^{r_{0}}\frac{r^{q+1}}{\psi^{q-1}}+\frac{r^{(1+\alpha)q+1}}{\psi^{q-1}}\ \mathrm{d}r\lesssim 1

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\ q+2

\displaystyle>(q-1)(1+\alpha)\ \mbox{ and }\ q(1+\alpha)+2>(q-1)(1+\alpha)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\ \alpha(q-1)

\displaystyle<3.

Using now
r\partial_{r}\psi\lesssim\psi
and
r\partial_{r}^{2}\psi\lesssim\partial_{r}\psi
, we further have

\displaystyle|\nabla\mathbf{w}_{h}|\lesssim|\partial_{r}\mathbf{w}_{h}|+|\partial_{3}\mathbf{w}_{h}|+\Big|\frac{\mathbf{w}_{h}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{r}}{r}\Big|\lesssim\frac{1}{\psi}+\frac{r}{\psi^{2}}+\frac{\partial_{r}\psi}{\psi},\qquad|\partial_{h}\mathbf{w}_{h}|\lesssim\frac{1}{\psi}+\frac{r}{\psi^{2}}.

In particular, it is enough to estimate
\nabla\mathbf{w}_{h}
, since the most restrictive term is
r/\psi^{2}
. Hence, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{h,r_{0}}}|\nabla\mathbf{w}_{h}|^{q}\ \mathrm{d}x\lesssim\int_{0}^{r_{0}}\int_{0}^{\psi}\frac{r}{\psi^{q}}+\frac{r^{q+1}}{\psi^{2q}}+\frac{r(\partial_{r}\psi)^{q}}{\psi^{q}}\ \mathrm{d}x_{3}\ \mathrm{d}r\lesssim\int_{0}^{r_{0}}\frac{r}{\psi^{q-1}}+\frac{r^{q+1}}{\psi^{2q-1}}+\frac{r^{\alpha q+1}}{\psi^{q-1}}\ \mathrm{d}r\lesssim 1

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\ 2>(q-1)(1+\alpha)\ \mbox{ and }\ q+2>(2q-1)(1+\alpha)\ \mbox{ and }\ \alpha q+2>(q-1)(1+\alpha)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\ q<\frac{3+\alpha}{1+2\alpha}.

∎

Remark 3.2
.

The condition
\alpha(q-1)<3
coming from
\mathbf{w}_{h}
is consistent with the results of
[
45
, Theorem 3.2]
, where the author showed that collision is forbidden as long as
\alpha(q-1)\geq 3
. Especially, for shapes of class
C^{1,1}
like balls, this states that no collision can occur as long as
q\geq 4
, which fits the assumptions made in
[
17
]
and
[
39
]
. Moreover, the difference
q-\frac{2+\alpha}{1+2\alpha}
occurs in the incompressible two-dimensional setting in
[
20
, Theorem 3.2]
as an optimal value for the solid to move vertically. Our fraction
\frac{3+\alpha}{1+2\alpha}
thus seems to be a three-dimensional counterpart to that; see also the work
[
21
]
for the 3D case, where precisely this value occurs. Furthermore, we shall compare this with the value of
\beta
occurring in Theorem
6.1
later on.

3.2
Estimates near the collision – Proof of Theorem
2.2

Let
0<T<T_{*}
and let
\zeta\in C^{1}([0,T))
with
0\leq\zeta\leq 1
,
\zeta^{\prime}\leq 0
,
\zeta(T)=0
, and
\zeta=1
near
t=0
. For instance, setting
\tilde{\zeta}(t)=\exp[T^{-2}-(T^{2}-t^{2})^{-1}]
, the function

\displaystyle\zeta(t)=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }0\leq t<(1-\frac{1}{k})T,\\
\tilde{\zeta}(kt-(k-1)T)&\text{else}\end{cases}

(3.9)

for some
k\geq 2
will do the job. We take
\zeta(t){\mathbf{w}}_{h(t)}
as test function in the weak formulation of the momentum equation (
2.1
)
2
with right-hand side
{\mathbf{f}}=-g{\mathbf{e}}_{3}
,
g>0
. Recalling
\div\mathbf{w}_{h}=0
and
\partial_{t}\mathbf{w}_{h(t)}=\dot{h}(t)\partial_{h}\mathbf{w}_{h(t)}
, we get

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\int_{0}^{T}\zeta\int_{\Omega}\rho\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{u}:\mathbb{D}({\mathbf{w}}_{h})\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}t+\int^{T}_{0}\zeta^{\prime}\int_{\Omega}\rho\mathbf{u}\cdot{\mathbf{w}}_{h}\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}t\\
&\quad+\int^{T}_{0}\zeta\dot{h}\int_{\Omega}\rho\mathbf{u}\cdot{\partial_{h}\mathbf{w}}_{h}\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}t-\int^{T}_{0}\zeta\int_{\Omega}{\mathbb{S}}:\mathbb{D}({\mathbf{w}}_{h})\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}t\\
=&\int_{0}^{T}\zeta\int_{\Omega}\rho g\mathbf{e}_{3}\cdot\mathbf{w}_{h}\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}t-\int_{\Omega}\mathbf{m}_{0}\cdot\mathbf{w}_{h}\ \mathrm{d}x\\
=&\int_{0}^{T}\zeta\int_{\mathcal{S}_{h}}\rho g\mathbf{e}_{3}\cdot\mathbf{w}_{h}\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{T}\zeta\int_{\mathcal{F}_{h}}\rho g\mathbf{e}_{3}\cdot\mathbf{w}_{h}\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}t-\int_{\Omega}\mathbf{m}_{0}\cdot\mathbf{w}_{h}\ \mathrm{d}x.\end{split}

(3.10)

Observe that we have
\mathbf{w}_{h}=\mathbf{e}_{3}
on
\mathcal{S}_{h}
, so for a sequence
\zeta_{k}\to 1
in
L^{1}([0,T))
,

\displaystyle\int^{T}_{0}\zeta_{k}\int_{\mathcal{S}_{h}}\rho g{\mathbf{e}}_{3}\cdot{\mathbf{w}}_{h}\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}t=\int_{0}^{T}\zeta_{k}\int_{\mathcal{S}_{h}}\rho_{\mathcal{S}}g\to mgT.

An example of such a sequence is precisely given by (
3.9
). In particular, for a proper choice of
\zeta
, it follows that

\frac{1}{2}mgT\leq\int_{0}^{T}\zeta\int_{\Omega}\rho\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{u}:\mathbb{D}({\mathbf{w}}_{h})\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}t+\int^{T}_{0}\zeta^{\prime}\int_{\Omega}\rho\mathbf{u}\cdot{\mathbf{w}}_{h}\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}t+\int^{T}_{0}\zeta\dot{h}\int_{\Omega}\rho\mathbf{u}\cdot{\partial_{h}\mathbf{w}}_{h}\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}t\\
\quad-\int^{T}_{0}\zeta\int_{\Omega}{\mathbb{S}}:\mathbb{D}({\mathbf{w}}_{h})\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}t-\int_{0}^{T}\zeta\int_{\mathcal{F}_{h}}\rho g\mathbf{e}_{3}\cdot\mathbf{w}_{h}\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}t+\int_{\Omega}\mathbf{m}_{0}\cdot\mathbf{w}_{h}\ \mathrm{d}x=\sum_{j=1}^{6}I_{j}.

(3.11)

We will estimate each
I_{j}
separately, and set our focus on the explicit dependence on
T
and
m
. For the latter purpose, we split each density dependent integral into its fluid and solid part
I_{j}^{f}
and
I_{j}^{s}
, respectively.

\bullet
For
I_{2}^{f}
, we have by
|\zeta^{\prime}|=-\zeta^{\prime}\geq 0
,
\zeta(T)=0
, and
\zeta(0)=1

\displaystyle|I_{2}^{f}|

\displaystyle\leq-\int_{0}^{T}\zeta^{\prime}\int_{\mathcal{F}_{h}}\rho|\mathbf{u}||\mathbf{w}_{h}|\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}t=-\int_{0}^{T}\zeta^{\prime}\int_{\mathcal{F}_{h}}\sqrt{\rho}\sqrt{\rho}|\mathbf{u}||\mathbf{w}_{h}|\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq-\int_{0}^{T}\zeta^{\prime}\|\sqrt{\rho}\|_{L^{2\gamma}({\mathcal{F}_{h}})}\|\sqrt{\rho}\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{2}({\mathcal{F}_{h}})}\|\mathbf{w}_{h}\|_{L^{\frac{2\gamma}{\gamma-1}}({\mathcal{F}_{h}})}\ \mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\|\rho\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{\gamma}({\mathcal{F}(\cdot)}))}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\rho|\mathbf{u}|^{2}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{1}(\Omega))}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\mathbf{w}_{h}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{\frac{2\gamma}{\gamma-1}}(\mathcal{F}(\cdot)))}\zeta(0)\lesssim(E_{0}+1)^{\frac{1}{2\gamma}+\frac{1}{2}},

where we have used the estimate (
2.5
) and Lemma
3.1
under the condition

\displaystyle\frac{2\gamma}{\gamma-1}<1+\frac{3}{\alpha}\Leftrightarrow\alpha<\frac{3(\gamma-1)}{\gamma+1}.

\bullet
For
I_{2}^{s}
, recall that the solid rotates at most around the
x_{3}
-axis, hence
\omega(t)=\pm|\omega(t)|\mathbf{e}_{3}
. Further, due to
\mathbf{u}|_{\mathcal{S}_{h}}=\dot{\mathbf{G}}(t)+\omega\times(x-\mathbf{G}(t))
,
\mathbf{G}(t)=\mathbf{G}(0)+(h(t)-h(0))\mathbf{e}_{3}
,
\rho|_{\mathcal{S}_{h}}=\rho_{\mathcal{S}}>0
, and
\mathbf{w}_{h}|_{\mathcal{S}_{h}}=\mathbf{e}_{3}
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}_{h}}\rho\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{w}_{h}\ \mathrm{d}x=\rho_{\mathcal{S}}\int_{\mathcal{S}_{h}}\big[\dot{h}\mathbf{e}_{3}\pm|\omega|\mathbf{e}_{3}\times(x-\mathbf{G}(0)-(h-h(0))\mathbf{e}_{3})\big]\cdot\mathbf{e}_{3}\ \mathrm{d}x=m\dot{h}.

Moreover, from the bounds (
2.5
), we infer

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in(0,T)}|\dot{h}|^{2}=\sup_{t\in(0,T)}\frac{2}{m}\int_{\mathcal{S}_{h}}\rho_{\mathcal{S}}|\dot{h}|^{2}\ \mathrm{d}x\leq\sup_{t\in(0,T)}\frac{2}{m}\int_{\mathcal{S}_{h}}\rho_{\mathcal{S}}|\mathbf{u}|^{2}\ \mathrm{d}x\lesssim\frac{2}{m}(E_{0}+1).

(3.12)

Hence, by the choice of
\zeta
such that
|\zeta^{\prime}|=-\zeta^{\prime}
and
\zeta(0)=1+\zeta(T)=1
, we get

\displaystyle|I_{2}^{s}|\lesssim-\int_{0}^{T}\zeta^{\prime}m|\dot{h}|\ \mathrm{d}t\lesssim\sqrt{m}(E_{0}+1)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

\bullet
For
I_{3}
, observe that
I_{3}^{s}=0
due to
\partial_{h}\mathbf{w}_{h}|_{\mathcal{S}_{h}}=\partial_{h}\mathbf{e}_{3}=0
. Next, by Sobolev embedding (
1.4
) and (
2.5
),

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{p}(0,T;L^{p^{\ast}}(\Omega))}\lesssim\|\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{p}(0,T;W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega))}\lesssim(E_{0}+1)^{\frac{1}{p}},

where we set
p^{\ast}=3p/(3-p)
. Thus,

\displaystyle|I_{3}|

\displaystyle=|I_{3}^{f}|\leq\int_{0}^{T}\zeta|\dot{h}(t)|\,\|\rho\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{\gamma}(\mathcal{F}(\cdot)))}\|\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{p}(0,T;L^{p^{\ast}}(\Omega))}\|\partial_{h}\mathbf{w}_{h}\|_{L^{\frac{p^{\ast}\gamma}{p^{\ast}(\gamma-1)-\gamma}}(\mathcal{F}(\cdot))}\ \mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\lesssim(E_{0}+1)^{\frac{1}{\gamma}+\frac{1}{p}}\|\dot{h}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T)}\|\zeta\|_{L^{1}(0,T)}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{1}{m}}(E_{0}+1)^{\frac{1}{\gamma}+\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{2}}T,

where we have used the estimates (
2.5
), (
3.12
), and Lemma
3.1
under the condition

\displaystyle\frac{p^{\ast}\gamma}{p^{\ast}(\gamma-1)-\gamma}<\frac{3+\alpha}{1+2\alpha}\Leftrightarrow\alpha<\frac{2p^{\ast}\gamma-3p^{\ast}-3\gamma}{p^{\ast}\gamma+p^{\ast}+\gamma}=\frac{9(p\gamma-p-\gamma)}{2p\gamma+3p+3\gamma}.

\bullet
Regarding
I_{4}
, using that
\mathbb{S}\in L^{p^{\prime}}((0,T)\times\Omega)
is bounded by
c_{1}>0
(see (
2.2
)), we calculate

\displaystyle|I_{4}|

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{T}\zeta\|\mathbb{S}\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega)}\|\nabla\mathbf{w}_{h}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\ \mathrm{d}t\leq\|\zeta\|_{L^{\frac{p}{p-1}}(0,T)}\|\mathbb{S}\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}((0,T)\times\Omega)}\|\nabla\mathbf{w}_{h}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{p}(\Omega))}

\displaystyle\lesssim(E_{0}+1)^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}}T^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}},

where we have used Lemma
3.1
under the condition

\displaystyle p<\frac{3+\alpha}{1+2\alpha}\Leftrightarrow\alpha<\frac{3-p}{2p-1}.

\bullet
For
I_{5}=I_{5}^{f}
,

\displaystyle|I_{5}|

\displaystyle\leq g\int_{0}^{T}\zeta\|\rho\|_{L^{\gamma}(\mathcal{F}_{h})}\|\mathbf{w}_{h}\|_{L^{\frac{\gamma}{\gamma-1}}(\Omega)}\leq g\|\zeta\|_{L^{1}(0,T)}\|\rho\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{\gamma}(\mathcal{F}(\cdot)))}\|\mathbf{w}_{h}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{\frac{\gamma}{\gamma-1}}(\Omega))}

\displaystyle\leq g(E_{0}+1)^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}T,

by using Lemma
3.1
under the condition

\displaystyle\frac{\gamma}{\gamma-1}<1+\frac{3}{\alpha}\Leftrightarrow\alpha<3(\gamma-1).

\bullet
Similar to
I_{2}^{f}
, we have for
I_{6}^{f}
the estimate

\displaystyle|I_{6}^{f}|\leq\|\mathbf{m}_{0}\|_{L^{\frac{2\gamma}{\gamma+1}}(\mathcal{F}(0))}\|\mathbf{w}_{h}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{\frac{2\gamma}{\gamma-1}}(\Omega))}\lesssim\bigg\|\frac{|\mathbf{m}_{0}|^{2}}{\rho_{0}}\bigg\|_{L^{1}(\mathcal{F}(0))}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\rho_{0}\|_{L^{\gamma}(\mathcal{F}(0))}^{\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim(E_{0}+1)^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2\gamma}}.

\bullet
For
I_{6}^{s}
, where
\mathbf{w}_{h}=\mathbf{e}_{3}
,
\mathbf{m}_{0}=(\rho\mathbf{u})(0)=\rho_{\mathcal{S}}(\dot{h}\mathbf{e}_{3}+\omega\times(x-h\mathbf{e}_{3}))
, and
\omega=\pm|\omega|\mathbf{e}_{3}
, we have similarly to
I_{2}^{s}
that

\displaystyle|I_{6}^{s}|=\bigg|\int_{\mathcal{S}(0)}\mathbf{m}_{0}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{3}\ \mathrm{d}x\bigg|=\bigg|\int_{\mathcal{S}(0)}\rho_{\mathcal{S}}\dot{h}\ \mathrm{d}x\bigg|\leq m\|\dot{h}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T)}\lesssim\sqrt{m}(E_{0}+1)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

\bullet
Let us turn to
I_{1}
. Due to
\mathbf{w}_{h}|_{\mathcal{S}_{h}}=\mathbf{e}_{3}
, we see that
I_{1}^{s}=0
since
\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{w}_{h})=0
there. Hence, we calculate

\displaystyle|I_{1}|

\displaystyle=|I_{1}^{f}|\lesssim\int_{0}^{T}\zeta\|\rho\|_{L^{\gamma}(\mathcal{F}_{h}))}\|\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{p^{\ast}}(\Omega)}^{2}\|\nabla\mathbf{w}_{h}\|_{L^{\frac{p^{\ast}\gamma}{p^{\ast}(\gamma-1)-2\gamma}}(\Omega)}\ \mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\rho\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{\gamma}(\mathcal{F}_{h}))}\|\nabla\mathbf{w}_{h}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{\frac{p^{\ast}\gamma}{p^{\ast}(\gamma-1)-2\gamma}}(\Omega))}\int_{0}^{T}\zeta\|\nabla\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}^{2}\ \mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\lesssim(E_{0}+1)^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}\|\zeta\|_{L^{\frac{p}{p-2}}(0,T)}\|\nabla\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{p}((0,T)\times\Omega)}^{2}\lesssim(E_{0}+1)^{\frac{1}{\gamma}+\frac{2}{p}}T^{1-\frac{2}{p}},

by using the estimate (
2.5
) and Lemma
3.1
under the condition

\displaystyle\frac{p^{\ast}\gamma}{p^{\ast}(\gamma-1)-2\gamma}<\frac{3+\alpha}{1+2\alpha}\Leftrightarrow\alpha<\frac{2p^{\ast}\gamma-3p^{\ast}-6\gamma}{p^{\ast}\gamma+p^{\ast}+2\gamma}=\frac{3(4p\gamma-3p-6\gamma)}{p\gamma+3p+6\gamma}.

Let us emphasize that this term is the only place where the assumption
p\geq 2
is needed.

Collecting all the requirements made above, we infer

\displaystyle\gamma>\frac{3}{2},\ \ 2\leq p<3,\ \ p\gamma>p+\gamma,\ \ 4p\gamma>3p+6\gamma,

which translates into

\displaystyle\frac{3}{2}<\gamma\leq 3,\ \frac{6\gamma}{4\gamma-3}<p<3,\quad\text{or}\quad\gamma>3,\ 2\leq p<3.

Note further that for any
\gamma\geq\frac{3}{2}
and any
\frac{\gamma}{\gamma-1}<p<3
,

\displaystyle\frac{3(4p\gamma-3p-6\gamma)}{p\gamma+3p+6\gamma}\leq\frac{9(p\gamma-p-\gamma)}{2p\gamma+3p+3\gamma}\leq\frac{3(\gamma-1)}{\gamma+1}\leq 3(\gamma-1),

and that all estimates are independent of the choice of
\zeta
. Hence, we can take a sequence
\zeta_{k}\to 1
in
L^{r}([0,T))
for some suitable
r>1
without changing the bounds obtained (again, (
3.9
) is a suitable choice). In turn, collecting all the estimates above, we finally arrive at

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}mgT\leq C_{0}(1+\sqrt{m}+\sqrt{m}^{-1})\bigg(1+(E_{0}+1)^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2\gamma}}+(E_{0}+1)^{\frac{1}{2}}+(E_{0}+1)^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle\quad+(E_{0}+1)^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{\gamma}+\frac{1}{p}}+g(E_{0}+1)^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}+(E_{0}+1)^{\frac{1}{\gamma}+\frac{2}{p}}\bigg)(1+T^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}}+T^{\frac{1}{p}}+T^{1-\frac{2}{p}}+T),

which, after dividing by
\frac{1}{2}mg
, noticing that due to
p\geq 2
the largest exponent is
\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{\gamma}+\frac{1}{p}
, and using Young’s inequality (
1.6
) on several terms, leads to

\displaystyle T\leq C_{0}\max\{m^{-1/2},m^{-3/2}\}\bigg(1+E_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{\gamma}+\frac{1}{p}}\bigg)(1+T),

(3.13)

where
C_{0}
only depends on
p,\gamma,g,\alpha
, the bounds on
\mathbf{w}_{h}
obtained in Lemma
3.1
, and the implicit constant appearing in (
2.5
), provided

\displaystyle\alpha<\min\bigg\{\frac{3-p}{2p-1},\frac{3(4p\gamma-3p-6\gamma)}{p\gamma+3p+6\gamma}\bigg\}\quad\text{with}

\displaystyle\frac{3}{2}<\gamma\leq 3,\ \frac{6\gamma}{4\gamma-3}<p<3,\quad\text{or}\quad\gamma>3,\ 2\leq p<3.

Recalling the definition of
E_{0}
from (
2.6
) as

\displaystyle E_{0}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{F}(0)}\bigg(\frac{|\mathbf{m}_{0}|^{2}}{2\rho_{0}}+P(\rho_{0},\vartheta_{0})\bigg)\ \mathrm{d}x+\frac{m}{2}|\mathbf{V}_{0}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{J}(0)\omega_{0}\cdot\omega_{0},

\displaystyle\mathbb{J}(0)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{S}_{0}-\mathbf{G}_{0}}\rho_{\mathcal{S}}\Big(|x|^{2}{\mathbb{I}}-x\otimes x\Big)\ \mathrm{d}x,

we see that collision can occur only if the solid’s mass in (
3.13
) is large enough, meaning in fact its density is very high. Heuristically, this shall be clear: a light object would swim rather than sink. Since
E_{0}
depends on the solid’s mass, we require the solid initially to have low vertical and rotational speed. More precisely, choosing
\mathbf{V}_{0}
and
\omega_{0}
such that
|\mathbf{V}_{0}|,|\omega_{0}|=\mathcal{O}(m^{-\frac{1}{2}})
, and choosing
m
high enough such that

\displaystyle C_{0}\max\{m^{-1/2},m^{-3/2}\}\bigg(1+E_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{\gamma}+\frac{1}{p}}\bigg)<1,

(3.14)

the solid touches the boundary of
\Omega
in finite time, ending the proof of Theorem
2.2
.

Remark 3.3
.

We see that if, by change, the constant
C_{0}<1
small enough, then we can get rid of the assumption on the smallness of
\mathbf{V}_{0}
and
\omega_{0}
by also choosing
m<1
. Indeed, in this case
\max\{m^{-1/2},m^{-3/2}\}=m^{-3/2}
and
E_{0}\lesssim 1
. Hence, for appropriate values
m<1
and
C_{0}m^{-3/2}<1
, inequality (
3.14
) can still be valid.

Remark 3.4
.

For the case
d=2
, although the construction of
\mathbf{w}_{h}
is slightly different (see Section
6.2
), the same proof of Lemma
3.1
shows

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{w}_{h}\|_{L^{q}(\Omega_{h,r_{0}})}

\displaystyle\lesssim 1\text{ for any }q<1+\frac{2}{\alpha},

\displaystyle\|\partial_{h}\mathbf{w}_{h}\|_{L^{q}(\Omega_{h,r_{0}})}+\|\nabla\mathbf{w}_{h}\|_{L^{q}(\Omega_{h,r_{0}})}

\displaystyle\lesssim 1\text{ for any }q<\frac{2+\alpha}{1+2\alpha},

the range of
q
we shall compare with the one from Remark
3.2
and Theorem
6.1
. Regarding the estimate of
I_{4}
, we thus find again
|I_{4}|\lesssim(E_{0}+1)^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}}T^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}}
provided

\displaystyle p<\frac{2+\alpha}{1+2\alpha}\Leftrightarrow\alpha<\frac{2-p}{2p-1},\quad 1<p<2.

The estimate for the convective term
I_{1}
, however, still needs
p\geq 2
to comply with the square integrability of
\nabla\mathbf{u}
in time. Hence, for the two-dimensional Navier-Stokes equations, we cannot conclude that collision happens. On the other hand, if we drop the convective term, the estimates for the fluid parts of the integrals remain the same provided

\displaystyle\text{for }I_{2}^{f}:\quad\frac{2\gamma}{\gamma-1}<1+\frac{2}{\alpha}\Leftrightarrow\alpha<\frac{2(\gamma-1)}{\gamma+1},

\displaystyle\text{for }I_{5}:\quad\frac{\gamma}{\gamma-1}<1+\frac{2}{\alpha}\Leftrightarrow\alpha<2(\gamma-1),

\displaystyle\text{for }I_{3}:\quad\frac{p^{\ast}}{p^{\ast}(\gamma-1)-\gamma}<\frac{2+\alpha}{1+2\alpha},

where now
p^{\ast}=2p/(2-p)
. This last condition is equivalent to

\displaystyle\alpha<\frac{p^{\ast}\gamma-2p^{\ast}-2\gamma}{p^{\ast}\gamma+p^{\ast}+\gamma}=\frac{4(p\gamma-p-\gamma)}{p\gamma+2p+2\gamma},\quad p>\frac{\gamma}{\gamma-1}.

Hence, we find that collision for the two-dimensional compressible Stokes equations happens provided

\displaystyle\frac{\gamma}{\gamma-1}<p<2,\quad\gamma>2,\quad\alpha<\min\left\{\frac{2-p}{2p-1},\frac{4(p\gamma-p-\gamma)}{p\gamma+2p+2\gamma}\right\}.

Note that this corresponds purely to the case of
shear-thinning
fluids, in contrast to the three-dimensional case.

Chapter 4
Specific model examples

This chapter is devoted to investigate some precise examples of (Navier-)Stokes and Navier-Stokes-Fourier equations as well as stress tensors
\mathbb{S}
fulfilling the requirements
(S1)
–
(S3)
. We start with the easiest case of incompressible Stokes equations, and will end with a model of compressible, heat conducting, non-Newtonian fluids. Again, we strongly remark that the question of
existence
of weak and/or strong solutions is just known in some special cases, and sometimes even just for fluids without immersed bodies. However, as the system of fluid-structure can be written as a single system of pure-fluid type, for which existence results where obtained, it is not unreasonable to assume that such a solution with the desired properties exists. We will give some references to available existence results at appropriate places. Moreover, the fact that collision might occur shows that the solutions, if available, have just
finite time of existence
; in particular, they cannot be prolonged after the time
T_{\ast}>0
where collision happens, which mathematically results in a blow-up of some
L^{q}
-norms of the velocity’s gradient (see also Remark
2.5
).

4.1
Incompressible Stokes

Probably the easiest model of fluid flow around a rigid object travelling through the fluid is given by the following set of linear, incompressible Stokes equations:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\div\mathbf{u}=0&\text{in }\mathcal{F}(t),\\
\partial_{t}\mathbf{u}-\div\mathbb{S}+\nabla p=-g\mathbf{e}_{3}&\text{in }\mathcal{F}(t),\\
\mathbf{u}=0&\text{on }\partial\Omega,\\
\mathbf{u}=\dot{\mathbf{G}}(t)+\omega(t)\times(x-\mathbf{G}(t))&\text{on }\partial\mathcal{S}(t),\\
m\ddot{\mathbf{G}}=-\int_{\partial\mathcal{S}}(\mathbb{S}-p\mathbb{I})\mathbf{n}\ \mathrm{d}\sigma-\int_{\mathcal{S}}\rho_{\mathcal{S}}g\mathbf{e}_{3}\ \mathrm{d}x,\\
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}(\mathbb{J}\omega)=-\int_{\partial\mathcal{S}}(x-\mathbf{G})\times(\mathbb{S}-p\mathbb{I})\mathbf{n}\ \mathrm{d}\sigma-\int_{\mathcal{S}}(x-\mathbf{G})\times\rho_{\mathcal{S}}g\mathbf{e}_{3}\ \mathrm{d}x,\\
\mathbf{u}(0)=\mathbf{u}_{0},\ \mathbf{G}(0)=\mathbf{G}_{0},\ \dot{\mathbf{G}}(0)=\mathbf{V}_{0},\ \omega(0)=\omega_{0}&\text{in }\mathcal{F}(0),\end{cases}

(4.1)

where
(\rho_{\mathcal{S}},m,g)\in(0,\infty)^{3}
,
\mathbb{J}\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}
, and
\mathbf{u}_{0},\mathbf{G}_{0},\mathbf{V}_{0},\omega_{0}
are as before. The stress tensor is given by Newton’s rheological law

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}=2\mu\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{u})=\mu\big(\nabla\mathbf{u}+\nabla^{T}\mathbf{u}\big),\quad\mu>0,

thus the term
\div\mathbb{S}
can also be written in the more common form
\mu\Delta\mathbf{u}
, and we set the (constant) fluid’s density to be equal to
1
. As easily seen,
\mathbb{S}
fulfils all the requirements stated in
(S1)
–
(S3)
. Indeed, continuity
(S1)
is obvious from the definition of
\mathbb{S}
. Regarding monotonicity
(S2)
, for all
\mathbb{M},\mathbb{N}\in\mathbb{R}_{\rm sym}^{3\times 3}
we have

\displaystyle[\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{M})-\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{N})]:(\mathbb{M}-\mathbb{N})=2\mu|\mathbb{M}-\mathbb{N}|^{2}\geq 0.

Setting
\mathbb{N}=0
in the above, we conclude the growth condition
(S3)
by choosing
c_{0}=c_{1}=2\mu>0
,
\delta=0
, and
p=2
.

The definition of weak solutions for system (
4.1
) is classical. For completeness, we state it here.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
\mathbf{u}_{0}\in L^{2}(\Omega)
such that
\div\mathbf{u}_{0}=0
and
\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{u}_{0})=0
on
\mathcal{S}
. We say that
\mathbf{u}\in L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))\cap L^{2}(0,T;W_{0}^{1,2}(\Omega))
with
\div\mathbf{u}=0
is a finite energy weak solution to (
4.1
) if:

•

The weak formulation of the momentum equation holds:

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\mathbf{u}(\tau)\cdot\phi(\tau)\ \mathrm{d}x-\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{\Omega}\mathbf{u}\cdot\partial_{t}\phi\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{\Omega}\mathbb{S}:\nabla\phi\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\mathbf{u}_{0}\cdot\phi(0)\ \mathrm{d}x+\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{\Omega}\mathbf{f}\cdot\phi\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}t

with
\mathbf{f}=-g\mathbf{e}_{3}
, for any
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}([0,T)\times\Omega)
such that
\div\phi=0
and
\mathbb{D}(\phi)=0
in a neighborhood of
\mathcal{S}
;

•

The energy inequality

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\frac{1}{2}|\mathbf{u}|^{2}(\tau)\ \mathrm{d}x+\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{\Omega}\mathbb{S}:\nabla\mathbf{u}\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}t\leq\int_{\Omega}\frac{1}{2}|\mathbf{u}_{0}|^{2}\ \mathrm{d}x+\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{\Omega}\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathbf{u}\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}t

(4.2)

holds for almost any
\tau\in[0,T]
, where
\mathbf{f}=-g\mathbf{e}_{3}
.

We note that the energy
equality
formally follows from multiplying the momentum equation by the solution
\mathbf{u}
, and indeed holds for smooth (classical) solutions. That it is replaced in (
4.2
) by an
inequality
is due to the fact that merely
weak
solutions can dissipate more energy than expected. Another point of view is that norms are just weakly lower semi-continuous rather than continuous, thus the former equality changes into inequality.

By the classical theory for Stokes and Navier-Stokes equations (see
[
15
,
36
,
37
]
), we can state the following

Theorem 4.2
.

Let the initial datum
\mathbf{u}_{0}\in L^{2}(\Omega)
with
\div\mathbf{u}_{0}=0
and
\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{u}_{0})=0
on
\mathcal{S}
. Then there exists a weak solution to system (
4.1
) in the sense of Definition
4.1
.

From inequality (
4.2
), we immediately get the desired bounds on the velocity. Indeed, using that for gravity
g\mathbf{e}_{3}=g\nabla[x\mapsto x_{3}]
, we integrate by parts and use the zero boundary conditions of
\mathbf{u}
on
\partial\Omega
to obtain

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{\Omega}\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathbf{u}\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}t=-\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{\Omega}g\mathbf{e}_{3}\cdot\mathbf{u}\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}t=\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{\Omega}gx_{3}\div\mathbf{u}\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}t=0

(4.3)

by the solenoidality of
\mathbf{u}
. Hence, using further Korn’s and Poincaré’s inequality (
1.1
)–(
1.2
), we end up with

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))}^{2}+\|\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;W_{0}^{1,2}(\Omega))}^{2}\leq C(\Omega,\mu)E_{0},\quad E_{0}=\int_{\Omega}\frac{1}{2}|\mathbf{u}_{0}|^{2}\ \mathrm{d}x.

In view of the above, Theorem
2.2
is applicable and we ensure collision as long as
\alpha<\frac{1}{3}
.

Remark 4.3
.

As already stated, collision can be proven even for the case
\alpha\geq\frac{1}{3}
by means of analyzing the drag force and the corresponding ODE for the distance
h(t)
, see Chapter
6
and
[
23
, Section 3.1]
.

Remark 4.4
.

To get a lean notation in the following sections, we emphasize that the equations (
4.1
)
3
–(
4.1
)
6
will not change in any model under consideration, so we skip their occurrence later on. Moreover, in the case of compressible and/or heat conducting fluids, the initial conditions (
4.1
)
7
are completed with initial conditions for the new unknowns, so we also omit them in the sequel.

4.2
Incompressible non-Newtonian Navier-Stokes

Another well-known model is the non-linear Navier-Stokes system as investigated in
[
4
,
11
,
36
,
52
]
(for the pure fluid system). With regard to Remark
4.4
, we modify the momentum equation (
4.1
)
2
in the following way:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathbf{u}+\div(\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{u})-\div\mathbb{S}+\nabla p=-g\mathbf{e}_{3}\quad\text{in }\mathcal{F}(t),

(4.4)

where the stress tensor
\mathbb{S}=\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{u}))
now is just assumed to satisfy the assumptions
(S1)
–
(S3)
with
\vartheta\equiv\text{constant}
. All other equations in (
4.1
) remain unchanged. Also, the notion of weak solutions as given in Definition
4.1
is the same with obvious changes in the weak momentum formulation due to the presence of the convective term, and the energy inequality (
4.2
) still remains valid due to

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\div(\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{u})\cdot\mathbf{u}\ \mathrm{d}x=\int_{\Omega}\frac{1}{2}\div(|\mathbf{u}|^{2}\mathbf{u})\ \mathrm{d}x=\int_{\partial\Omega}\frac{1}{2}|\mathbf{u}|^{2}\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{n}\ \mathrm{d}x=0.

Using the growth condition
(S3)
on
\mathbb{S}
, we obtain in the same way as before the uniform bounds

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))}^{2}+\|\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{p}(0,T;W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega))}^{p}\leq C(\Omega,\delta,c_{0},c_{1},p)(E_{0}+1).

Hence, Theorem
2.2
yields collision as long as
2\leq p<3
and
\alpha<\frac{3-p}{2p-1}
. We recall that it seems reasonable that
\alpha\to 0
as
p\to 3
, since the lower tip of the solid shall be “sharper” for a shear-thickening fluid to “cut” through it. Moreover, neglecting the convective part
\div(\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{u})
to get a non-Newtonian incompressible Stokes system, we can handle all
p\in(1,3)
and all
\alpha<\frac{3-p}{2p-1}
, thus also allowing for shear-thinning fluids.

4.3
Incompressible non-Newtonian Navier-Stokes-Fourier

Regarding heat conducting fluids, we have to insert in the model equations for the temperature. Moreover, the energy
inequality
satisfied for weak solutions is now replaced by an energy
equality
, together with an additional
entropy inequality
. The new fluid’s system reads

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\div\mathbf{u}=0&\text{in }\mathcal{F}(t),\\
\partial_{t}\mathbf{u}+\div(\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{u})-\div\mathbb{S}+\nabla p=-g\mathbf{e}_{3}&\text{in }\mathcal{F}(t),\\
\partial_{t}\vartheta+\mathbf{u}\cdot\nabla\vartheta+\div\mathbf{q}=\frac{1}{\vartheta}\Big(\mathbb{S}:\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{u})-\frac{\mathbf{q}(\vartheta,\nabla\vartheta)\cdot\nabla\vartheta}{\vartheta}\Big)&\text{in }\mathcal{F}(t),\\
\big[\mathbf{q}(\vartheta,\nabla\vartheta)-\mathbf{q}(\vartheta_{s},\nabla\vartheta_{s})\big]\cdot\mathbf{n}=0&\text{on }\partial\mathcal{S}(t),\\
\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{n}=0&\text{on }\partial\Omega,\end{cases}

where the heat flow vector is given by Fourier’s law

\displaystyle\mathbf{q}(\vartheta,\nabla\vartheta)=-\kappa(\vartheta)\nabla\vartheta,

and the heat conductivity
\kappa
is assumed to be a continuous function of the temperature satisfying
\kappa(\vartheta)\sim 1+\vartheta^{\beta}
for some
\beta>1
. Such models where investigated in
[
39
]
for the case of so-called Boussinesq approximation, where
g\mathbf{e}_{3}
is replaced by
g\vartheta\mathbf{e}_{3}
, and collision was ruled out as the growth parameter
p\geq 4
, as well as in
[
41
]
and
[
6
]
. In the latter reference, the constructed solutions satisfy the energy inequality

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\int_{\Omega}\frac{1}{2}|\mathbf{u}|^{2}+\vartheta\ \mathrm{d}x\leq 0,

from which we infer

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))}^{2}+\|\vartheta\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{1}(\Omega))}\leq E_{0}.

Note that this does not provide us with any information on the gradient of the velocity. However, the entropy inequality given by

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\int_{\Omega}\frac{1}{\vartheta}\bigg(\mathbb{S}:\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{u})-\frac{\mathbf{q}\cdot\nabla\vartheta}{\vartheta}\bigg)\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}t\leq 0

forces

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{p}(0,T;W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega))}^{p}+\|\nabla\log\vartheta\|_{L^{2}((0,T)\times\Omega)}^{2}+\|\nabla\vartheta^{\frac{\beta}{2}}\|_{L^{2}((0,T)\times\Omega)}^{2}\lesssim E_{0}.

Recalling that the temperature estimates are not important for our analysis, we again arrive at the required estimate (
2.5
).

4.4
Compressible Navier-Stokes

Taking into account that the density of the fluid might change over time and also from one position in space to another (as is the case for the prime example of gases
1
1

1

Note however carefully that even liquids are compressible: water has a compressibility of about
5\cdot 10^{-10}\,{\rm Pa}^{-1}
, which is clearly “almost zero” compared to air with compressibility approximately
1\,{\rm Pa}^{-1}
.
), we have to modify the fluid’s system accordingly. More precisely, it now reads

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\rho+\div(\rho\mathbf{u})=0&\text{in }\mathcal{F}(t),\\
\partial_{t}(\rho\mathbf{u})+\div(\rho\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{u})-\div\mathbb{S}+\nabla p(\rho)=-\rho g\mathbf{e}_{3}&\text{in }\mathcal{F}(t),\\
\rho(0)=\rho_{0},\ (\rho\mathbf{u})(0)=\mathbf{m}_{0}&\text{in }\mathcal{F}(0),\end{cases}

(4.5)

where in contrast to the incompressible models in the preceding sections, the pressure is now a function of the unknown density
\rho
. To specify the pressure growth given in (
2.7
), we will assume a barotropic pressure law of the form

\displaystyle p\in C([0,\infty))\cap C^{2}((0,\infty)),\quad p(0)=0,\quad p^{\prime}(\rho)>0\ (\rho>0),\quad\lim_{\rho\to\infty}\frac{p^{\prime}(\rho)}{\rho^{\gamma-1}}=p_{\infty}>0,\quad\gamma>\frac{3}{2}.

The easiest example of such a pressure is given by the usually used barotropic law
p(\rho)=\rho^{\gamma}
, although other examples are possible. The stress tensor is given by Newton’s law

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}=2\mu\bigg(\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{u})-\frac{1}{3}\div\mathbf{u}\mathbb{I}\bigg)+\eta\div\mathbf{u}\mathbb{I},\quad\mu>0,\,\eta\geq 0,

and fulfils requirements
(S1)
–
(S3)
the same way as in the incompressible case (
4.1
). Since the former part of
\mathbb{S}
is trace-free, the parameter
\mu
is called
shear viscosity
, whereas
\eta
is commonly known as the
bulk viscosity
. The definition of weak solutions is also similar to the one for incompressible fluids, and existence of such was shown in
[
15
]
.

Definition 4.5
.

Let
\gamma>\frac{3}{2}
,
\rho_{0}\in L^{\gamma}(\Omega)
, and
\mathbf{m}_{0}\in L^{\frac{2\gamma}{\gamma+1}}(\Omega)
, together with the compatibility conditions

\displaystyle\rho_{0}\geq 0,\quad\mathbf{m}_{0}=0\text{ whenenver }\rho_{0}=0,\quad\frac{|\mathbf{m}_{0}|^{2}}{\rho_{0}}\in L^{1}(\Omega).

We call a couple
(\rho,\mathbf{u})
a finite energy weak solution of system (
4.5
) if:

•

The solution belongs to the regularity class

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}\in L^{2}(0,T;W_{0}^{1,2}(\Omega)),\quad\rho\in L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{\gamma}(\Omega)),\quad\rho|\mathbf{u}|^{2}\in L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{1}(\Omega)),

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{u})=0\text{ on }\mathcal{S},\quad\rho\geq 0\text{ a.e.\penalty\ in }(0,T)\times\Omega,\quad\rho=\rho_{\mathcal{S}}\text{ on }\mathcal{S};

•

The weak formulation of the continuity equation holds:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{\Omega}\rho(\partial_{t}\psi+\mathbf{u}\cdot\nabla\psi)\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}t=\int_{\Omega}\rho(\tau)\psi(\tau)\ \mathrm{d}x-\int_{\Omega}\rho_{0}\psi(0)\ \mathrm{d}x

for any
\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}([0,T)\times\overline{\Omega})
;

•

The weak formulation of the momentum equation holds:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{\Omega}\rho\mathbf{u}\cdot\partial_{t}\phi+\rho\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{u}:\nabla\phi+p(\rho)\div\phi-\mathbb{S}:\nabla\phi+\rho\mathbf{f}\cdot\phi\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}(\rho\mathbf{u})(\tau)\cdot\phi(\tau)\ \mathrm{d}x-\int_{\Omega}\mathbf{m}_{0}\cdot\phi(0)\ \mathrm{d}x

with
\mathbf{f}=-g\mathbf{e}_{3}
, for any
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}([0,T)\times\Omega)
such that
\mathbb{D}(\phi)=0
in a neighborhood of
\mathcal{S}
;

•

The energy inequality

\displaystyle\left[\int_{\Omega}\frac{1}{2}\rho|\mathbf{u}|^{2}+P(\rho)\ \mathrm{d}x\right]_{t=0}^{t=\tau}+\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{\Omega}\mathbb{S}:\nabla\mathbf{u}\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}t\leq\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{\Omega}\rho\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathbf{u}\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}t

(4.6)

holds for almost every
\tau\in[0,T]
, where
\mathbf{f}=-g\mathbf{e}_{3}
,
(\rho|\mathbf{u}|^{2})(0):=|\mathbf{m}_{0}|^{2}/\rho_{0}
, and the pressure potential
P
is determined by

\displaystyle\rho P^{\prime}(\rho)-P(\rho)=p(\rho),\quad P^{\prime\prime}(\rho)=p^{\prime}(\rho)/\rho.

(4.7)

From the definition of
P
in (
4.7
), it follows immediately that
P
essentially behaves as
p
; indeed, in the case where
p(\rho)=\rho^{\gamma}
, we have
P(\rho)=\rho^{\gamma}/(\gamma-1)
. Moreover, from the definition of
p(\rho)
, we see that there is an absolute constant
C_{P}(\gamma)>0
such that

\displaystyle C_{P}^{-1}P(\rho)\leq\rho^{\gamma}\leq C_{P}P(\rho)\quad\forall\rho\geq 0.

Regarding the force term, we use the same trick as before to rewrite
\mathbf{e}_{3}=\nabla[x\mapsto x_{3}]
and use the continuity equation to infer

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{\Omega}g\mathbf{e}_{3}\cdot\rho\mathbf{u}\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{\Omega}gx_{3}\div(\rho\mathbf{u})\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}t=\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{\Omega}gx_{3}\partial_{t}\rho\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}gx_{3}(\rho(\tau)-\rho(0))\ \mathrm{d}x\leq 2\|\rho\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{\gamma}(\Omega))}\|gx_{3}\|_{L^{1}(0,T;L^{\gamma^{\prime}}(\Omega))}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2C_{P}}\|\rho\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{\gamma}(\Omega))}^{\gamma}+C(\Omega,\gamma,g)\leq\frac{1}{2}\|P(\rho)\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{1}(\Omega))}+C(\Omega,\gamma,g).

Thus, using Grönwall’s inequality (
1.5
), the energy inequality (
4.6
) implies
