\displaystyle\|\rho\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{\gamma}(\Omega))}^{\gamma}+\|\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;W_{0}^{1,2}(\Omega))}^{2}+\|\rho|\mathbf{u}|^{2}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{1}(\Omega))}\leq C(\Omega,\mu,\eta,\gamma,g)(E_{0}+1),

which is precisely inequality (
2.5
) for constant temperature and
p=2
.

4.5
Incompressible inhomogeneous Navier-Stokes

The model (
4.5
) takes the full compressibility of the gas into account. If one wants to focus on deep water, this model is in some sense “too accurate”. One can thus investigate an “in-between-case” of density dependent, but incompressible flow. The equations (
4.5
) stay the same with the additional constraint that
\div\mathbf{u}=0
. Note that this forces the pressure
p
to be, like in the purely incompressible case, the Lagrange multiplier for the incompressibility condition and hence, no constitutive relation for
p
is needed. Definition
4.5
of weak solutions modifies in the following way:

Definition 4.6
.

Let
\rho_{0}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)
with
\rho_{0}\geq 0
, and
\mathbf{u}_{0}\in L^{2}(\Omega)
with
\div\mathbf{u}_{0}=0
and
\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{u}_{0})=0
on
\mathcal{S}
. We call a couple
(\rho,\mathbf{u})
a finite energy weak solution of system (
4.5
), supplemented with
\div\mathbf{u}=0
, if:

•

The solution belongs to the regularity class

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}\in L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))\cap L^{2}(0,T;W_{0}^{1,2}(\Omega)),\quad\rho\in L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{\infty}(\Omega)),

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{u})=0\text{ on }\mathcal{S},\quad\rho\geq 0\text{ a.e.\penalty\ in }(0,T)\times\Omega,\quad\rho=\rho_{\mathcal{S}}\text{ on }\mathcal{S},

\displaystyle\|\rho\|_{L^{p}(0,T;L^{p}(\Omega))}=\|\rho_{0}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\quad\text{for any }p\in[1,\infty),

\displaystyle\|\rho(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}=\|\rho_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\text{ for a.e. }t\in[0,T];

•

The weak formulation of the continuity equation holds:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{\Omega}\rho(\partial_{t}\psi+\mathbf{u}\cdot\nabla\psi)\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}t=\int_{\Omega}\rho(\tau)\psi(\tau)\ \mathrm{d}x-\int_{\Omega}\rho_{0}\psi(0)\ \mathrm{d}x

for any
\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}([0,T)\times\overline{\Omega})
;

•

The weak formulation of the momentum equation holds:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{\Omega}\rho\mathbf{u}\cdot\partial_{t}\phi+\rho\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{u}:\nabla\phi-\mathbb{S}:\nabla\phi+\rho\mathbf{f}\cdot\phi\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}(\rho\mathbf{u})(\tau)\cdot\phi(\tau)\ \mathrm{d}x-\int_{\Omega}\mathbf{m}_{0}\cdot\phi(0)\ \mathrm{d}x

with
\mathbf{f}=-g\mathbf{e}_{3}
, for any
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}([0,T)\times\Omega)
such that
\div\phi=0
and
\mathbb{D}(\phi)=0
in a neighborhood of
\mathcal{S}
;

•

The energy inequality

\displaystyle\left[\int_{\Omega}\frac{1}{2}\rho|\mathbf{u}|^{2}\ \mathrm{d}x\right]_{t=0}^{t=\tau}+\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{\Omega}\mathbb{S}:\nabla\mathbf{u}\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}t\leq\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{\Omega}\rho\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathbf{u}\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}t

(4.8)

holds for almost every
\tau\in[0,T]
, where
\mathbf{f}=-g\mathbf{e}_{3}
.

Existence of weak solutions to the above problem in 2D and no vacuum
\rho_{0}\geq\underline{\rho}>0
, even for several bodies, has been shown in
[
44
, Theorem 2.1]
, where the authors also investigate collision (see Theorem 2.2 in there); note, however, that the boundary of the bodies is assumed to be of class
C^{2}
. For the speed of collision, if it happens at time
T_{*}\in[0,T]
, they derive for the height function
h(t)=\dist(\mathcal{S},\partial\Omega)
that

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to T_{*}}h(t)|t-T_{*}|^{-\eta}=0,\qquad\eta=2.

Indeed, the exponent
\eta=2
here is not surprising: we remark that this leads to
\beta=\frac{3}{4}
in Theorem
6.1
below. This fraction will occur later on again, see Remark
7.4
, and has to do with the fact that solids of class
C^{2}
roughly should behave like balls, i.e., the Hölder exponent is
\alpha=1
. For
\alpha\in[0,1)
, although an existence results seems to be missing in the literature, our collision result would then achieved in the same way as before, namely by noting that the force term is estimated as

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{\Omega}\rho\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathbf{u}\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}gx_{3}(\rho_{0}-\rho(\tau))\ \mathrm{d}x\leq 2\|gx_{3}\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}\|\rho_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)},

where we used that
\|\rho(\tau)\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}=\|\rho_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}
. Hence, we end up with

\displaystyle\|\rho\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{\infty}(\Omega))}+\|\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;W_{0}^{1,2}(\Omega))}^{2}+\|\rho|\mathbf{u}|^{2}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{1}(\Omega))}\leq C(\Omega,\mu,\eta,g)(E_{0}+1),

which is inequality (
2.5
) again upon replacing
P(\rho_{0},\vartheta_{0})
by
\|\rho_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}
in (
2.6
). Note especially that fits the computations of the foregoing section and thus yields Theorem
2.2
with
\gamma=\infty
.

4.6
Compressible non-Newtonian Navier-Stokes-Fourier

We close this chapter in putting together all the systems above to get a model for heat conducting, compressible, non-Newtonian fluids. The equations read

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\rho+\div(\rho\mathbf{u})=0&\text{in }\mathcal{F}(t),\\
\partial_{t}(\rho\mathbf{u})+\div(\rho\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{u})-\div\mathbb{S}+\nabla p(\rho,\vartheta)=-\rho g\mathbf{e}_{3}&\text{in }\mathcal{F}(t),\\
\partial_{t}(\rho s)+\div(\rho s\mathbf{u})+\div\frac{\mathbf{q}}{\vartheta}=\sigma&\text{in }\mathcal{F}(t).\end{cases}

Here,
s=s(\rho,\vartheta)
is the specific entropy, which is related to the internal energy
e=e(\rho,\vartheta)
, the pressure
p=p(\rho,\vartheta)
, the density
\rho
, and the temperature
\vartheta
through Gibbs’ relation

\displaystyle\vartheta Ds=De+pD\bigg(\frac{1}{\rho}\bigg).

(4.9)

Further, the entropy production rate
\sigma
fulfils

\displaystyle\sigma\geq\frac{1}{\vartheta}\bigg(\mathbb{S}:\nabla\mathbf{u}-\frac{\mathbf{q}\cdot\nabla\vartheta}{\vartheta}\bigg)

in the sense of measures. Moreover, we will impose some constitutive relations, the precise motivation behind can be found in
[
19
, Section 1.4]
(for
\gamma=\frac{5}{3}
):

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} p(\rho,\vartheta)&=p_{\rm m}(\rho,\vartheta)+p_{\rm rad}(\vartheta),&p_{\rm m}(\rho,\vartheta)&=\vartheta^{\frac{\gamma}{\gamma-1}}\mathfrak{P}\bigg(\frac{\rho}{\vartheta^{\frac{1}{\gamma-1}}}\bigg),&p_{\rm rad}(\vartheta)&=\frac{a}{3}\vartheta^{4},\\
e(\rho,\vartheta)&=e_{\rm m}(\rho,\vartheta)+e_{\rm rad}(\vartheta),&e_{\rm m}(\rho,\vartheta)&=\frac{1}{\gamma-1}\frac{\vartheta^{\frac{\gamma}{\gamma-1}}}{\rho}\mathfrak{P}\bigg(\frac{\rho}{\vartheta^{\frac{1}{\gamma-1}}}\bigg),&e_{\rm rad}(\vartheta)&=\frac{a}{\rho}\vartheta^{4},\\
s(\rho,\vartheta)&=s_{\rm m}(\rho,\vartheta)+s_{\rm rad}(\vartheta),&s_{\rm m}(\rho,\vartheta)&=\mathfrak{S}\bigg(\frac{\rho}{\vartheta^{\frac{1}{\gamma-1}}}\bigg),&s_{\rm rad}(\vartheta)&=\frac{4a}{3}\frac{\vartheta^{3}}{\rho},\end{aligned}

(4.10)

where
a>0
is the Stefan-Boltzmann constant,
\mathfrak{P}\in C([0,\infty))\cap C^{2}((0,\infty))
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathfrak{P}(0)=0,\quad\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}(Z)>0\ (Z>0),\quad 0<\frac{\gamma\mathfrak{P}(Z)-\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}(Z)Z}{Z}\leq c\ (Z\geq 0),

and

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}(Z)=-\frac{1}{\gamma-1}\frac{\gamma\mathfrak{P}(Z)-\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}(Z)Z}{Z^{2}}<0.

It follows that the function
Z\mapsto\mathfrak{P}(Z)/Z^{\gamma}
is decreasing, and we assume

\displaystyle\lim_{Z\to\infty}\frac{\mathfrak{P}(Z)}{Z^{\gamma}}=p_{\infty}>0.

Note in particular that
p(\rho,\vartheta)
complies with the growth assumption (
2.7
).

Similar to the incompressible heat-conducting case, we have inequalities for the energy and entropy, finally resulting in uniform bounds

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{p}(0,T;W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega))}^{p}+\|\rho\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{\gamma}(\Omega))}^{\gamma}+\|\rho|\mathbf{u}|^{2}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{1}(\Omega))}\lesssim E_{0}+1,

where the implicit constant depends on the data. Again, these bounds enable us to conclude.

Chapter 5
Newtonian flow with temperature-growing viscosities

In this short chapter, we investigate a different model for viscosity that does not fit into the assumptions
(S1)
–
(S3)
: viscosities that can grow to infinity as the temperature does. The version of what we present here was earlier given in
[
40
]
. To fix the setting, let

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}=2\mu(\vartheta)\bigg(\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{u})-\frac{1}{3}\div\mathbf{u}\mathbb{I}\bigg)+\eta(\vartheta)\div\mathbf{u}\mathbb{I},

where the viscosity coefficients
\mu,\eta
are assumed to be continuous functions on
(0,\infty)
,
\mu
is moreover Lipschitz continuous, and they satisfy

\displaystyle 1+\vartheta

\displaystyle\lesssim\mu(\vartheta),\quad|\mu^{\prime}|\lesssim 1,

\displaystyle 0\leq\eta(\vartheta)\lesssim 1+\vartheta.

Note that this means we consider a Newtonian fluid with growing viscosities that are
not
uniformly bounded in the temperature variable, thus not fulfilling
(S3)
.

The equations governing the fluid’s motion are now given by

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\rho+\div(\rho\mathbf{u})=0&\text{in }\mathcal{F},\\
\partial_{t}(\rho\mathbf{u})+\div(\rho\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{u})-\div\mathbb{S}+\nabla p(\rho,\vartheta)=\rho\mathbf{f}&\text{in }\mathcal{F},\\
m\ddot{\mathbf{G}}(t)=-\int_{\partial\mathcal{S}}(\mathbb{S}-p\mathbb{I})\mathbf{n}\ \mathrm{d}\sigma+\int_{\mathcal{S}}\rho_{\mathcal{S}}\mathbf{f}\ \mathrm{d}x&\text{in }\mathcal{F},\\
\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}(\mathbb{J}\omega)=-\int_{\partial\mathcal{S}}(x-\mathbf{G})\times(\mathbb{S}-p\mathbb{I})\mathbf{n}\ \mathrm{d}\sigma+\int_{\mathcal{S}}(x-\mathbf{G})\times\rho_{\mathcal{S}}\mathbf{f}\ \mathrm{d}x&\text{in }\mathcal{F},\\
\partial_{t}(\rho s)+\div(\rho s\mathbf{u})+\div\frac{\mathbf{q}}{\vartheta}=\frac{1}{\vartheta}\big(\mathbb{S}:\nabla\mathbf{u}-\frac{\mathbf{q}\cdot\nabla\vartheta}{\vartheta}\big)&\text{in }\mathcal{F},\\
\mathbf{u}=\dot{\mathbf{G}}(t)+\omega(t)\times(x-\mathbf{G}(t))&\text{on }\partial\mathcal{S},\\
\mathbf{u}=0&\text{on }\partial\Omega,\\
\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{n}=0&\text{on }\partial\Omega.\end{cases}

(5.1)

Here, the pressure is given by a combination of adiabatic pressure law, Boyle-Mariott law, and radiation pressure arising from the Stefan-Boltzmann law as

p(\rho,\vartheta)=p_{\infty}\rho^{\gamma}+c_{v}(\gamma-1)\rho\vartheta+\frac{a}{3}\vartheta^{4},

the heat flow vector
\mathbf{q}=\mathbf{q}(\vartheta,\nabla\vartheta)
is given by Fourier’s law

\displaystyle\mathbf{q}(\vartheta,\nabla\vartheta)=-\kappa(\vartheta)\nabla\vartheta

with the heat capacity coefficient satisfying

\displaystyle\kappa(\vartheta)\sim 1+\vartheta^{\beta}\ \text{for some}\ \beta>1,

and the specific entropy
s=s(\rho,\vartheta)
is connected to the pressure
p(\rho,\vartheta)
and the internal energy
e(\rho,\vartheta)
of the fluid through Gibbs’ relation (
4.9
). Note that this relation determines the internal energy and specific entropy as

\displaystyle e(\rho,\vartheta)=\frac{p_{\infty}}{\gamma-1}\rho^{\gamma-1}+c_{v}\vartheta+\frac{a}{\rho}\vartheta^{4},

\displaystyle s(\rho,\vartheta)=\log\bigg(\frac{\vartheta}{\rho^{\gamma-1}}\bigg)^{c_{v}}+\frac{4a}{3}\frac{\vartheta^{3}}{\rho},

where
c_{v}>0
is the specific heat capacity at constant volume. In the language of (
4.10
), this is equivalent to the choice

\displaystyle\mathfrak{P}(Z)=p_{\infty}Z^{\gamma}+c_{v}(\gamma-1)Z,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}(Z)=-c_{v}(\gamma-1)\log Z.

Note that both
\mathfrak{P}
and
\mathfrak{S}
satisfy all the assumptions of the previous chapter.

Denoting now
\vartheta_{s}
the solid’s temperature, we extend the temperature similarly to the velocity and density as

\displaystyle\vartheta=\begin{cases}\vartheta&\text{in }\mathcal{F},\\
\vartheta_{s}&\text{in }\mathcal{S},\end{cases}

and we consider the continuity of the heat flux
\mathbf{q}(\vartheta,\nabla\vartheta)\cdot\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{q}(\vartheta_{s},\nabla\vartheta_{s})\cdot\mathbf{n}
on
\partial\mathcal{S}
. Moreover, for simplicity we assume that the heat capacity coefficient of the solid is the same as the fluid’s one (this can be generalized, see
[
3
, Equation (4.23)]
).

Noticing that the existence proof presented in
[
3
]
also works for any
\beta>2
instead of the considered
\beta=3
, in such case we have the uniform bound

\displaystyle\|\vartheta^{\frac{\beta}{2}}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;W^{1,2}(\Omega))}^{2}\lesssim E_{0}+1,

where here

\displaystyle E_{0}=\int_{\mathcal{F}(0)}\frac{|\mathbf{m}_{0}|^{2}}{2\rho_{0}}+\rho_{0}e(\rho_{0},\vartheta_{0})\ \mathrm{d}x+\frac{m}{2}|\mathbf{V}_{0}|^{2}+\mathbb{J}(0)\omega_{0}\cdot\omega_{0}.

Thanks to Sobolev embedding, this yields

\displaystyle\vartheta^{\frac{\beta}{2}}\in L^{2}(0,T;L^{6}(\Omega)),\quad\text{that is,}\quad\vartheta\in L^{\beta}(0,T;L^{3\beta}(\Omega)),

in turn,

\displaystyle\|\vartheta\|_{L^{\beta}(0,T;L^{3\beta}(\Omega))}^{\beta}\lesssim E_{0}+1.

Accordingly, the estimate for the stress tensor changes into

\displaystyle\left|\int_{0}^{T}\zeta\int_{\Omega}\mathbb{S}:\nabla\mathbf{w}_{h}\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}t\right|\lesssim\int_{0}^{T}\zeta\|\vartheta\|_{L^{3\beta}(\Omega)}\|\nabla\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\|\nabla\mathbf{w}_{h}\|_{L^{\frac{6\beta}{3\beta-2}}(\Omega)}\ \mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\|\zeta\|_{L^{\frac{2\beta}{\beta-2}}(0,T)}\|\vartheta\|_{L^{\beta}(0,T;L^{3\beta}(\Omega))}\|\nabla\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{2}((0,T)\times\Omega)}\|\nabla\mathbf{w}_{h}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{\frac{\beta}{3\beta-2}}(\Omega))}

\displaystyle\lesssim(E_{0}+1)^{\frac{1}{\beta}+\frac{1}{2}}T^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\beta}},

provided

\displaystyle\frac{6\beta}{3\beta-2}<\frac{3+\alpha}{1+2\alpha}\Leftrightarrow\alpha<\frac{3(\beta-2)}{9\beta+2},

while all the other estimates stay the same. Hence, repeating the arguments from Section
3.2
, we find that collision occurs provided

\displaystyle\gamma>3,\ \beta>2,\ \alpha<\bigg\{\frac{3(\gamma-3)}{4\gamma+3},\frac{3(\beta-2)}{9\beta+2}\bigg\}.

As can be easily seen, the same arguments can be used for temperature-dependent non-Newtonian fluids, provided the stress tensor decomposes like

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}(\vartheta,\mathbb{M})=\mu(\vartheta)\tilde{\mathbb{S}}(\mathbb{M})+\eta(\vartheta)|\div\mathbf{u}|^{p-2}\div\mathbf{u}\mathbb{I}

for some tensor
\tilde{\mathbb{S}}
satisfying
(S1)
–
(S3)
, and
\mu,\eta
are as above.

Remark 5.1
.

As a matter of fact, all the analyses in this chapter also hold for the incompressible case, which (roughly speaking) corresponds to
\gamma=\infty
. Thus, collision for this type of heat conducting compressible fluids occurs if
\beta>2
and
\alpha<\frac{3(\beta-2)}{9\beta+2}
. Also here, for constant temperature corresponding to a perfectly heat conducting fluid, we recover the borderline value
\alpha<\frac{1}{3}
in the limit
\beta\to\infty
, see Remark
2.4
.

Chapter 6
Incompressible fluids: A review

In contrast to the foregoing chapters dealing with a rather general class of fluids in three spatial dimensions, in this and the following chapters we want to recall the known collision results for incompressible Newtonian fluids, for a certain range of the shape value of
\alpha
, and also comparing to results in two space dimensions. To begin with, let us recall the incompressible Navier-Stokes equations for Newtonian fluids as

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\div\mathbf{u}=0&\text{in }\mathcal{F}(t),\\
\rho_{\mathcal{F}}(\partial_{t}\mathbf{u}+\div(\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{u}))-\div\mathbb{S}+\nabla p=\rho_{\mathcal{F}}\mathbf{f}&\text{in }\mathcal{F}(t),\\
\mathbf{u}=0&\text{on }\partial\Omega,\\
\mathbf{u}(0)=\mathbf{u}_{0}&\text{in }\mathcal{F}(0),\end{cases}

(6.1)

complemented with the fluid-structure interaction terms on
\partial\mathcal{S}(t)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}m\ddot{\mathbf{G}}=-\int_{\partial\mathcal{S}}(\mathbb{S}-p\mathbb{I})\mathbf{n}\ \mathrm{d}\sigma+\int_{\mathcal{S}}\rho_{\mathcal{S}}\mathbf{f}\ \mathrm{d}x,\\
\mathbf{u}=\dot{\mathbf{G}}(t)+\omega(t)(x-\mathbf{G}(t))^{\perp}&\text{ if }d=2,\\
\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}(J\omega)=-\int_{\partial\mathcal{S}}(x-\mathbf{G})^{\perp}\cdot(\mathbb{S}-p\mathbb{I})\mathbf{n}\ \mathrm{d}\sigma+\int_{\mathcal{S}}(x-\mathbf{G})^{\perp}\cdot\rho_{\mathcal{S}}\mathbf{f}\ \mathrm{d}x&\text{ if }d=2,\\
\mathbf{u}=\dot{\mathbf{G}}(t)+\omega(t)\times(x-\mathbf{G}(t))&\text{ if }d=3,\\
\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}(\mathbb{J}\omega)=-\int_{\partial\mathcal{S}}(x-\mathbf{G})\times(\mathbb{S}-p\mathbb{I})\mathbf{n}\ \mathrm{d}\sigma+\int_{\mathcal{S}}(x-\mathbf{G})\times\rho_{\mathcal{S}}\mathbf{f}\ \mathrm{d}x&\text{ if }d=3,\\
\end{cases}

(6.2)

where

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}=2\mu\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{u})=\mu(\nabla\mathbf{u}+\nabla^{T}\mathbf{u}),\quad\mu>0,

\rho_{\mathcal{F}},\rho_{\mathcal{S}}>0
are the (constant) fluid’s and solid’s density, respectively, and, for
d=2
, the moment of inertia
J=J(t)>0
is a scalar function. Note that from the form of
\mathbb{S}
, according to
\div(\nabla^{T}\mathbf{u})=\nabla\div\mathbf{u}=0
, we also see that we may write the term
\div\mathbb{S}
in a more common way as
\mu\Delta\mathbf{u}
. For simplicity in notation, without loss of generality we can and will set
\mu=1
in the sequel. Moreover, we assume that the driving force
\mathbf{f}
is gravity, meaning
\mathbf{f}=-g\mathbf{e}_{d}=-g\nabla[x\mapsto x_{d}]
for the gravitational constant
g>0
.

6.1
Starovoitov’s result

In
[
45
]
, the author considered the incompressible Navier-Stokes equations with a solid of class
C^{1,\alpha}
. The aim of this section is to prove his main result on (no-)collision:

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
d\in\{2,3\}
, be a compact domain of class
C^{1,\alpha}
,
\alpha\in[0,1]
. If, for some
p\in[1,\infty]
, we have
\mathbf{u}\in L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))\cap L^{2}(0,T;W^{1,p}(\Omega))
, then the distance function
h(t)=\dist(\partial\mathcal{S}(t),\partial\Omega)
satisfies

\displaystyle|\dot{h}(t)|\leq Ch^{\beta}(t)\|\mathbf{u}(t)\|_{W^{1,p}(\Omega)}

(6.3)

with
\beta=2-\frac{1}{1+\alpha}\big(1+\frac{d-1}{p}\big)-\frac{1}{p}=\frac{1+2\alpha}{p(1+\alpha)}(p-\frac{d+\alpha}{1+2\alpha})
.

Further, if additionally
\mathbf{u}\in L^{q}(0,T;W^{1,p}(\Omega))
for some
q\in[1,\infty]
, then:

a)

If
h(T_{\ast})=0
for some
T_{\ast}\in[0,T]
and
\beta<1
, that is,
\alpha(p-1)<d
, then collision occurs with rate
\lim_{t\to T_{\ast}}h(t)|t-T_{\ast}|^{-\eta}=0
for
\eta=\frac{q-1}{q}\frac{1}{1-\beta}=\frac{q-1}{q}\frac{(1+\alpha)p}{d-\alpha(p-1)}
.

b)

If
h(T_{\ast})>0
for some
T_{\ast}\in[0,T]
and
\beta\geq 1
, that is,
\alpha(p-1)\geq d
, then
h(t)>0
for all
t\in[0,T]
.

We shall compare the above result, especially the value of
\beta
, with the bounds of the test function
\mathbf{w}_{h}
found in Lemma
3.1
(see also Remark
3.2
). Note also that in three dimensions and for
p=2
, the value for
\beta
in (
6.3
) is
\beta=\frac{3\alpha-1}{2(1+\alpha)}
, and also notice that
\beta<0
precisely if
\alpha
is less than our favourite fraction
\alpha<\frac{1}{3}
. We will see this number again later on. Let us moreover emphasize that this theorem does
not
prove that collision happens. It rather states that
if
at some time
T_{\ast}
the distance
h
vanishes, then it does with zero speed and a certain rate determined by
\eta
. Indeed, as we will see in the next subsections, in dimension two the value for
\alpha
to let collision happen is quite restricted, whereas in the three-dimensional case and
p=2
we can allow for
all
values
\alpha\in[0,1)
. Again,
\alpha=1
is excluded since this corresponds to a ball-shaped object.

On the other hand, Starovoitov also showed that collision can happen by giving a precise example: he constructed a solution, the “remainder” of which when inserting in the Navier-Stokes equations gives an additional “singular–in–
W^{-1,2}
” force, showing that there exists a force such that the solid touches the container’s bottom; we will come back to this in the next chapter. At this point, let us put a quote made by P. Constantin: “
Be careful with statements that say ‘there exists a force’. Every function is a solution to Navier-Stokes: there exists a force.
’’
1
1

1

P. Constantin at
Shocking Developments: New Directions in Compressible and Incompressible Flows: A Conference in Honor of Alexis Vasseur’s 50th Birthday
, Leipzig, 26.06.–30.06.2023

Proof of Theorem
6.1
.

We just consider the case
d=3
. The case
d=2
follows the same lines with obvious changes in the notations/definitions. To begin, since
\mathcal{S}
is rigid, we have
\mathbf{u}|_{\partial\mathcal{S}}=\dot{\mathbf{G}}(t)+\omega(t)\times(x-\mathbf{G}(t))
. Then,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}}|\mathbf{u}|^{2}\ \mathrm{d}x=\int_{\mathcal{S}}|\dot{\mathbf{G}}+\omega\times(x-\mathbf{G})|^{2}\ \mathrm{d}x=\int_{\mathcal{S}}|\dot{\mathbf{G}}|^{2}+|\omega\times(x-\mathbf{G})|^{2}\ \mathrm{d}x,

where we used that
\int_{\mathcal{S}}\dot{\mathbf{G}}\cdot[\omega\times(x-\mathbf{G})]\ \mathrm{d}x=\dot{\mathbf{G}}\cdot[\omega\times\int_{\mathcal{S}}x-\mathbf{G}\ \mathrm{d}x]=0
by the definition of
\mathbf{G}
as the center of mass. This identity yields immediately a bound for
\dot{\mathbf{G}}
. To get a bound on
\omega
itself, we take a small ball
B=B(\mathbf{G})\subset\mathcal{S}
with midpoint
\mathbf{G}
and write

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}}|\omega\times(x-\mathbf{G})|^{2}\ \mathrm{d}x\geq\int_{B}|\omega\times(x-\mathbf{G})|^{2}\ \mathrm{d}x=\int_{B(0)}|\omega\times x|^{2}\ \mathrm{d}x.

Note that the last integral now is rotationally symmetric
for any rotation
. Thus, we choose to rotate the coordinate system such that
\omega=|\omega|\mathbf{e}_{1}
, yielding

\displaystyle\int_{B(0)}|\omega\times x|^{2}\ \mathrm{d}x=|\omega|^{2}\int_{B(0)}|\mathbf{e}_{1}\times x|^{2}\ \mathrm{d}x\gtrsim|\omega|^{2}.

This finally enables us to conclude that

\displaystyle|\dot{\mathbf{G}}|^{2}+|\omega|^{2}\lesssim\int_{\mathcal{S}}|\mathbf{u}|^{2}\ \mathrm{d}x

and since
\mathbf{u}\in L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))
, we find that both
\dot{\mathbf{G}}
and
\omega
are bounded in
L^{\infty}(0,T)
, telling that the motion of
\mathcal{S}
and in particular
h
is Lipschitz.

Having this in mind, let
P(t)\in\partial\mathcal{S}(t)
and
Q\in\partial\Omega
be two points realizing the distance
h(t)=|P(t)-Q|
(note that
Q
might also depend on time in general, however, we can choose our coordinate system in such a way that
Q
is fixed and even
Q=0
). Denote
x^{\prime}=(x_{1},x_{2})
. Since
\partial\mathcal{S}
and
\partial\Omega
are of class
C^{1,\alpha}
, there are constants
k,r>0
such that the “parabolic shells”

\displaystyle\pi^{+}(t)

\displaystyle:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:|x^{\prime}|<r,\ h(t)+k|x^{\prime}|^{1+\alpha}<x_{3}<h(t)+kr^{1+\alpha}\}\subset\mathcal{S}(t),

\displaystyle\pi^{-}(t)

\displaystyle:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:|x^{\prime}|<r,\ -kr^{1+\alpha}<x_{3}<-k|x^{\prime}|^{1+\alpha}\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\Omega.

Set further

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{r}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:|x^{\prime}|<r,\ -k|x^{\prime}|^{1+\alpha}<x_{3}<h(t)+k|x^{\prime}|^{1+\alpha}\}.

Note that we can split
\partial\mathcal{G}_{r}=\Gamma_{r}^{+}\cup\Gamma_{r}^{-}\cup\Gamma_{r}^{0}
, where
\Gamma_{r}^{\pm}=\partial\mathcal{G}_{r}\cap\partial\pi^{\pm}
, and
\Gamma_{r}^{0}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:|x^{\prime}|=r,\ -kr^{1+\alpha}\leq x_{3}\leq kr^{1+\alpha}\}
.

Extending the velocity by
\mathbf{u}=0
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\Omega
, we see that by
\div\mathbf{u}=0

\displaystyle\int_{\partial\mathcal{G}_{r}}\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{n}\ \mathrm{d}\sigma=\int_{\mathcal{G}_{r}}\div\mathbf{u}\ \mathrm{d}x=0=\int_{\Gamma_{r}^{-}}\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{n}\ \mathrm{d}\sigma.

Furthermore,

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma_{r}^{+}}\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{n}\ \mathrm{d}\sigma=\int_{\partial\pi^{+}(t)}\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{n}\ \mathrm{d}\sigma-\int_{A_{k}}\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{n}\ \mathrm{d}\sigma,

where
A_{k}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:|x^{\prime}|\leq r,\ x_{3}=h(t)+kr^{1+\alpha}\}
is the “upper part” of
\pi^{+}(t)
. Since
\mathbf{u}=\dot{\mathbf{G}}+\omega\times(x-\mathbf{G})
is rigid on
\mathcal{S}
, by Gauß’ theorem, the integral over
\partial\pi^{+}(t)
vanishes. Moreover, by the same token,
\mathbf{u}
is the same at every hight, meaning we can change integration over
A_{k}
by integration over
A_{k=0}
. Then, denoting
\mathbf{u}_{P}
and
\mathbf{n}_{P}
the velocity and the outward normal at the point
P\in\partial\mathcal{S}
, respectively, we have
\mathbf{u}|_{A_{k=0}}=\mathbf{u}_{P}
and
\mathbf{n}|_{A_{k=0}}=-\mathbf{n}_{P}
, leading to

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma_{r}^{+}}\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{n}\ \mathrm{d}\sigma=\int_{\{|x^{\prime}|\leq r,\ x_{3}=h(t)\}}\mathbf{u}_{P}\cdot\mathbf{n}_{P}\ \mathrm{d}\sigma=\pi r^{2}(\mathbf{u}_{P}\cdot\mathbf{n}_{P}).

Altogether, the above calculations, together with
\Gamma_{r}^{+}=\partial\mathcal{G}_{r}\setminus(\Gamma_{r}^{0}\cup\Gamma_{r}^{-})
and
|\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{n}|\leq|\mathbf{u}|
, imply

\displaystyle\pi r^{2}|\mathbf{u}_{P}\cdot\mathbf{n}_{P}|\leq\int_{\Gamma_{r}^{0}}|\mathbf{u}|\ \mathrm{d}\sigma.

Integrating this last inequality with respect to
r
from 0 to some
\rho\in(0,r)
, we find

\displaystyle\frac{\pi}{3}\rho^{3}|\mathbf{u}_{P}\cdot\mathbf{n}_{P}|\leq\int_{\mathcal{G}_{\rho}}|\mathbf{u}|\ \mathrm{d}x.

We further estimate by the use of the Poincaré inequality (
1.2
)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{G}_{\rho}}|\mathbf{u}|\ \mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq|\mathcal{G}_{\rho}|^{1-\frac{1}{p}}\|\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{p}(\mathcal{G}_{\rho})}\lesssim(h+2k\rho^{1+\alpha})|\mathcal{G}_{\rho}|^{1-\frac{1}{p}}\|\nabla\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{p}(\mathcal{G}_{\rho})}

\displaystyle\leq(h+2k\rho^{1+\alpha})|\mathcal{G}_{\rho}|^{1-\frac{1}{p}}\|\nabla\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}.

Seeing that
|\mathcal{G}_{\rho}|\lesssim\rho^{2}(h+2k\rho^{1+\alpha})
yields

\displaystyle|\mathbf{u}_{P}\cdot\mathbf{n}_{P}|\lesssim\rho^{-1-\frac{2}{p}}(h+2k\rho^{1+\alpha})^{2-\frac{1}{p}}\|\mathbf{u}\|_{W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)}.

We may now take
\rho=ch^{1/(1+\alpha)}
, where
c>0
is such that
\rho\in(0,r)
; in particular, the choice
c=r\diam(\Omega)^{-\frac{1}{1+\alpha}}
is allowed. We finally get

\displaystyle|\mathbf{u}_{P}\cdot\mathbf{n}_{P}|\lesssim h^{\beta}\|\mathbf{u}\|_{W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)}\ \text{ with }\ \beta=2-\frac{1}{1+\alpha}\left(1+\frac{2}{p}\right)-\frac{1}{p}.

It remains to show that
|\mathbf{u}_{P}\cdot\mathbf{n}_{P}|
is an upper bound for the time derivative
\dot{h}
. We will indeed show that
\dot{h}=-\mathbf{u}_{P}\cdot\mathbf{n}_{P}
, meaning the speed of the distance change is decreasing and happens in the normal direction of the solid’s movement towards the container’s bottom, which one might intuitively expect. To this end, define a function
\mathfrak{y}:[0,T]\times\partial\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
such that
y=x+\mathfrak{y}(t,x)\mathbf{\nu}_{x}\in\partial\mathcal{S}(t)
, where
\mathbf{\nu}_{x}
is the internal normal on
\partial\Omega
at
x\in\partial\Omega
. Note that
\mathfrak{y}
is well defined on some neighborhood
U\subset\partial\Omega
of
Q
by regularity of
\partial\mathcal{S}
and
\partial\Omega
. Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}_{y}=\dot{y}=\frac{\partial y}{\partial t}=\dot{\mathfrak{y}}\nu_{x}\ \Rightarrow\ \dot{\mathfrak{y}}=(\mathbf{u}_{y}\cdot\mathbf{n}_{y})(\nu_{x}\cdot\mathbf{n}_{y})^{-1}

for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
and any
x\in U
, where
\mathbf{n}_{y}
is the outward normal at
y=x+\mathfrak{y}(t,x)\nu_{x}\in\partial\mathcal{S}
. We see that for
x=Q
(and so
y=P
), we get
\nu_{x}\cdot\mathbf{n}_{y}=-1
and hence

\displaystyle\dot{\mathfrak{y}}(t,Q)=-\mathbf{u}_{P}\cdot\mathbf{n}_{P}.

Seeing finally that by definition
h(t)=\mathfrak{y}(t,Q)
, we obtain (
6.3
).

To show the last assertions, we integrate (
6.3
) from
s
to
t
to obtain

\displaystyle H_{\beta}(s)-C\bigg|\int_{s}^{t}\|\mathbf{u}(\tau)\|_{W^{1,p}(\Omega)}\ \mathrm{d}\tau\bigg|\leq H_{\beta}(t)\leq H_{\beta}(s)+C\bigg|\int_{s}^{t}\|\mathbf{u}(\tau)\|_{W^{1,p}(\Omega)}\ \mathrm{d}\tau\bigg|,

(6.4)

where

\displaystyle H_{\beta}=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{1-\beta}h^{1-\beta}&\text{if }\beta\neq 1,\\
\log h&\text{if }\beta=1.\end{cases}

Assertion a) now follows by setting
s=T_{\ast}
for which
H_{\beta}(T_{\ast})=\frac{1}{1-\beta}h^{1-\beta}(T_{\ast})=0
, and applying Hölder’s inequality (
1.7
), together with
\|\mathbf{u}(\tau)\|_{W^{1,p}(\Omega)}\in L^{q}(0,T)
, to get (for
T_{\ast}<t
)

\displaystyle h^{1-\beta}(t)\leq C\|\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{q}(T_{\ast},t;W^{1,p}(\Omega))}|t-T_{\ast}|^{1-\frac{1}{q}}.

Seeing that
\|\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{q}(T_{\ast},t;W^{1,p}(\Omega))}\to 0
as
t\to T_{\ast}
, we have
\lim_{t\to T_{\ast}}h(t)|t-T_{\ast}|^{-\eta}=0
as wished. The case
T_{\ast}>t
follows the same lines.

The second statement b) follows similarly. Indeed, if
\beta=1
, then (
6.4
) forces

\displaystyle h(t)\geq h(T_{\ast})\exp\bigg(-C\bigg|\int_{T_{\ast}}^{t}\|\mathbf{u}(\tau)\|_{W^{1,p}(\Omega)}\ \mathrm{d}\tau\bigg|\bigg)

for all
t\in[0,T]
and hence
h(t)>0
for any
t\in[0,T]
. For the case
\beta>1
, we calculate

\displaystyle h^{1-\beta}(t)\leq h^{1-\beta}(T_{\ast})+C\|\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{q}(0,T;W^{1,p}(\Omega))}|t-T_{\ast}|^{1-\frac{1}{q}}.

Since the right-hand side of this inequality is finite for any
t\in[0,T]
, this together with
\beta>1
means that
h
can never vanish, thus showing the result.
∎

6.2
Finer estimates and a wider class of obstacles

After determining the rate of collision, if it happens, let us answer the question
whether
there is a configuration such that the solid collides with its container. The (no-)collision results we present here were previously obtained for the linear Stokes equations in
[
23
]
and for the case
d=3
, and in
[
22
]
for
d=2
(see also
[
30
]
for the case of a ball-shaped obstacle). Therefore, we shall also concentrate on this case here.

6.2.1
Preliminaries

All the following techniques are similar to the proof of Theorem
2.2
, in the sense that we have to construct an appropriate test function
\mathbf{w}_{h}
. For this reason, we give here some new estimates, and apply them later in the special cases.

Indeed, the same arguments/heuristics made in Section
3.1
yield that an appropriate test function for
d=2
is given by
\mathbf{w}_{h}=\nabla^{\perp}\phi_{h}
, where
\phi_{h}
in the region beneath
\mathcal{S}
is now given by

\displaystyle\phi_{h}(x_{1},x_{2})

\displaystyle=x_{1}\Phi\bigg(\frac{x_{2}}{\psi_{h}(x_{1})}\bigg),\quad\Phi(t)=t^{2}(3-2t),

\displaystyle\psi_{h}(x_{1})

\displaystyle=h+|x_{1}|^{1+\alpha}\ \text{ or }\ \psi_{h}(x_{1})=1+h-\sqrt{1-x_{1}^{2}}.

Note especially that in 2D, we have
r=|x_{1}|
, so
\phi_{h}
has the same structure as in 3D. Extending
\phi_{h}
in a proper way to the whole of
\Omega
similar as before, we have a test function
\mathbf{w}_{h}
as wished. More precisely, with the notations as in (
3.3
) (and obvious adaptations for
d=2
), we set

\displaystyle\phi_{h}(x_{1},x_{2})=x_{1}\begin{cases}1&\text{on }\mathcal{S}_{h},\\
(1-\chi(x_{1},x_{2}))\eta(x_{1},x_{2}-h+h(0))+\chi(x_{1},x_{2})\Phi\left(\frac{x_{2}}{\psi_{h}(x_{1})}\right)&\text{on }\Omega\setminus\mathcal{S}_{h},\end{cases}

(6.5)

and
\mathbf{w}_{h}=\nabla^{\perp}\phi_{h}
.

To start, we generalize estimate (
3.8
) to all values of
(\alpha,q,s)\in(0,\infty)^{3}
:

Lemma 6.2
.

The integral

\displaystyle\int_{-r_{0}}^{r_{0}}\frac{r^{q}}{(h+r^{1+\alpha})^{s}}\ \mathrm{d}r

behaves like

i)

h^{\frac{q+1}{1+\alpha}-s}
, if
q+1<s(1+\alpha)
;

ii)

\log h
, if
q+1=s(1+\alpha)
;

iii)

1
, if
q+1>s(1+\alpha)
.

Proof.

This is straightforward calculation and we leave the details to the reader as an exercise.
∎

To lean notation, we will make the following agreement: for
d=2
, we set
\mathbf{w}_{h}=\nabla^{\perp}\phi_{h}
, where
\phi_{h}
is as in (
6.5
). If
d=3
, we set
\mathbf{w}_{h}=\nabla\times(\phi_{h}\mathbf{e}_{\theta})
, where this time
\phi_{h}
is as in (
3.5
). With this convention, we have:

Lemma 6.3
.

For all
\alpha\in[0,1]
, the function
\mathbf{w}_{h}
satisfies:

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{w}_{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\lesssim 1,

\displaystyle 1\lesssim h^{\frac{3\alpha-(d-2)}{2(1+\alpha)}}\|\nabla\mathbf{w}_{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\lesssim 1,

\displaystyle\|\nabla\mathbf{w}_{h}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega\setminus\Omega_{h,r_{0}})}

\displaystyle\lesssim 1.

Moreover, for
r=|x_{1}|
if
d=2
, and
r=\sqrt{x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}}
if
d=3
,

\displaystyle\sup_{r<r_{0}}|\psi_{h}(r)|^{\frac{3}{2}}\left(\int_{0}^{\psi_{h}(r)}|\nabla\mathbf{w}_{h}(r,x_{d})|^{2}\ \mathrm{d}x_{d}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 1,

\displaystyle\int_{-r_{0}}^{r_{0}}\int_{0}^{\psi_{h}(r)}\psi_{h}^{2}(r)|\partial_{h}\mathbf{w}_{h}|^{2}\ \mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\lesssim 1.

Proof.

The proof is follows the same lines as the one for Lemma
3.1
and using Lemma
6.2
, once seen that for the ball case
\frac{1}{2}r^{2}\leq 1-\sqrt{1-r^{2}}\leq r^{2}
for
r\leq 1
, that
\ \mathrm{d}x=\ \mathrm{d}x_{1}\ \mathrm{d}x_{2}
if
d=2
, and
\ \mathrm{d}x=r\ \mathrm{d}r\ \mathrm{d}\theta\ \mathrm{d}x_{3}
if
d=3
. In the latter case, we even can replace the integral over
\theta
by the supremum over
\theta\in(0,2\pi)
.
∎

Remark 6.4
.

Compared to the calculations done in Remark
3.4
, Lemma
6.3
gives the correct behavior of
\nabla\mathbf{w}_{h}
in
L^{p}(\Omega)
for
p=2
instead of
1<p<2
.

Let
T_{\ast}\in(0,\infty]
be the maximal existence time of the solution
(\rho,\mathbf{u})
to (
6.1
)–(
6.2
). To show the (no-)collision result, we would like to test the momentum equation by
\mathbf{w}_{h}
and integrate by parts. As already noticed, the drag
\mathcal{D}_{h}
is the main driving force to establish collision, or to prevent from it. In particular, we would need to shift the Laplace from the fluid’s velocity
\mathbf{u}
to the test function
\mathbf{w}_{h}
; however, this causes quite strong singularities. To catch them, we will introduce a pressure
q_{h}
, which we then can insert in the weak formulation of the momentum equation without changes. Note that here the incompressibility condition of the fluid comes into play; without it, we would get an additional term
\int_{\Omega}q_{h}\div\mathbf{u}\ \mathrm{d}x
, which we do not know how to handle.

Lemma 6.5
.

There exists
q_{h}\in C^{\infty}(0,T_{\ast};C(\overline{\Omega}))
such that for any
\varphi\in W_{0}^{1,2}(\Omega)
with
\varphi|_{\partial\mathcal{S}}=\mathbf{e}_{d}
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{F}(t)}|(\Delta\mathbf{w}_{h}-\nabla q_{h})\cdot\varphi|\ \mathrm{d}x\lesssim\|\varphi\|_{W_{0}^{1,2}(\Omega)}.

(6.6)

Proof.

The case
d=2
.
Recalling
\mathbf{w}_{h}=\nabla^{\perp}\phi_{h}
, we set

\displaystyle q_{h}=\partial_{12}^{2}\phi_{h}-\int_{0}^{x_{1}}\partial_{222}^{3}\phi_{h}(t,x_{2})\ \mathrm{d}t.

Although this definition seems quite artificial at first glance, it is easily obtained by computing
\Delta\mathbf{w}_{h}
and “removing” all terms that contain a singular part in
h
; particularly, we want to have as less derivatives in
x_{2}
-direction as possible. Indeed, we calculate

\displaystyle(-\Delta\mathbf{w}_{h})_{1}

\displaystyle=\Delta\partial_{2}\phi_{h}=\partial_{112}^{3}\phi_{h}+\partial_{222}^{3}\phi_{h},

\displaystyle(\Delta\mathbf{w}_{h})_{2}

\displaystyle=\Delta\partial_{1}\phi_{h}=\partial_{111}^{3}\phi_{h}+\partial_{122}^{3}\phi_{h}.

Since
\phi_{h}
and in turn
q_{h}
are smooth outside
\Omega_{h,r_{0}}
, we just have to focus on this inner part. Note that inside
\Omega_{h,r_{0}}
, we have

\displaystyle\phi_{h}(x_{1},x_{2})=x_{1}\Phi\left(\frac{x_{2}}{\psi_{h}(x_{1})}\right),\ \Phi(t)=t^{2}(3-2t).

Hence,

\displaystyle q_{h}=\partial_{12}^{2}\phi_{h}+12\int_{0}^{x_{1}}\frac{t}{\psi_{h}(t)^{3}}\ \mathrm{d}t\ \text{in}\ \Omega_{h,r_{0}},

and thus
\partial_{1}q_{h}=\partial_{112}^{3}\phi_{h}-\partial_{222}^{3}\phi_{h}
and
\partial_{2}q_{h}=\partial_{122}^{3}\phi_{h}
, which precisely cancel the terms of
x_{2}
-order two and three occurring in
(\Delta\mathbf{w}_{h})_{1}
and
(\Delta\mathbf{w}_{h})_{2}
. Thus, we see

\displaystyle\Delta\mathbf{w}_{h}-\nabla q_{h}=\begin{pmatrix}-2\partial_{112}^{3}\phi_{h}\\
\partial_{111}^{3}\phi_{h}\end{pmatrix}\ \text{in}\ \Omega_{h,r_{0}}.

From the definition of
\mathbf{w}_{h}
, we have
\mathbf{w}_{h}\in C^{\infty}(\Omega\setminus\Omega_{h,r_{0}})
. Moreover, we see that the most singular term in
\Delta\mathbf{w}_{h}-\nabla q_{h}
is
\partial_{112}^{3}\phi_{h}
; indeed, we have for
\psi_{h}(x_{1})=h+|x_{1}|^{1+\alpha}

\displaystyle|\partial_{112}^{3}\phi_{h}|\lesssim x_{2}\frac{\psi_{h}^{\prime}}{\psi_{h}^{3}}+x_{2}^{2}\frac{\psi_{h}^{\prime}}{\psi_{h}^{4}},

\displaystyle|\partial_{111}^{3}\phi_{h}|\lesssim x_{2}^{2}\frac{\psi_{h}^{\prime\prime}}{\psi_{h}^{3}}+x_{2}^{3}\frac{\psi_{h}^{\prime\prime}}{\psi_{h}^{4}}

(6.7)

and thus, by
\psi_{h}^{\prime}(x_{1})\sim x_{1}^{\alpha}
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{\Omega_{h,r_{0}}}|\partial_{112}^{3}\phi_{h}|^{p}\ \mathrm{d}x&\lesssim\int_{-r_{0}}^{r_{0}}\int_{0}^{\psi_{h}(x_{1})}x_{2}^{p}\frac{|\psi_{h}^{\prime}|^{p}}{\psi_{h}^{3p}}+x_{2}^{2p}\frac{|\psi_{h}^{\prime}|^{p}}{\psi_{h}^{4p}}\ \mathrm{d}x_{2}\ \mathrm{d}x_{1}\lesssim\int_{0}^{r_{0}}\frac{x_{1}^{\alpha p}}{(h+x_{1}^{1+\alpha})^{2p-1}},\\
\int_{\Omega_{h,r_{0}}}|\partial_{111}^{3}\phi_{h}|^{p}\ \mathrm{d}x&\lesssim\int_{-r_{0}}^{r_{0}}\int_{0}^{\psi_{h}(x_{1})}x_{2}^{2p}\frac{|\psi_{h}^{\prime\prime}|^{p}}{\psi_{h}^{3p}}+x_{2}^{3p}\frac{|\psi_{h}^{\prime\prime}|^{p}}{\psi_{h}^{4p}}\ \mathrm{d}x_{2}\ \mathrm{d}x_{1}\lesssim\int_{0}^{r_{0}}\frac{x_{1}^{(\alpha-1)p}}{(h+x_{1}^{1+\alpha})^{p-1}}.\end{split}

(6.8)

In the case
\psi_{h}(x_{1})=1+h-\sqrt{1-x_{1}^{2}}
, we first check that for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\displaystyle|\psi_{h}^{(2k+1)}(x_{1})|\lesssim|x_{1}|,\qquad|\psi_{h}^{(2k)}(x_{1})|\lesssim 1\qquad\forall|x_{1}|\leq r_{0}<1.

Consequently, the estimates (
6.7
) have to be replaced by

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} |\partial_{112}^{3}\phi_{h}|&\lesssim x_{2}\bigg(\frac{|x_{1}|}{\psi_{h}^{3}}+\frac{|x_{1}|^{3}}{\psi_{h}^{4}}\bigg)+x_{2}^{2}\bigg(\frac{|x_{1}|}{\psi_{h}^{4}}+\frac{|x_{1}|^{3}}{\psi_{h}^{5}}\bigg)\\
|\partial_{111}^{3}\phi_{h}|&\lesssim x_{2}^{2}\bigg(\frac{1}{\psi^{3}}+\frac{x_{1}^{2}}{\psi^{4}}+\frac{x_{1}^{4}}{\psi^{5}}\bigg)+x_{2}^{3}\bigg(\frac{1}{\psi^{4}}+\frac{x_{1}^{2}}{\psi^{5}}+\frac{x_{1}^{4}}{\psi^{6}}\bigg).\end{aligned}

These bounds precisely correspond to
\alpha=1
, hence we do not have to distinguish between
\alpha=1
and
\alpha<1
in the sequel.

For the exponents occurring in (
6.8
), we have

\displaystyle\alpha p+1

\displaystyle<(2p-1)(1+\alpha)\Longleftrightarrow p>1,

\displaystyle(\alpha-1)p+1

\displaystyle>(p-1)(1+\alpha)\Longleftrightarrow p<\frac{2+\alpha}{2}.

Hence, using Lemma
6.2
, we see that the second integral in (
6.8
) is uniformly bounded in
h
for all
p<(2+\alpha)/2
, whereas the first one is always unbounded. Nonetheless, it is now obvious that
\Delta\mathbf{w}_{h}-\nabla q_{h}\in L^{p}(\Omega)
for any
p>1
(
without
a uniform bound in
h
), thus for
h>0
the integral in (
6.6
) is well defined since by Sobolev embedding (
1.4
),
\varphi\in W_{0}^{1,2}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L^{q}(\Omega)
for any
1\leq q<\infty
.

To prove the desired inequality (
6.6
), by
\mathbf{w}_{h},q_{h}\in C^{\infty}(\Omega\setminus\Omega_{h,r_{0}})
, we might without loss of generality assume that
\supp\varphi\subset\overline{\Omega}_{h,r_{0}}
such that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{F}(t)}(\Delta\mathbf{w}_{h}-\nabla q_{h})\cdot\varphi\ \mathrm{d}x=\int_{\Omega_{h,r_{0}}}(\Delta\mathbf{w}_{h}-\nabla q_{h})\cdot\varphi\ \mathrm{d}x.

Since
\varphi|_{\partial\mathcal{S}_{h}}=(0,1)^{T}
, we integrate by parts to obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{h,r_{0}}}(\Delta\mathbf{w}_{h}-\nabla q_{h})\cdot\varphi\ \mathrm{d}x=-\int_{\partial\mathcal{S}_{h}\cap\partial\Omega_{h,r_{0}}}\partial_{11}^{2}\phi_{h}\mathbf{n}_{1}\ \mathrm{d}\sigma-\int_{\Omega_{h,r_{0}}}\partial_{11}^{2}\phi_{h}(2\partial_{2}\varphi_{1}-\partial_{1}\varphi_{2})\ \mathrm{d}x,

where we calculate

\displaystyle\partial_{11}^{2}\phi_{h}=-\Phi^{\prime\prime}\left(\frac{x_{2}}{\psi_{h}}\right)\frac{x_{1}x_{2}^{2}(\psi_{h}^{\prime})^{2}}{\psi_{h}^{4}}-\Phi^{\prime}\left(\frac{x_{2}}{\psi_{h}}\right)\left(2\frac{x_{2}\psi_{h}^{\prime}}{\psi_{h}^{2}}+\frac{x_{1}x_{2}\psi_{h}^{\prime\prime}}{\psi_{h}^{2}}-2\frac{x_{1}x_{2}(\psi_{h}^{\prime})^{2}}{\psi_{h}^{3}}\right).

By Lemma
6.2
, we can check that
\partial_{11}^{2}\phi_{h}
is bounded in
L^{2}(\Omega_{h,r_{0}})
uniformly in
h
. Furthermore, by definition of
\Phi
, we have
\Phi^{\prime}(1)=0
and
\Phi^{\prime\prime}(1)=-6
such that, together with
x_{2}=\psi_{h}
on
\partial\mathcal{S}_{h}
,

\displaystyle\partial_{11}^{2}\phi_{h}=\frac{6x_{1}x_{2}^{2}(\psi_{h}^{\prime})^{2}}{\psi_{h}^{2}}=\frac{6x_{1}(\psi_{h}^{\prime})^{2}}{\psi_{h}^{2}}.

Lastly, as
\partial\mathcal{S}_{h}\cap\partial\Omega_{h,r_{0}}
is parametrized by the curve
\mathord{\text{\char 21\relax}}:(-r_{0},r_{0})\ni x_{1}\mapsto(x_{1},\psi_{h}(x_{1}))^{T}
, we find

\displaystyle\mathbf{n}=\frac{\mathord{\text{\char 21\relax}}^{\prime}(x_{1})^{\perp}}{|\mathord{\text{\char 21\relax}}^{\prime}(x_{1})|}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+(\psi_{h}^{\prime})^{2}}}(-\psi_{h}^{\prime},1)^{T},

hence

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\partial\mathcal{S}_{h}}\partial_{11}^{2}\phi_{h}\mathbf{n}_{1}\ \mathrm{d}\sigma\right|\leq\int_{-r_{0}}^{r_{0}}\left|\frac{6x_{1}(\psi_{h}^{\prime}(x_{1}))^{2}}{(\psi_{h}(x_{1}))^{2}}\ \frac{\psi_{h}^{\prime}(x_{1})}{\sqrt{1+(\psi_{h}^{\prime}(x_{1}))^{2}}}\right|\ \mathrm{d}x_{1}.

Note that the last integral hides a factor
\|\varphi_{2}\|_{L^{\infty}(\partial\Omega_{h,r_{0}})}
by
\varphi|_{\partial\mathcal{S}_{h}}=(0,1)^{T}
and thus
1=\|\varphi_{2}\|_{L^{\infty}(\partial\Omega_{h,r_{0}})}
. Moreover, by standard trace inequality,
\|\varphi_{2}\|_{L^{\infty}(\partial\Omega_{h,r_{0}})}\lesssim\|\varphi\|_{W_{0}^{1,2}(\Omega)}
. Hence, for
\psi_{h}(x_{1})=h+|x_{1}|^{1+\alpha}
, we have
\psi_{h}^{\prime}(x_{1})\sim x_{1}^{\alpha}
and this integral may be estimated again uniformly in
h
with the help of Lemma
6.2
. The same conclusion holds for
\psi_{h}(x_{1})=1+h-\sqrt{1-x_{1}^{2}}
as
\psi_{h}(x_{1})\sim x_{1}^{2}
for
|x_{1}|\leq r_{0}<1
. Consequently, we arrive at

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\Omega_{h,r_{0}}}(\Delta\mathbf{w}_{h}-\nabla q_{h})\cdot\varphi\ \mathrm{d}x\right|\lesssim\|\varphi_{2}\|_{L^{\infty}(\partial\Omega_{h,r_{0}})}+\|\nabla\varphi\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{h,r_{0}})}\lesssim\|\varphi\|_{W_{0}^{1,2}(\Omega)}.

The case
d=3
.
Very similar, we proceed for the three-dimensional case. Recall that
\mathbf{w}_{h}=\nabla\times(\phi_{h}\mathbf{e}_{\theta})
. Calculating as before
\Delta\mathbf{w}_{h}
, where in 3D and cylindrical coordinates

\displaystyle\nabla=\mathbf{e}_{r}\partial_{r}+r^{-1}\mathbf{e}_{\theta}\partial_{\theta}+\mathbf{e}_{3}\partial_{3},

\displaystyle\Delta=r^{-1}\partial_{r}(r\partial_{r})+r^{-2}\partial_{\theta\theta}^{2}+\partial_{33}^{2},

we find that we may simply choose
q_{h}
as

\displaystyle q_{h}(r,x_{3})=\partial_{r3}^{2}\phi_{h}(r,x_{3})-\int_{0}^{r}\partial_{333}^{3}\phi_{h}(t,x_{3})\ \mathrm{d}t.

Similar calculations as for the 2D case show the result. We leave the details to the reader (see, for instance,
[
31
, Section 3.4]
).
∎

Before coming to the proof of Theorem
6.7
, we need the following proposition to control the drag force and the remainder arising from the weak formulation of the momentum equation.

Proposition 6.6
.

Let
\mathbf{w}_{h},q_{h}
be as above and
(\rho,\mathbf{u})
be the solution to equations (
6.1
)–(
6.2
). Define further

\displaystyle n(h)

\displaystyle:=\int_{\partial\mathcal{F}_{h}}(\nabla\mathbf{w}_{h}-q_{h}\mathbb{I})\mathbf{n}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{d}\ \mathrm{d}\sigma,

\displaystyle R(t)

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\rho\mathbf{u}\cdot\partial_{t}\mathbf{w}_{h}+\rho\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{u}:\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{w}_{h})\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\Omega}\mathbf{m}_{0}\cdot\mathbf{w}_{h(0)}\ \mathrm{d}x-\int_{\Omega}\rho(t)\mathbf{u}(t)\cdot\mathbf{w}_{h(t)}\ \mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathcal{F}(s)}(\Delta\mathbf{w}_{h(s)}-\nabla q_{h(s)})\cdot\mathbf{u}(s)\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}s.

Then, for
h>0
small enough, all
\alpha\in[0,1]
, and all
0\leq t<T_{\ast}
,

\displaystyle 1\lesssim h^{\beta}n(h)

\displaystyle\lesssim 1,\quad\beta=\frac{3\alpha-(d-2)}{1+\alpha},

\displaystyle|R(t)|

\displaystyle\lesssim 1+\sqrt{t}.

Proof.

We start with the estimate for
n(h)
. Using
\div\mathbf{w}_{h}=0
, integration by parts gives

\displaystyle n(h)=2\int_{\Omega}|\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{w}_{h})|^{2}\ \mathrm{d}x+\int_{\mathcal{F}_{h}}(\Delta\mathbf{w}_{h}-\nabla q_{h})\cdot\mathbf{w}_{h}\ \mathrm{d}x.

By Lemma
6.3
, we have
\|\nabla\mathbf{w}_{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\sim h^{(d-2-3\alpha)/(1+\alpha)}
. Combining this with Lemma
6.5
yields

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{\mathcal{F}_{h}}(\Delta\mathbf{w}_{h}-\nabla q_{h})\cdot\mathbf{w}_{h}\ \mathrm{d}x\bigg|\lesssim\|\nabla\mathbf{w}_{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\lesssim 1+\delta\|\nabla\mathbf{w}_{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\lesssim 1+\delta h^{\frac{d-2-3\alpha}{1+\alpha}}

for
\delta>0
small enough (see (
1.6
)). In particular,

\displaystyle n(h)\lesssim\|\nabla\mathbf{w}_{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}+\bigg|\int_{\mathcal{F}_{h}}(\Delta\mathbf{w}_{h}-\nabla q_{h})\cdot\mathbf{w}_{h}\ \mathrm{d}x\bigg|\lesssim 1+h^{\frac{d-2-3\alpha}{1+\alpha}}\lesssim h^{-\beta}

as wished. Additionally, for
h<1
,

\displaystyle n(h)\gtrsim\|\nabla\mathbf{w}_{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}-\bigg|\int_{\mathcal{F}_{h}}(\Delta\mathbf{w}_{h}-\nabla q_{h})\cdot\mathbf{w}_{h}\ \mathrm{d}x\bigg|\gtrsim(1-\delta)h^{\frac{d-2-3\alpha}{1+\alpha}}-1\gtrsim h^{\frac{d-2-3\alpha}{1+\alpha}}.

To estimate
R(t)
, we control each term separately. Recalling again Lemma
6.5
, integrating this inequality with respect to
t
, and using Hölder’s inequality (
1.7
), we get

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\bigg|\int_{\mathcal{F}(s)}(\Delta\mathbf{w}_{h(s)}-\nabla q_{h(s)})\cdot\mathbf{u}(s)\ \mathrm{d}x\bigg|\ \mathrm{d}s\lesssim\|\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{2}(0,t;W^{1,2}(\Omega))}\sqrt{t}\leq C(\|\mathbf{u}_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)})\sqrt{t},

the last estimate coming from basic energy estimates for the velocity
\mathbf{u}
, and the fact that the gravitational force
g\mathbf{e}_{2}
is conservative (compare this with (
4.3
)); thus, the constant does not depend on the time
t
. Similarly,

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{\Omega}\mathbf{m}_{0}\cdot\mathbf{w}_{h(0)}\ \mathrm{d}x-\int_{\Omega}\rho(t)\mathbf{u}(t)\cdot\mathbf{w}_{h(t)}\ \mathrm{d}x\bigg|\lesssim\|\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,t;L^{2}(\Omega))}\|\mathbf{w}_{h(\cdot)}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,t;L^{2}(\Omega))}\leq C(\|\mathbf{u}_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}).

Before we come to the term involving
\partial_{t}\mathbf{w}_{h}
, we state the following general result: for any
(r,\mathbf{v})\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)\times W_{0}^{1,2}(\Omega)
and any
\mathbf{\phi}\in W_{0}^{1,2}(\Omega)
,

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{\Omega}r\mathbf{v}\cdot\phi\ \mathrm{d}x\bigg|\lesssim\|r\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\|\nabla\mathbf{v}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\bigg(\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\Omega\setminus\Omega_{h,r_{0}})}+\bigg(\int_{-r_{0}}^{r_{0}}\int_{0}^{\psi_{h}(x_{1})}|\psi_{h}(x_{1})|^{2}|\phi(x)|^{2}\ \mathrm{d}x\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg).

The proof of this inequality follows easily from splitting
\Omega=(\Omega\setminus\Omega_{h,r_{0}})\cup\Omega_{h,r_{0}}
, using Hölder’s and Poincaré’s inequality (
1.7
) and (
1.2
) for the first part, and Hardy’s inequality (
1.3
) for the second. We leave the details to the reader.

Using the above inequality for
\phi=\partial_{t}\mathbf{w}_{h}=\dot{h}\partial_{h}\mathbf{w}_{h}
, combined with Lemma
6.3
and the fact that
\partial_{h}\mathbf{w}_{h}
is bounded outside the region
\Omega_{h,r_{0}}
by smoothness of
\mathbf{w}_{h}
there, we arrive at

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\rho\mathbf{u}\cdot\partial_{t}\mathbf{w}_{h}\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}t\bigg|\lesssim\|\dot{h}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T_{\ast}))}\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla\mathbf{u}(s)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\bigg(\|\partial_{h}\mathbf{w}_{h(s)}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega\setminus\Omega_{h(s),r_{0}})}

\displaystyle\quad+\bigg(\int_{-r_{0}}^{r_{0}}\int_{0}^{\psi_{h}(x_{1})}|\psi_{h}(x_{1})|^{2}|\partial_{h}\mathbf{w}_{h(s)}(x)|^{2}\ \mathrm{d}x\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg)\ \mathrm{d}s\leq C(\|\mathbf{u}_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)})\sqrt{t}.

To finally deal with the convective term, we use similarly as before a general result: for any
(r,\mathbf{v})\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)\times W_{0}^{1,2}(\Omega)
and any
\phi\in W_{0}^{1,2}(\Omega)
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\bigg|\int_{\Omega}r\mathbf{v}\otimes\mathbf{v}:\mathbb{D}(\phi)\ \mathrm{d}x\bigg|&\lesssim\|r\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\|\nabla\mathbf{v}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\bigg(\|\mathbb{D}(\phi)\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega\setminus\Omega_{h,r_{0}})}\\
&\quad+\sup_{x_{1}\in(-r_{0},r_{0})}|\psi_{h}(x_{1})|^{\frac{3}{2}}\bigg(\int_{0}^{\psi_{h}(x_{1})}|\nabla\phi(x)|^{2}\ \mathrm{d}x_{1}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg).\end{split}

(6.9)

As before, the first part of this inequality follows from Hölder’s and Poincaré’s inequality (
1.7
) and (
1.2
). The second part uses a refined Poincaré inequality

\displaystyle\bigg(\int_{0}^{l}|\mathbf{v}|^{p}\ \mathrm{d}x\bigg)^{\frac{1}{p}}\lesssim l^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{p}}\bigg(\int_{0}^{l}|\partial_{x}\mathbf{v}|^{2}\ \mathrm{d}x\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\ \forall p\geq 2\ \forall\mathbf{v}\in W^{1,2}(0,l),\ \mathbf{v}(0)=0

that can be obtained via a scaling argument. We use this refined inequality for
l=\psi_{h}(x_{1})
and
p=4
to conclude (
6.9
). Finally, we proceed as before, using again Lemma
6.3
to get

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{\Omega}\rho\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{u}:\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{w}_{h})\ \mathrm{d}x\bigg|\leq C(\|\mathbf{u}_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}).

Putting together the bounds obtained, we finish the proof of the proposition.
∎

6.2.2
The (no-)collision results

We will prove the (no-)collision results in a general setting, meaning for both two and three dimensions, as well as for both parabolic and ball-shaped solids. Our main theorem reads:

Theorem 6.7
.

Let
d\in\{2,3\}
, and
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be of class
C^{1,\alpha}
for some
\alpha\in[0,1]
such that
\dist(\mathcal{S}(0),\partial\Omega)>0
. Moreover, assume that the solid’s density
\rho_{\mathcal{S}}
is larger than the fluid’s one
\rho_{\mathcal{F}}
, meaning
\rho_{\mathcal{S}}>\rho_{\mathcal{F}}
.

1.

If
\alpha<\frac{d-1}{2}
, then the solid collides with
\partial\Omega
in finite time.

2.

If
\alpha\geq\frac{d-1}{2}
, then the solid stays away from
\partial\Omega
for all times.

The proof of the above theorem we present here is the one given in
[
30
]
for the case
d=2
, but the same ideas also work for
d=3
, see
[
31
]
, where a ball-shaped solid is considered, and
[
23
]
for an energy-based consideration for parabolic shapes. Moreover, combined with Starovoitov’s results, we can state the following

Corollary 6.8
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
6.7
, for
2\alpha<d-1
such that collision happens, the solid collides with rate

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to T_{\ast}}h(t)|t-T_{\ast}|^{-\eta}=0,\qquad\eta=\frac{1+\alpha}{d-\alpha}.

Remark 6.9
.

In contrast to Theorem
6.7
, Starovoitov gave an example of a moving ball such that collision appears, which we will review in Chapter
7
. As it seems at first glance to be in contradiction with the above result such a ball shall behave like a “parabola” with
{\alpha=1}
, we see that Starovoitov’s example needs an additional “singular” force
\mathbf{f}\in L^{2}(0,T;\widehat{W}^{-1,2}(\Omega))
adapted to the constructed velocity to ensure collision, where
\widehat{W}^{-1,2}(\Omega)
is the dual to
\{\mathbf{u}\in W_{0}^{1,2}(\Omega):{\div\mathbf{u}=0}\}
. On the other hand, Theorem
6.7
is valid for arbitrary forces in
(L^{p}+\nabla L^{p})(\Omega)
,
1<p\leq\infty
, sufficiently regular in time.

With the help of Lemmata
6.3
,
6.5
, and Proposition
6.6
, we are ready to prove Theorem
6.7
.

Proof of Theorem
6.7
.

By the regularity achieved for
\mathbf{w}_{h}
, we are allowed to use it as a test function for the momentum equation. This gives

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\rho\mathbf{u}\cdot\partial_{s}\mathbf{w}_{h(s)}+\rho\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{u}:\nabla\mathbf{w}_{h(s)}-\nabla\mathbf{u}:\nabla\mathbf{w}_{h(s)}-\rho g\mathbf{e}_{d}\cdot\mathbf{w}_{h(s)}\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}(\rho\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{w}_{h})(t)\ \mathrm{d}x-\int_{\Omega}\mathbf{m}_{0}\cdot\mathbf{w}_{h(0)}\ \mathrm{d}x.

Since
\rho=\rho_{\mathcal{F}}\chi_{\mathcal{F}(t)}+\rho_{\mathcal{S}}\chi_{\mathcal{S}(t)}
and
\div\mathbf{w}_{h}=0
, we get

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\rho g\mathbf{e}_{d}\cdot\mathbf{w}_{h(t)}\ \mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{S}(t)}\rho_{\mathcal{S}}g\mathbf{e}_{d}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{d}+\int_{\mathcal{F}(t)}\rho_{\mathcal{F}}g\nabla[x\mapsto x_{d}]\cdot\mathbf{w}_{h(t)}

\displaystyle=\rho_{\mathcal{S}}g|\mathcal{S}(0)|+\rho_{\mathcal{F}}g\int_{\partial\mathcal{F}(t)\setminus\partial\Omega}x_{d}\mathbf{e}_{d}\cdot\mathbf{n}\ \mathrm{d}\sigma=\rho_{\mathcal{S}}g|\mathcal{S}(0)|-\rho_{\mathcal{F}}g\int_{\partial\mathcal{S}(t)}x_{d}\mathbf{e}_{d}\cdot\mathbf{n}\ \mathrm{d}\sigma

\displaystyle=\rho_{\mathcal{S}}g|\mathcal{S}(0)|-\rho_{\mathcal{F}}g\int_{\mathcal{S}(t)}\div(x_{d}\mathbf{e}_{d})\ \mathrm{d}x=g|\mathcal{S}(0)|(\rho_{\mathcal{S}}-\rho_{\mathcal{F}}),

where we have used that
\mathbf{w}_{h}|_{\partial\Omega}=0
and
\mathbf{n}|_{\partial\mathcal{F}(t)\setminus\partial\Omega}=-\mathbf{n}|_{\partial\mathcal{S}(t)}
. Further, we write

\displaystyle 2\int_{\Omega}\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{u})(t):\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{w}_{h(t)})\ \mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\dot{h}(t)\int_{\partial\mathcal{F}(t)}(\nabla\mathbf{w}_{h(t)}-q_{h(t)}\mathbb{I})\mathbf{n}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{d}\ \mathrm{d}\sigma-\int_{\mathcal{F}(t)}(\Delta\mathbf{w}_{h(t)}-\nabla q_{h(t)})\cdot\mathbf{u}\ \mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\dot{h}(t)n(h)-\int_{\mathcal{F}(t)}(\Delta\mathbf{w}_{h(t)}-\nabla q_{h(t)})\cdot\mathbf{u}\ \mathrm{d}x,

where
n(h)
is as in Proposition
6.6
. Note that here we have used
\div\mathbf{u}=0
in order to “smuggle in” the pressure
q_{h(t)}
. Denoting

\displaystyle N(h(t)):=\int_{0}^{t}n(h(s))\ \mathrm{d}s,

and recalling the definition of
R(t)
as in Proposition
6.6
, we can write the weak formulation of the momentum equation as

\displaystyle N(h(t))+(\rho_{\mathcal{S}}-\rho_{\mathcal{F}})g|\mathcal{S}(0)|t

\displaystyle=R(t).

The proof is now easily finished: if
\beta\geq 1
in Proposition
6.6
, then

\displaystyle N(h(t))\gtrsim(\rho_{\mathcal{F}}-\rho_{\mathcal{S}})t-(1+\sqrt{t}).

Moreover, by the same token for
h>0
small enough,

\displaystyle N(h(t))\lesssim\begin{cases}|\log h|&\text{if }\beta=1,\\
h^{1-\beta}&\text{if }\beta>1.\end{cases}

This yields

\displaystyle|\log h|(t)\lesssim(\rho_{\mathcal{S}}-\rho_{\mathcal{F}})t+\sqrt{t}+1<\infty\quad\forall\ t<T_{\ast},

in particular,

\displaystyle h(t)\geq C\exp\{-(\rho_{\mathcal{S}}-\rho_{\mathcal{F}})t-\sqrt{t}\}.

Recalling
\rho_{\mathcal{S}}>\rho_{\mathcal{F}}
, this means that
h
cannot vanish in finite time and no collision occurs. Especially, the maximal existence time of the solution
(\rho,\mathbf{u})
is
T_{\ast}=\infty
. Note that
\beta\geq 1
is satisfied if
d=2
and
\alpha\geq 1/2
, or if
d=3
and
\alpha=1
, which fits precisely the cases of a “blunt” parabola and a ball-shaped obstacle, respectively.

If
\beta<1
which can be just the case for
d=2
and
\alpha<1/2
, then
n\in L^{1}(0,T_{\ast})
and
N
as its primitive is continuous. Since
h(t)
is bounded below and above, we deduce

\displaystyle-\infty<\inf_{t\in(0,T_{\ast})}N(h(t))\lesssim(\rho_{\mathcal{F}}-\rho_{\mathcal{S}})t+\sqrt{t}+1.

(6.10)

If now
T_{\ast}=\infty
, we can send
t\to\infty
on the right-hand side of the above inequality. But
\rho_{\mathcal{S}}>\rho_{\mathcal{F}}
, thus the right-hand side goes to
-\infty
, which is a contradiction. Eventually,
T_{\ast}<\infty
, meaning that
h
vanishes in finite time and collision occurs. The proof is finished.
∎

6.2.3
Concluding remarks

A similar but more sophisticated estimation of the drag force using energy considerations was given in
[
23
, Section 3.1]
, although just for the incompressible Stokes equations. To begin, the drag force on the solid
\mathcal{S}
is defined as the component of the total force the fluid applies on the solid which is parallel to the flow velocity, that is,

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{h}=\int_{\partial\mathcal{S}_{h}}\mathbf{u}\cdot(\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{u})-p\mathbb{I})\mathbf{n}\ \mathrm{d}\sigma.

Next, by the standard existence theory of Stokes equations, we can show the following

Lemma 6.10
.

There exists a unique solution
\mathbf{v}_{h}\in W_{0}^{1,2}(\Omega)
to

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\div\mathbf{v}_{h}=0&\text{in }\mathcal{F}_{h},\\
-\Delta\mathbf{v}_{h}+\nabla p_{h}=0&\text{in }\mathcal{F}_{h},\\
\mathbf{v}_{h}=\mathbf{e}_{3}&\text{on }\mathcal{S}_{h},\\
\mathbf{v}_{h}=0&\text{on }\partial\Omega.\end{cases}

Since
\mathbf{v}_{h}|_{\partial\mathcal{S}_{h}}=\mathbf{e}_{3}
,
\mathbf{v}_{h}|_{\partial\Omega}=0
, and
\div\mathbf{v}_{h}=0
, we may write

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{D}_{h}&=\int_{\partial\mathcal{S}_{h}\cup\partial\Omega}\mathbf{v}_{h}\cdot(\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{v}_{h})-p_{h}\mathbb{I})\mathbf{n}\ \mathrm{d}\sigma=\int_{\mathcal{F}_{h}}\div(\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{v}_{h})\mathbf{v}_{h}-p_{h}\mathbf{v}_{h})\ \mathrm{d}x\\
&=\int_{\mathcal{F}_{h}}(\Delta\mathbf{v}_{h}-\nabla p_{h})\cdot\mathbf{v}_{h}+\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{v}_{h}):\nabla\mathbf{v}_{h}-p_{h}\div\mathbf{v}_{h}\ \mathrm{d}x=\int_{\mathcal{F}_{h}}|\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{v}_{h})|^{2}\ \mathrm{d}x.\end{split}

(6.11)

Thus, the drag force is completely determined by the behavior of the symmetric gradient of
\mathbf{v}_{h}
. The test functions
\mathbf{w}_{h}
constructed above now “almost” solve this Stokes problem for
\mathbf{v}_{h}
in the sense that
-\Delta\mathbf{w}_{h}+\nabla q_{h}
does not vanish, but is controlled as seen by Lemma
6.5
. Hence, the drag force calculated with
\mathbf{w}_{h}
instead of
\mathbf{v}_{h}
shall behave similarly. Indeed, the outcomes of
[
23
]
roughly read

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{h}(\mathbf{w}_{h})\sim\begin{cases}h^{\frac{1-3\alpha}{1+\alpha}}&\text{if }\alpha>\frac{1}{3},\\
|\log h|&\text{if }\alpha=\frac{1}{3},\\
1&\text{if }\alpha<\frac{1}{3},\end{cases}

which fits our calculations done before. Note moreover that this means the convective term
\rho_{\mathcal{F}}\div(\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{u})
does not play a role in the question whether or not collision occurs. Heuristically, this is again easy to explain: by Theorem
6.1
, the solid touches the ground with zero speed, meaning the fluid behaves as a creeping (also called Stokes) flow. Such flows are usually modelled by the Navier-Stokes equations with negligible convective term (if the fluid’s velocity is small, then the “quadratic” convective term is even smaller), which gives rise precisely to the Stokes equations.

As for compressible fluids, there arises another difficulty. Applying the same technique as before, introducing the functions
(\mathbf{w}_{h},q_{h})
as test functions, and integrating by parts in the term
\int_{\Omega}\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{u}):\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{w}_{h})\ \mathrm{d}x
, we get an additional term

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{F}_{h}}q_{h}\div\mathbf{u}\ \mathrm{d}x,

which we cannot control although the pressure
q_{h}
is explicitly given.
Moreover, we cannot apply the same technique as for incompressible fluids since there might be regions with
\rho_{\mathcal{F}}=0
(vacuum), but also regions with
\rho_{\mathcal{F}}>\rho_{\mathcal{S}}
, hence, naively, inequality (
6.10
) tells us nothing.

Lastly, let us also mention Starovoitov’s work
[
45
]
, where the author gives a detailed analysis of the solid’s behavior
during
contact. The main outcomes of this work are that if
d=2
, the solid sticks to the boundary of its container as long as
\alpha\geq\frac{2}{3}
(in fact, for non-Newtonian fluids with
\mathbf{u}\in L^{p}(0,T;W^{1,p}(\Omega))
, this holds as long as
(2p-1)\alpha\geq 2
). If
d=3
, the solid still can rotate around an axis orthogonal to the boundary at the point of collision as long as
\alpha=1
(non-Newtonian:
(2p-1)\alpha\geq 3
). In particular, if the solid touches the boundary in more than one point, then also in three dimensions, it sticks motionless on
\partial\Omega
. The same restrictions occur in
[
20
, Theorem 3.2]
for
d=2
, and
[
21
, Theorem 1.1]
for
d=3
as optimal values in the sense that for the reversed inequalities, there exist
\Omega
and a vector field
\mathbf{u}\in L^{p}(0,T;W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega))
such that the solid
\mathcal{S}
still moves (surprisingly, when dropping the assumption
\div\mathbf{u}=0
, the condition for
d=2
has to be replaced by
(p-1)\alpha\geq 2
, see
[
20
, Theorem 2.1]
). We will come back to this in the next chapter.

Chapter 7
Special forces, non-uniqueness, and no-collision with controls

In this chapter, we investigate the following three problems for ball-shaped solids:

1.

Give a specific example of a solution
\mathbf{u}
and a driving force
\mathbf{f}
for incompressible Navier-Stokes equations such that collision for a ball-shaped solid happens.

2.

Show that, for a specific force
\mathbf{f}
, the solution to the Navier-Stokes equations is not unique.

3.

Show that collision in a compressible fluid is forbidden for additional controls, or higher regularity of the solution.

Recall that for incompressible fluids and gravity, we just proved that collision for a ball-shaped body is
forbidden
. As we will see in the next section, this does not contradict the outcome for the first problem, since the corresponding driving force will be sufficiently “bad”.

7.1
A singular force

Let us start with the example of a “singular” driving force for incompressible Navier-Stokes equations for the two-dimensional case
d=2
, following the presentation of
[
45
]
. We make the assumptions
\Omega=B_{R}(0)
,
\mathcal{S}(t)=B_{r}(\mathbf{G}(t))
,
0<r<R
, and
\mathbf{G}(t)=({\mathrm{g}}(t),0)
with
|{\mathrm{g}}(t)|\leq R-r
for all
t\in(0,T)
, meaning the solid moves inside
\Omega
just along the horizontal axis. The idea in constructing a colliding solution will be the following: first, we define a specific velocity field having the desired properties of energy estimates and collision; second, the driving force will be the error coming from inserting the constructed solution in the Navier-Stokes equations. This follows the heuristics of “construct a function that you want, calculate the derivatives, and call everything that you don’t want to have the driving force”.

Following these heuristics, we construct a velocity field that will ensure collision in finite time. We will rely on the construction of
[
46
, Section 2]
, however, we choose here to directly apply polar coordinates instead of first using Cartesian coordinates and then make a second change of variables as in the reference. To this end, let us define a map
F:(0,T)\times\Omega\ni(t,\rho,\theta)\mapsto x\in\Omega
via

\displaystyle x_{1}

\displaystyle=F_{1}(t,\rho,\theta)=\rho\cos\theta+\sigma(t)\big(R-\rho\big),

\displaystyle x_{2}

\displaystyle=F_{2}(t,\rho,\theta)=\rho\sin\theta,

where
\sigma(t)={\mathrm{g}}(t)(R-r)^{-1}\in[0,1)
such that
\lim_{t\to T_{\ast}}\sigma(t)=1
. As it is convenient for us, for
\xi=(\rho\cos\theta,\rho\sin\theta)
, we will not distinguish between
F(t,\rho,\theta)
and
F(t,\xi)=F(t,\rho\cos\theta,\rho\sin\theta)
. It is easy to see that for any
t\in(0,T)
we have
F(t,\Omega)=\Omega
and
F(t,B_{r}(0))=\mathcal{S}(t)
. Moreover, if
\xi\in\partial\Omega
such that
\rho=R
, then
F(t,\xi)=\xi
. Computing the inverse mapping
F^{-1}(t,x)
, we first find

\displaystyle(x_{1}-\sigma R)^{2}+x_{2}^{2}=\rho^{2}(1-\sigma^{2})-2\sigma\rho(x_{1}-\sigma R),

which then yields

\displaystyle\rho=\frac{1}{1-\sigma^{2}}\left(\sigma(x_{1}-\sigma R)+\sqrt{(1+\sigma^{2})(x_{1}-\sigma R)^{2}+x_{2}^{2}}\right),

\displaystyle\theta=\begin{cases}\arcsin\frac{x_{2}}{\rho}&\text{if }x_{2}\geq 0,\\
2\pi-\arcsin\frac{x_{2}}{\rho}&\text{if }x_{2}<0.\end{cases}

Especially, we have
F^{-1}(t,\Omega)=\Omega
and
F^{-1}(t,\mathcal{S}(t))=B_{r}(0)
. The actions of
F
and
F^{-1}
are depicted in Figure
7.1
.

\xi_{1}
\xi_{2}
\Omega
r
R
x_{1}
x_{2}
\Omega
\mathcal{S}
x=F(t,\xi)
\xi=F^{-1}(t,x)

Figure 7.1:
The actions of the map
F(t,\xi)=F(t,\rho,\theta)
and its inverse
F^{-1}(t,x)
.

Remark 7.1
.

We will use a similar mapping to transform a ball falling over a half-plane to a concentric situation in Chapter
8
by using complex analysis. In fact, the same results found in the present section can be obtained by mapping the domain
\Omega\setminus\mathcal{S}=B_{R}(0)\setminus B_{r}(({\mathrm{g}},0))\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\sim\mathbb{C}
to the concentric domain
B_{1}(0)\setminus B_{r_{0}}(0)\subset\mathbb{C}
by the conformal function

\displaystyle\tilde{F}(z)

\displaystyle=R\frac{z-c}{R^{2}-cz},\quad c=\frac{1}{2{\mathrm{g}}}(R^{2}+{\mathrm{g}}^{2}-r^{2}-W),\quad r_{0}=\tilde{F}({\mathrm{g}}+r)=\frac{2Rr}{R^{2}+r^{2}-{\mathrm{g}}^{2}+W},

\displaystyle W

\displaystyle=\big((R+r+{\mathrm{g}})(R+r-{\mathrm{g}})(R-r+{\mathrm{g}})(R-r-{\mathrm{g}})\big)^{\frac{1}{2}},

see
[
28
, §5.7]
for the derivation of this mapping.

Computing the Jacobi matrices of
F
and
F^{-1}
, we see

\displaystyle\nabla_{(\rho,\theta)}F

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta-\sigma&-\rho\sin\theta\\
\sin\theta&\rho\cos\theta\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}(F^{-1})

\displaystyle=[\nabla_{(\rho,\theta)}F]^{-1}\circ F^{-1}=\left[\frac{1}{\rho(1-\sigma\cos\theta)}\begin{pmatrix}\rho\cos\theta&\rho\sin\theta\\
-\sin\theta&\cos\theta-\sigma\end{pmatrix}\right]\circ F^{-1},

leading for the Jacobians to

\displaystyle J_{F}

\displaystyle=\det(\nabla_{\xi}F)=\rho(1-\sigma(t)\cos\theta),

\displaystyle J_{F^{-1}}|_{x=F(t,\xi)}

\displaystyle=J_{F}^{-1}\circ F^{-1}=\frac{1}{\rho(1-\sigma(t)\cos\theta)}\circ F^{-1}(t,x).

Since we consider a two-dimensional domain, it is convenient to define the velocity via a stream function
1
1

1

The streamlines
\mathbf{X}
defined by
\dot{\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{u}(t,\mathbf{X})
are in this context lines where the stream function
\psi(t,\mathbf{X})
is constant (that is, level sets of
\psi
).

\psi(t,x)
associated to the flow, that is,
\mathbf{u}(t,x)=\nabla^{\perp}\psi(t,x)
. We still want that

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}=0\ \text{on}\ \partial\Omega,\quad\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{u})=0\ \text{in}\ \mathcal{S}.

(7.1)

Let us consider the symmetric domain
B_{R}(0)\setminus B_{r}(0)=F(t,\Omega\setminus\mathcal{S}(t))
, and we search for a function
\tilde{\mathbf{u}}
inside this domain. Then, the function
\tilde{\mathbf{u}}(t,\xi)
shall satisfy

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{u}}(t,\xi)=0\ \text{whenever}\ |\xi|=R,\quad\mathbb{D}(\tilde{\mathbf{u}})(t,\xi)=0\ \text{whenever}\ |\xi|<r.

As our solid shall move in
x_{1}
-direction with speed
\dot{\mathrm{g}}(t)
, we search for
\tilde{\mathbf{u}}
in the form
\tilde{\mathbf{u}}(t,\xi)|_{B_{r}(0)}=\dot{\mathrm{g}}(t)(1,0)^{T}=\dot{\mathrm{g}}(t)\nabla^{\perp}[\xi\mapsto\xi_{2}=\rho\sin\theta]
. This leads us to the ansatz
\tilde{\psi}(t,\xi)=\tilde{\psi}(t,\rho,\theta)=\dot{\mathrm{g}}(t)\rho\phi(\rho)\sin\theta
for some smooth function
\phi
with
\phi(\rho)=1
for
\rho<r
. The boundary condition for
\tilde{\mathbf{u}}
on
\partial\Omega
then suggests to search
\phi
with
\phi(R)=0
. In all other parameters,
\phi
is free to choose; we therefore may choose this function such that

\displaystyle\phi:[0,\infty)\to[0,1]\ \text{is decreasing on}\ [0,\infty),

\displaystyle\phi(\rho)=1\ \text{if}\ \rho<r,\quad\phi(R)=\phi^{\prime}(r)=\phi^{\prime}(R)=0.

The example given in
[
46
]
is

\displaystyle\phi(\rho)=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }0\leq\rho<r,\\
(R-r)^{-3}(\rho-R)^{2}(2\rho-3r+R)&\text{if }r\leq\rho\leq R,\\
0&\text{else},\end{cases}

which is an easy function in the sense of applications and numerical experiments since it is just a polynomial of degree three. Especially for
r=1
and
R=3
,
\phi(\rho)=\frac{1}{4}\rho(\rho-3)^{2}
inside
[r,R]
. As another example the function
\zeta
introduced in Section
3.2
may serve.

Back to the velocity field in the original domain, we define
\mathbf{u}(t,x)=\nabla^{\perp}\psi(t,x)
with

\displaystyle\psi(t,x)=\tilde{\psi}(t,\rho,\theta)|_{(\rho,\theta)=F^{-1}(t,x)}=\dot{{\mathrm{g}}}(t)[\rho\phi(\rho)\sin\theta]\circ F^{-1}(t,x).

Note especially that
\mathbf{u}
satisfies precisely (
7.1
). To verify that
\mathbf{u}
serves as an appropriate solution to our problem, we have to verify that
\mathbf{u}\in L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))\cap L^{2}(0,T;W_{0}^{1,2}(\Omega))
. As for the
L^{2}
-norm and since
|\nabla^{\perp}\psi|=|\nabla\psi|
, we first calculate by chain rule

\displaystyle\begin{split}\nabla_{x}\psi(t,x)&=\left(\nabla_{(\rho,\theta)}\tilde{\psi}(t,\rho,\theta)\cdot[\nabla_{(\rho,\theta)}F(t,\rho,\theta)]^{-1}\right)\circ F^{-1}(t,x)\\
&=\left[\frac{\dot{{\mathrm{g}}}}{1-\sigma\cos\theta}\big(\rho\phi^{\prime}(\rho)\sin\theta\cos\theta,\ \phi(\rho)(1-\sigma\cos\theta)+\rho\phi^{\prime}(\rho)\sin^{2}\theta\big)\right]\circ F^{-1}(t,x).\end{split}

(7.2)

Thus, as
F(t,\Omega)=\Omega=F^{-1}(t,\Omega)
and
J_{F}=\rho(1-\sigma\cos\theta)
,

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}|\nabla_{x}\psi(t,x)|^{2}\ \mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\Big|\nabla_{(\rho,\theta)}\tilde{\psi}(t,\rho,\theta)\cdot[\nabla_{(\rho,\theta)}F(t,\rho,\theta)]^{-1}\cdot\sqrt{J_{F}}\Big|^{2}\circ F^{-1}(t,x)\cdot J_{F}^{-1}\ \mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{R}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\Big|\nabla_{(\rho,\theta)}\tilde{\psi}(t,\rho,\theta)\cdot[\nabla_{(\rho,\theta)}F(t,\rho,\theta)]^{-1}\Big|^{2}\rho(1-\sigma\cos\theta)\ \mathrm{d}\rho\ \mathrm{d}\theta.

With equation (
7.2
), we have that the integrand equals

\displaystyle\frac{|\dot{{\mathrm{g}}}|^{2}}{1-\sigma\cos\theta}\Big[\rho^{3}(\phi^{\prime}(\rho))^{2}\sin^{2}\theta+\rho^{2}(\phi^{2}(\rho))^{\prime}\sin^{2}\theta(1-\sigma\cos\theta)+\rho(\phi(\rho))^{2}(1-\sigma\cos\theta)^{2}\Big],

leading finally to

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}&=|\dot{{\mathrm{g}}}|^{2}\int_{0}^{R}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{1}{1-\sigma\cos\theta}\Big[\rho^{3}(\phi^{\prime}(\rho))^{2}\sin^{2}\theta\\
&\qquad+\rho^{2}(\phi^{2}(\rho))^{\prime}\sin^{2}\theta(1-\sigma\cos\theta)+\rho(\phi(\rho))^{2}(1-\sigma\cos\theta)^{2}\Big]\ \mathrm{d}\rho\ \mathrm{d}\theta\\
&=\mu_{1}(r,R)\nu_{1}(\sigma)|\dot{\sigma}|^{2},\end{split}

(7.3)

where

\displaystyle\mu_{1}(r,R)

\displaystyle=(R-r)^{2}\int_{r}^{R}\rho^{3}(\phi^{\prime}(\rho))^{2}\ \mathrm{d}\rho<\infty,

\displaystyle\nu_{1}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{\sin^{2}\theta}{1-\sigma\cos\theta}\ \mathrm{d}\theta=\frac{2\pi}{1+\sqrt{1-\sigma^{2}}}\in[\pi,2\pi].

In turn, we have:

Lemma 7.2
.

The function
\mathbf{u}\in L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))
if and only if
\dot{\sigma}\in L^{\infty}(0,T)
.

To verify that
\mathbf{u}\in L^{2}(0,T;W_{0}^{1,2}(\Omega))
, by boundedness of Riesz transform in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
(see (
1.8
)), it is enough to estimate
\Delta\psi
. First, using again chain rule,

\displaystyle\Delta_{x}\psi

\displaystyle=\div_{x}(\nabla_{x}\psi)=\div_{x}((\nabla_{(\rho,\theta)}\tilde{\psi}(t,\rho,\theta)\cdot[\nabla_{(\rho,\theta)}F(t,\rho,\theta)]^{-1})\circ F^{-1}(t,x))

\displaystyle=\left(\nabla_{(\rho,\theta)}[\nabla_{(\rho,\theta)}\tilde{\psi}(t,\rho,\theta)\cdot(\nabla_{(\rho,\theta)}F(t,\rho,\theta))^{-1}]:[\nabla_{(\rho,\theta)}F(t,\rho,\theta)]^{-T}\right)\circ F^{-1}(t,x).

Some quick formal discussion
2
2

2

Here, we do not have to concentrate on the dependence on
\rho
since this part will always be integrable due to compact support of
\phi
.
hints in terms of
\theta
that we have
\big|\nabla_{(\rho,\theta)}[\nabla_{(\rho,\theta)}F(t,\rho,\theta)]^{-1}\big|\sim\big|[\nabla_{(\rho,\theta)}F(t,\rho,\theta)]^{-1}\big|^{2}
. Hence,

\displaystyle|\Delta\psi|\sim\big|\nabla_{(\rho,\theta)}\tilde{\psi}(t,\rho,\theta)\cdot[\nabla_{(\rho,\theta)}F(t,\rho,\theta)]^{-1}\big|\cdot\big|[\nabla_{(\rho,\theta)}F(t,\rho,\theta)]^{-1}\big|\sim\sin\theta(1-\sigma\cos\theta)^{-2},

and therefore we shall expect

\displaystyle\|\Delta\psi\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\lesssim\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{\sin^{2}\theta}{(1-\sigma\cos\theta)^{3}}\ \mathrm{d}\theta.

To make this formal guess rigorous, we calculate

\displaystyle\nabla_{(\rho,\theta)}(\nabla_{(\rho,\theta)}\tilde{\psi}\cdot(\nabla_{(\rho,\theta)}F)^{-1})

\displaystyle=\nabla_{(\rho,\theta)}\left[\frac{\dot{{\mathrm{g}}}}{1-\sigma\cos\theta}(\rho\phi^{\prime}\sin\theta\cos\theta,\ \phi(1-\sigma\cos\theta)+\rho\phi^{\prime}\sin^{2}\theta)\right]

\displaystyle=\dot{{\mathrm{g}}}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{(\phi^{\prime}+\rho\phi^{\prime\prime})\sin\theta\cos\theta}{1-\sigma\cos\theta}&\rho\phi^{\prime}\frac{\cos^{2}\theta-\sin^{2}\theta-\sigma\cos^{3}\theta}{(1-\sigma\cos\theta)^{2}}\\
\phi^{\prime}+\frac{(\phi^{\prime}+\rho\phi^{\prime\prime})\sin^{2}\theta}{1-\sigma\cos\theta}&\rho\phi^{\prime}\frac{2\cos\theta\sin\theta-2\sigma\cos^{2}\theta\sin\theta-\sigma\sin^{3}\theta}{(1-\sigma\cos\theta)^{2}}\end{pmatrix},

thus

\displaystyle\Delta_{x}\psi

\displaystyle=\left[\frac{\dot{{\mathrm{g}}}}{1-\sigma\cos\theta}\left(\frac{\rho\phi^{\prime\prime}(\rho)\sin\theta}{1-\sigma\cos\theta}+3\phi^{\prime}(\rho)\sin\theta+\frac{\sigma^{2}\phi^{\prime}(\rho)\sin^{3}\theta}{(1-\sigma\cos\theta)^{2}}\right)\right]\circ F^{-1}(t,x),

\displaystyle|\Delta_{x}\psi|^{2}

\displaystyle=\left[\frac{|\dot{{\mathrm{g}}}|^{2}}{(1-\sigma\cos\theta)^{2}}\left(\frac{\rho^{2}(\phi^{\prime\prime}(\rho))^{2}\sin^{2}\theta}{(1-\sigma\cos\theta)^{2}}+\frac{3\rho[(\phi^{\prime}(\rho))^{2}]^{\prime}\sin^{2}\theta}{1-\sigma\cos\theta}+\frac{\rho[(\phi^{\prime}(\rho))^{2}]^{\prime}\sigma^{2}\sin^{4}\theta}{(1-\sigma\cos\theta)^{3}}\right.\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\left.\left.+9(\phi^{\prime}(\rho))^{2}\sin^{2}\theta+\frac{6\sigma^{2}(\phi^{\prime}(\rho))^{2}\sin^{4}\theta}{(1-\sigma\cos\theta)^{2}}+\frac{\sigma^{4}(\phi^{\prime}(\rho))^{2}\sin^{6}\theta}{(1-\sigma\cos\theta)^{4}}\right)\right]\circ F^{-1}(t,x).

Hence, for
f(\rho)=\rho^{2}(\phi^{\prime\prime}(\rho))^{2}+4\rho[(\phi^{\prime}(\rho))^{2}]^{\prime}+16(\phi^{\prime}(\rho))^{2}
, we finally get

\displaystyle\|\Delta\psi\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq|\dot{{\mathrm{g}}}|^{2}\int_{r}^{R}f(\rho)\ \mathrm{d}\rho\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{\sin^{2}\theta}{(1-\sigma\cos\theta)^{3}}+\frac{\sin^{2}\theta}{(1-\sigma\cos\theta)^{2}}+\frac{\sigma^{2}\sin^{4}\theta}{(1-\sigma\cos\theta)^{4}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{\sin^{2}\theta}{1-\sigma\cos\theta}+\frac{\sigma^{2}\sin^{4}\theta}{(1-\sigma\cos\theta)^{3}}+\frac{\sigma^{4}\sin^{6}\theta}{(1-\sigma\cos\theta)^{5}}\ \mathrm{d}\theta

\displaystyle\lesssim|\dot{{\mathrm{g}}}|^{2}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{\sin^{2}\theta}{(1-\sigma\cos\theta)^{3}}+\frac{\sigma^{2}\sin^{4}\theta}{(1-\sigma\cos\theta)^{4}}+\frac{\sigma^{4}\sin^{6}\theta}{(1-\sigma\cos\theta)^{5}}\ \mathrm{d}\theta,

where the implicit constant just depends on
r
and
R
(and
\phi
). Noting that in the above integral, all terms are equally singular as
\sigma\to 1
, we indeed find
3
3

3

This last integral can be solved in using the complex representations of
\sin\theta
and
\cos\theta
via
e^{i\theta}
and residue theorem (see
[
28
, §4.7]
). Similar calculations work for
\nu_{1}(\sigma)
in (
7.3
).

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{\sin^{2}\theta}{(1-\sigma\cos\theta)^{3}}+\frac{\sigma^{2}\sin^{4}\theta}{(1-\sigma\cos\theta)^{4}}+\frac{\sigma^{4}\sin^{6}\theta}{(1-\sigma\cos\theta)^{5}}\ \mathrm{d}\theta\lesssim\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{\sin^{2}\theta}{(1-\sigma\cos\theta)^{3}}\ \mathrm{d}\theta=\frac{\pi}{(1-\sigma^{2})^{3/2}};

in turn,

\displaystyle\|\Delta\psi\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\leq\mu_{2}(r,R)(1-\sigma^{2})^{-3/2}|\dot{\sigma}|^{2},

where
\mu_{2}(r,R)<\infty
is a positive constant depending only on
r
and
R
. Thus, we can state

Lemma 7.3
.

The function
\mathbf{u}\in L^{2}(0,T;W_{0}^{1,2}(\Omega))
if and only if
(1-\sigma^{2})^{-3/2}|\dot{\sigma}|^{2}\in L^{1}(0,T)
.

Remark 7.4
.

Note that the result of
[
46
, Lemma 2.2]
reads

\displaystyle\|\Delta\psi\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\leq\mu_{2}(r,R)(1-\sigma)^{-3/2}|\dot{\sigma}|^{2},

which, compared to our outcome, seems to miss an exponent
2
in
\sigma
. However, since
\sigma<1
, we immediately see
(1-\sigma^{2})^{-3/2}\leq(1-\sigma)^{-3/2}
; hence, our estimate is just sharper. Moreover, recalling that
h(t)=(R-r)(1-\sigma(t))
, and that Starovoitov’s condition reads
h^{-3/4}|\dot{h}|\in L^{2}(0,T)
, this fits precisely the estimate (
6.3
) in Theorem
6.1
, where then with
\alpha=1
and
p=d=2
, we have
\beta=\frac{1+2\alpha}{p(1+\alpha)}(p-\frac{d+\alpha}{1+2\alpha})=\frac{3}{4}
.

All that is left to do now is to find a function
\sigma(t)
with
\sigma(T_{\ast})=1
for some
T_{\ast}\in(0,T)
fulfilling the conditions of Lemmata
7.2
and
7.3
. Such a function is easy to find: let
T>0
and
T_{\ast}\in(0,T)
be fixed, then we can take

\displaystyle\sigma(t)=1-\bigg(\frac{t-T_{\ast}}{T}\bigg)^{4}.

(7.4)

It is easy to see that
\dot{\sigma},(1-\sigma^{2})^{-3/2}|\dot{\sigma}|^{2}\in L^{\infty}(0,T)
and in particular
\sigma
satisfies the conditions of Lemmata
7.2
and
7.3
. Hence, for this
\sigma
, the function
\mathbf{u}
constructed above is a velocity field satisfying the energy estimate and ensuring collision in finite time.

However, we are still missing one point: till now we don’t know that
\mathbf{u}
is a weak solution to the Stokes problem. To ensure also this last requirement, we need to bound the time derivative
\partial_{t}\mathbf{u}
. Obviously, it is enough to do this for
\partial_{t}\psi
, yielding

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\psi(t,x)

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}[\dot{\mathrm{g}}(t)\rho\phi(\rho)\sin\theta\circ F^{-1}(t,x)]

\displaystyle=\ddot{\mathrm{g}}(t)[\rho\phi(\rho)\sin\theta]\circ F^{-1}(t,x)+\dot{\mathrm{g}}(t)[\nabla_{(\rho,\theta)}(\rho\phi(\rho)\sin\theta)]\circ F^{-1}(t,x)\cdot\partial_{t}F^{-1}(t,x).

Together with
\partial_{t}F(t,\rho,\theta)=\dot{\sigma}(t)(R-\rho,0)^{T}
, by the usual change of variables
x=F(t,\rho,\theta)
we arrive at

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}\psi\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq\mu_{3}(r,R)(|\ddot{\sigma}|+|\dot{\sigma}|^{2})

for some constant
\mu_{3}(r,R)<\infty
. This yields

Lemma 7.5
.

If
\ddot{\sigma}\in L^{2}(0,T)
, then
\partial_{t}\mathbf{u}\in L^{2}(0,T;W^{-1,2}(\Omega))
.

Again, for our function
\ddot{\sigma}=\frac{{\rm d}^{2}}{{\rm d}t^{2}}(1-(t-T_{\ast})^{4}T^{-4})\in L^{\infty}(0,T)
, hence fulfilling the condition of the lemma. Setting now the force

\displaystyle\mathbf{f}:=\partial_{t}\mathbf{u}+\div(\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{u})-\div\mathbb{S}(\nabla\mathbf{u})\in L^{2}(0,T;W^{-1,2}(\Omega)),

we have proven the following

Theorem 7.6
.

If
\sigma(t)
is such that
\dot{\sigma}\in L^{\infty}(0,T)
,
\ddot{\sigma}\in L^{2}(0,T)
, and
(1-\sigma^{2})^{-3/2}|\dot{\sigma}|^{2}\in L^{1}(0,T)
, then there exists a force
\mathbf{f}\in L^{2}(0,T;W^{-1,2}(\Omega))
such that the function
\mathbf{u}
constructed above is a weak solution to the Navier-Stokes equations ensuring collision in finite time.

7.2
Two colliding solutions

Having found a solution to our problem, we investigate now the question whether this solution is uniquely determined. To this end, we will assume that there exists
T_{\ast}\in(0,T)
such that the ball-shaped body
\mathcal{S}=B(\mathbf{G}(t))
touches the boundary of
\Omega
. As seen in the last section, this can just happen if the driving force
\mathbf{f}
is “sufficiently bad”, meaning a distribution rather than a function. In particular, gravity is forbidden in this setting, since collision does not occur for a ball as proven in Chapter
6
.

We will follow the construction from
[
46
]
. Indeed, the idea is similar as in the foregoing section: the domains
\Omega
and
\mathcal{S}
will be the same disks in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. We also take the same velocity
\mathbf{u}=\nabla^{\perp}\psi(t,x)
up to time
T_{\ast}
. From time
T_{\ast}
