onwards, one solution will simply go “back in time”, whereas the other one will stick to the boundary of
\Omega
without any further movement. Note that this last solution is in some sense “allowed to exist”: indeed, as mentioned in Section
6.2.3
, our function
\mathbf{u}\in L^{p}(0,T;W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega))
with
p=2
and hence
(2p-1)\alpha\geq 2
as
\alpha=1
for a ball.

Recalling the form of
\sigma(t)
in (
7.4
), we see that
|\sigma(t)|<1
if
t\in[0,T_{\ast})\cup(T_{\ast},T]
and
\sigma(T_{\ast})=1
. Hence, in agreement with the calculations done in the previous section, we have
\mathbf{u}\in L^{\infty}(0,T_{\ast};L^{2}(\Omega))\cap L^{2}(0,T_{\ast};W_{0}^{1,2}(\Omega))
and
\partial_{t}\mathbf{u}\in L^{2}(0,T_{\ast};W^{-1,2}(\Omega))
. In particular, the function
\mathbf{u}
is strongly continuous in time in
L^{2}(\Omega)
, since
\|\mathbf{u}(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}
is continuous in time by (
7.3
). Thus, we can prolong
\mathbf{u}
continuously in time.

The first solution.

For
t>T_{\ast}
, we simply define as before
\mathbf{u}(t,x)=\nabla^{\perp}\psi(t,x)
. Since
\sigma
and hence
\psi
is symmetric in
(t-T_{\ast})
, this is the solution moving “back in time” (note that this is precisely the reason for taking the fourth power in the definition of
\sigma
). By construction, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}\in L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))\cap L^{2}(0,T;W_{0}^{1,2}(\Omega)),\quad\partial_{t}\mathbf{u}\in L^{2}(0,T;W^{-1,2}(\Omega)),

\displaystyle\div\mathbf{u}=0,\quad\mathbf{u}|_{\partial\Omega}=0,\quad\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{u})|_{\mathcal{S}}=0,

and that
\mathbf{u}
fulfils the energy inequality. All is left is to find a force
\mathbf{f}\in L^{2}(0,T;W^{-1,2}(\Omega))
such that the weak form of the momentum equation

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\partial_{t}\mathbf{u}\cdot\phi-(\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{u}-\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{u})):\mathbb{D}(\phi)\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}t=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\mathbf{f}\cdot\phi\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}t

holds for any
\phi\in L^{2}(0,T;W_{0}^{1,2}(\Omega))
such that
\div\phi=0
and
\mathbb{D}(\phi)|_{\mathcal{S}}=0
.

This is easy as well: by strong continuity of
\mathbf{u}
in time, we can view the left-hand side of the above equality as a continuous linear functional on
L^{2}(0,T;W_{0}^{1,2}(\Omega))
applied to
\phi
. In turn, we can find a force
\mathbf{f}
fulfilling the requirements needed; namely, as before,

\displaystyle\mathbf{f}:=\partial_{t}\mathbf{u}+\div(\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{u})-\div\mathbb{S}(\nabla\mathbf{u})\in L^{2}(0,T;W^{-1,2}(\Omega)).

(7.5)

As
\mathbf{f}
fulfils the weak formulation for
any

\phi\in L^{2}(0,T;W_{0}^{1,2}(\Omega))
, in particular it is an appropriate force for solenoidal
\phi
with
\mathbb{D}(\phi)|_{\mathcal{S}}=0
. This gives us the force and the first solution to our problem.

The second solution.

In order to finish the proof of non-uniqueness, we need to construct another solution
\mathbf{v}(t,x)
to the Navier-Stokes equations for the vector field
\mathbf{f}
constructed above. This solution will collide with
\partial\Omega
and then stick at its position. Note moreover that the body
\mathcal{S}
is a part of the solution; hence, for the second solution
\mathbf{v}
, we also need to make precise what the solid does. Denote by
\mathcal{R}
the domain occupied by the body for the velocity field
\mathbf{v}
.

We now proceed as the heuristics may suggest: the part until collision is

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}(t,x)=\mathbf{u}(t,x),\quad\mathcal{R}(t)=\mathcal{S}(t),\quad t\in[0,T_{\ast}].

As the solid touches
\partial\Omega
at
t=T_{\ast}
, the extended solution will be defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(t)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}(T_{\ast})=\mathcal{R}_{\ast}=\mathcal{S}(T_{\ast}),\quad t\in(T_{\ast},T],

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}(t,x)

\displaystyle=0\ \text{ for }\ t\in(T_{\ast},T],\quad x\in\mathcal{R}(t)=\mathcal{R}_{\ast}.

We remark that this extension, till now,
is just inside
\mathcal{R}
. It means that the solid does not move, but on the other hand, the fluid around may still do something. Thus, we search for
\mathbf{v}
in the (fixed) domain
\Omega\setminus\mathcal{R}_{\ast}
with right-hand side
\mathbf{f}
and
\mathbf{v}|_{\partial(\Omega\setminus\mathcal{R}_{\ast})}=0
. As
\mathbf{u}(T_{\ast},x)=0
for every
x\in\Omega
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{T_{\ast}}^{T}\int_{\Omega\setminus\mathcal{R}_{\ast}}\partial_{t}\mathbf{v}\cdot\phi-(\mathbf{v}\otimes\mathbf{v}-\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{v})):\mathbb{D}(\phi)-\mathbf{f}\cdot\phi\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}t=0

(7.6)

for any solenoidal function
\phi\in L^{2}(T_{\ast},T;W_{0}^{1,2}(\Omega\setminus\mathcal{R}_{\ast}))
with
\mathbb{D}(\phi)|_{\mathcal{R}_{\ast}}=0
. The general existence theory for two-dimensional incompressible Navier-Stokes equations
[
37
]
now implies that there is a unique function
\mathbf{v}\in L^{\infty}(T_{\ast},T;L^{2}(\Omega\setminus\mathcal{R}_{\ast}))\cap L^{2}(T_{\ast},T;W_{0}^{1,2}(\Omega\setminus\mathcal{R}_{\ast}))
such that
\div\mathbf{v}=0
and
\partial_{t}\mathbf{v}\in L^{2}(T_{\ast},T;W^{-1,2}(\Omega\setminus\mathcal{R}_{\ast}))
satisfying (
7.6
).

In turn, the functions
\mathbf{v}
and
\mathcal{R}
are now defined for all times
t\in[0,T]
and all
x\in\Omega
. It is left to show that
\mathbf{v}
and
\mathcal{R}
fulfil the momentum equation in its weak form in the whole of
\Omega
. We emphasize that the function
\mathbf{v}
inside
\Omega\setminus\mathcal{R}_{\ast}
is constructed using test functions from
L^{2}(T_{\ast},T;W_{0}^{1,2}(\Omega\setminus\mathcal{R}_{\ast}))
, whereas the momentum equation in the whole of
\Omega
needs to be valid for all test functions in
L^{2}(0,T;W_{0}^{1,2}(\Omega))
.

As
\mathbf{v}=0
in
\mathcal{R}(t)
for any
t\in[T_{\ast},T]
, it is enough to check that

\displaystyle\int_{T_{\ast}}^{T}\int_{\Omega\setminus\mathcal{R}_{\ast}}\partial_{t}\mathbf{v}\cdot\phi-(\mathbf{v}\otimes\mathbf{v}-\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{v})):\mathbb{D}(\phi)-\mathbf{f}\cdot\phi\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}t=0

for any solenoidal
\phi\in L^{2}(T_{\ast},T;W_{0}^{1,2}(\Omega))
with
\mathbb{D}(\phi)|_{\mathcal{R}}=0
. To this end, we recall a result from
[
45
, Theorem 2.1]
:

Lemma 7.7
.

Let
\mathcal{R}\subset\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be connected domains of class
C^{1,\alpha}
with
\alpha\in[\frac{2}{3},1]
such that
\dist(\mathcal{R},\partial\Omega)=0
. If
\phi\in W_{0}^{1,2}(\Omega)
is solenoidal and satisfies
\mathbb{D}(\phi)=0
on
\mathcal{R}
, then
\phi=0
on
\mathcal{R}
.

The above lemma in combination with (
7.6
), the fact that
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{u}
for any
t\in[0,T_{\ast}]
, and that
\mathbf{u}
is a weak solution to the Navier-Stokes equations with force
\mathbf{f}
given by (
7.5
) immediately yields the result. Moreover, as
\mathbf{v}
obviously differs from
\mathbf{u}
when
t>T_{\ast}
, this finishes the proof of non-uniqueness.

Remark 7.8
.

As for the three-dimensional case, we refer to
[
43
]
, where non-uniqueness was proven in a similar way as shown above. The ideas are essentially the same as in
[
46
]
, but the main difference is that the considered geometry consists of a bounded domain with two spherical holes, symmetric to a line on which the obstacle moves, and at a distance such that the body
\mathcal{S}
perfectly fits through them. This geometry enables the author to show that the force
\mathbf{f}
from (
7.5
) is not a mere distribution, but rather an element of
L^{2}(0,T;L^{p}(\Omega))
for some
1\leq p<2
.

7.3
Feedback law and higher regularity of solutions

We finish this chapter by again focussing on the compressible Navier-Stokes equations as given in Section
4.5
in dimension
d=3
, and give three particular examples of modified equations that ensure no collision.

Feedback law.

Following
[
34
, Section 4]
, the equations of motion of fluid-structure interaction we consider here are similar as before, the only difference being that we replace the fifth equation in (
4.1
) by

\displaystyle m\ddot{\mathbf{G}}=-\int_{\partial\mathcal{S}}\big(\mathbb{S}-p\mathbb{I}\big)\mathbf{n}\ \mathrm{d}\sigma+\mathbf{b},

where
\mathbf{b}(t)
is a feedback control of the form

\mathbf{b}(t)=-k_{p}(\mathbf{G}(t)-\mathbf{G}_{1})-k_{d}\dot{\mathbf{G}}(t).

(7.7)

In control engineering, such a feedback (
7.7
) is known as a
proportional-derivative (PD) controller
(see, for instance,
[
1
]
for a fluid-beam interaction, and the references therein). The feedback
\mathbf{b}(t)
can be thought of being generated by a (massless) spring with spring constant
k_{p}>0
, and a mechanical damper with damping constant
k_{d}\geq 0
connected between the solid’s center of mass
\mathbf{G}(t)
and a fixed point
\mathbf{G}_{1}\in\Omega
. The definition of weak solutions is similar to Definition
4.5
, the only difference being that the weak formulation of the momentum equation is replaced by

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{\Omega}(\rho\mathbf{u})\cdot\partial_{t}\phi+(\rho\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{u}):\mathbb{D}(\phi)+p(\rho)\div\phi-\mathbb{S}(\mathbf{u}):\mathbb{D}(\phi)\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}t\\
&=\int_{\Omega}\rho(\tau)\mathbf{u}(\tau)\cdot\phi(\tau)-\mathbf{m}_{0}\cdot\phi(0)\ \mathrm{d}x+\int_{0}^{\tau}\mathbf{b}\cdot\ell_{\phi}\ \mathrm{d}t\end{split}

(7.8)

for any
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}([0,T)\times\Omega)
with
\phi(t,x)=\ell_{\phi}(t)+\omega_{\phi}(t)(x-\mathbf{G}(t))
near
\mathcal{S}(t)
, and the energy inequality (
4.6
) is replaced by

\displaystyle\left[\int_{\Omega}\frac{1}{2}\rho|\mathbf{u}|^{2}+P(\rho)\ \mathrm{d}x\right]_{t=0}^{t=\tau}+\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{\Omega}\mathbb{S}:\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{u})\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}t\leq\int_{0}^{\tau}\mathbf{b}\cdot\dot{\mathbf{G}}\ \mathrm{d}t.

(7.9)

The existence of weak solutions can be established by following
[
16
, Theorem 4.1]
, and the existence of strong solutions can be found in
[
42
, Theorem 1.1]
. Combining the energy estimate (
7.9
) with the feedback law (
7.7
), we obtain the following no-collision result:

Proposition 7.9
.

Let
\mathbf{G}_{1}\in\Omega
with
\operatorname{dist}(\mathbf{G}_{1},\partial\Omega)>1
and assume that
\mathbf{b}
satisfies the feedback law (
7.7
). Let
(\rho,\mathbf{u},\mathbf{G})
be a weak solution. Then

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\int_{\mathcal{F}(\tau)}\frac{1}{2}\rho|\mathbf{u}|^{2}+P(\rho)\ \mathrm{d}x+\frac{m}{2}|\dot{\mathbf{G}}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{J}\omega\cdot\omega+\frac{k_{p}}{2}|\mathbf{G}(\tau)-\mathbf{G}_{1}|^{2}+{k_{d}}\int_{0}^{\tau}|\dot{\mathbf{G}}|^{2}\ \mathrm{d}t\\
&+\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{\mathcal{F}(\tau)}\mathbb{S}:\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{u})\ \mathrm{d}x\ \mathrm{d}t\\
&\leq\int_{\mathcal{F}(0)}\frac{|\mathbf{m}_{0}|^{2}}{2\rho_{0}}+P(\rho_{0})\ \mathrm{d}x+\frac{m}{2}|\mathbf{V}_{0}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{J}\omega_{0}\cdot\omega_{0}+\frac{k_{p}}{2}|\mathbf{G}_{1}|^{2}.\end{split}

(7.10)

Moreover, if there exists
\delta\in(0,\dist(\mathbf{G}_{1},\partial\Omega)-1)
such that

\displaystyle\frac{2}{k_{p}}\left(\int_{\mathcal{F}(0)}\frac{|\mathbf{m}_{0}|^{2}}{2\rho_{0}}+P(\rho_{0})\ \mathrm{d}x+\frac{m}{2}|\mathbf{G}_{0}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{J}\omega_{0}\cdot\omega_{0}+\frac{k_{p}}{2}|\mathbf{G}_{1}-\mathbf{G}_{0}|^{2}\right)\leq\delta^{2},

(7.11)

then there exists
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(\delta)>0
such that

\dist(\mathbf{G}(t),\partial\Omega)\geq 1+\varepsilon\quad\forall t\geq 0.

(7.12)

Proof.

As
\mathbf{b}(t)=-k_{p}(\mathbf{G}(t)-\mathbf{G}_{1})-k_{d}\dot{\mathbf{G}}(t)
, we obtain

-\mathbf{b}\cdot\dot{\mathbf{G}}=k_{p}(\mathbf{G}(t)-\mathbf{G}_{1})\cdot\dot{\mathbf{G}}(t)+k_{d}|\dot{\mathbf{G}}(t)|^{2}=\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\left(\frac{k_{p}}{2}|\mathbf{G}(t)-\mathbf{G}_{1}|^{2}\right)+k_{d}|\dot{\mathbf{G}}(t)|^{2}.

(7.13)

Inserting (
7.13
) into the energy inequality (
7.9
), and using the extensions of
\rho,\mathbf{u}
as in (
2.4
), we conclude (
7.10
).

In order to establish (
7.12
), we use (
7.10
) and (
7.11
) to obtain

\displaystyle|\mathbf{G}(t)-\mathbf{G}_{1}|^{2}\leq\frac{2}{k_{p}}\left(\int_{\mathcal{F}(0)}\frac{|\mathbf{m}_{0}|^{2}}{2\rho_{0}}+P(\rho_{0})\ \mathrm{d}x+\frac{m}{2}|\mathbf{V}_{0}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{J}\omega_{0}\cdot\omega_{0}+\frac{k_{p}}{2}|\mathbf{G}_{0}-\mathbf{G}_{1}|^{2}\right)\leq\delta^{2}.

(7.14)

Finally, we conclude (
7.12
) by

\displaystyle\dist(\mathbf{G}(t),\partial\Omega)\geq\dist(\mathbf{G}_{1},\partial\Omega)-|\mathbf{G}(t)-\mathbf{G}_{1}|\geq 1+\underset{=:\varepsilon>0}{\underbrace{[\dist(\mathbf{G}_{1},\partial\Omega)-1-\delta]}}.

∎

Remark 7.10
.

Another way how to interpret (
7.11
) is as follows: If the initial center of mass of the solid is close enough to the given point
\mathbf{G}_{1}
, and the spring is stiff enough, meaning
k_{p}\gg 1
, then, regardless of its mass and the initial fluid’s energy, the solid stays close to
\mathbf{G}_{1}
for all times.

Besov spaces.

A little more advanced as the situation above is the fact that if we allow for higher regularity, then we can obtain the no-collision result even
without
the external PD-controller. To this end, for
k\in\mathbb{N}
and every
0<s<k
,
1\leq p,q<\infty
, we define the
Besov space

B^{s}_{q,p}(\Omega)
by real interpolation of Sobolev spaces as
B^{s}_{q,p}(\Omega)=(L^{q}(\Omega),W^{k,q}(\Omega))_{s/k,p}
. Besov spaces are somewhat related to Hölder spaces in the setting of Lebesgue and Sobolev spaces and can measure the “smoothness” of a function
f\in L^{q}(\Omega)
. There exist several equivalent definitions of these spaces; one of them is the following (see
[
10
]
):

Definition 7.11
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be an open set,
1\leq q<\infty
, and
f\in L^{q}(\Omega)
. Let
\mathbb{I}(f)=f
be the identity operator, and denote for any
h\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
by
\tau_{h}(f,x)=f(x+h)
the shift operator. Define

\displaystyle\bigtriangleup_{h}^{k}(f,x,\Omega)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}[\tau_{h}-\mathbb{I}]^{k}(f,x)&\text{if }x+jh\in\Omega,\ j\in\{0,...,k\},\\
0&\text{else}\,;\end{cases}

\displaystyle\omega_{k,q}(f,t,\Omega)

\displaystyle=\sup_{|h|\leq t}\|\bigtriangleup_{h}^{k}(f,\cdot,\Omega)\|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}.

For
0<s<k
and
1\leq p<\infty
, the Besov space
B_{q,p}^{s}(\Omega)
is defined as all functions
f\in L^{q}(\Omega)
such that

\displaystyle|f|_{B_{q,p}^{s}(\Omega)}^{p}=\int_{0}^{1}\left|\frac{\omega_{k,q}(f,t,\Omega)}{t^{s}}\right|^{p}\frac{{\rm d}t}{t}<\infty.

It becomes a Banach space if we equip it with the norm
\|\cdot\|_{B_{q,p}^{s}(\Omega)}=\|\cdot\|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}+|\cdot|_{B_{q,p}^{s}(\Omega)}
.

We will not give further properties of these spaces since it would go beyond the scope of this chapter, so we refer to
[
10
,
49
]
for a detailed presentation of Besov spaces. With this at hand, as in
[
27
, Theorem 1.2]
, we can establish the following result for a smooth rigid body
\mathcal{S}(t)
:

Theorem 7.12
.

Let
2<p<\infty
,
3<q<\infty
satisfy the condition
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{2q}\neq\frac{1}{2}
. Let the initial data satisfy

\rho_{0}\in W^{1,q}(\mathcal{F}(0)),\quad\mathbf{u}_{0}\in B^{2(1-1/p)}_{q,p}(\mathcal{F}(0)),\quad\inf_{\mathcal{F}(0)}\rho_{0}>0,

\mathbf{G}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{3},\quad\mathbf{V}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{3},\quad\omega_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{3},

\frac{1}{|\mathcal{F}(0)|}\int_{\mathcal{F}(0)}\rho_{0}=\bar{\rho}>0,

\mathbf{u}_{0}=0\mbox{ on }\partial\Omega,\quad\mathbf{u}_{0}=\mathbf{V}_{0}+\omega_{0}\times(y-\mathbf{G}_{0})\mbox{ on }\partial\mathcal{S}(0).

If there exists
\delta>0
and
\varepsilon>0
such that

\displaystyle\|(\rho_{0}-\bar{\rho},\mathbf{u}_{0},\mathbf{V}_{0},\omega_{0})\|_{W^{1,q}\times B^{2(1-1/p)}_{q,p}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\leq\delta,\quad\operatorname{dist}(\mathcal{S}(0),\partial\Omega)\geq\varepsilon>0,

then

\displaystyle\dist(\mathcal{S}(t),\partial\Omega)\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\mbox{ for all }t\in[0,\infty).

Smoothies.

As the last example in this chapter we mention the result for so-called multi-polar or
k
-fluids as described in
[
18
]
. These fluids are characterized by a higher-order stress tensor, hence giving higher regularity to the fluid’s velocity and, in turn, to the density. More precisely, for
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the stress tensor takes the form

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}(\nabla\mathbf{u})=\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}(-1)^{j}\Delta^{j}\bigg[\mu_{j}\bigg(\nabla\mathbf{u}+\nabla^{T}\mathbf{u}-\frac{2}{3}\div\mathbf{u}\mathbb{I}\bigg)+\eta_{j}\div\mathbf{u}\mathbb{I}\bigg],

where
\mu_{j},\eta_{j}\geq 0
,
\mu_{k-1}>0
are the (constant) viscosity coefficients, and
\Delta^{j}
is the standard Laplacian applied
j
times. In this context, classical Newtonian fluids are mono-polar, that is,
k=1
in the above. Again by the properties of Riesz transform (
1.8
), one shall expect that the velocity field
\mathbf{u}
is very regular; in particular,
\mathbf{u}\in L^{2}(0,T;W_{0}^{k,2}(\Omega))
. The density then can be shown to be bounded away from zero and infinity as long as the initial density is; hence, these types of fluids behave roughly as incompressible ones, and indeed also
\mathbf{u}\in L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))
and
\rho\in L^{\infty}((0,T)\times\Omega)\cap C([0,T];L^{1}(\Omega))
is bounded away from zero.

Since
\mathbf{u}\in L^{2}(0,T;W_{0}^{k,2}(\Omega))
, by Sobolev embedding (
1.4
) one a priori has
\mathbf{u}\in L^{2}(0,T;C_{0}^{1}(\overline{\Omega}))
as long as
k\geq 3
; in particular, the streamlines given by the unique solution of

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\mathbf{X}(t,x)=\mathbf{u}(t,\mathbf{X}(t,x)),\quad t>0,\quad\mathbf{X}(0,x)=x,

are well defined and no collision can occur.

Chapter 8
Other models

Having investigated several (in)compressible systems in two and three dimensions for smooth and non-smooth bodies, we review here the (no-)collision results for several other models:

•

Ideal incompressible fluids: Here, the viscosity is zero, and collision happens with non-zero speed.

•

One-dimensional models: We will show that points never collide.

•

Slip boundary conditions: This is a kind of roughness allowing for collision.

•

Tresca’s boundary condition: It is a variant of slip conditions, also allowing the solid to touch the container’s wall.

Without being exhaustive, we will just focus on the main ideas in the references given for the specific problem. The interested reader might consult them to get detailed versions of the statements and proofs.

8.1
Euler fluids: a perfect flow

Let us start with the description of incompressible, inviscid, and irrotational fluids and the collision result given in
[
33
]
. In their paper, however, no real PDE is stated; the whole procedure rather relies on local coordinates, the physical principle of least action, and the resulting Lagrangian. For this reason, let us recall how one can get from the common PDE formulation to the Lagrangian one, where we follow the presentation of
[
14
]
.

First, the incompressible Euler
1
1

1

after Leonhard Euler (1707–1783)
equations are given by the system

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\div\mathbf{u}=0&\text{in }(0,T)\times\Omega,\\
\rho_{\mathcal{F}}(\partial_{t}\mathbf{u}+\div(\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{u}))+\nabla p=0&\text{in }(0,T)\times\Omega,\\
\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{n}=0&\text{on }(0,T)\times\partial\Omega,\end{cases}

(8.1)

together with the usual coupling equations for the solid inside the fluid (with
\mathbb{S}=0
). The streamlines satisfy

\displaystyle\dot{\mathbf{X}}(t,x_{0})=\mathbf{u}(t,\mathbf{X}(t,x_{0})),\quad\mathbf{X}(0,x_{0})=x_{0},

for any point
x_{0}\in\Omega
, where
\dot{\mathbf{X}}=\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\mathbf{X}
. Now, as the flow is incompressible (and hence volume preserving), we have

\displaystyle\det(\nabla_{x_{0}}\mathbf{X}(t,x_{0}))=1\quad\forall(t,x_{0})\in(0,T)\times\Omega.

Further, if
\mathbf{u}
is a solution to (
8.1
), we get

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathcal{F}}\ddot{\mathbf{X}}(t,x_{0})

\displaystyle=\rho_{\mathcal{F}}\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\mathbf{u}(t,\mathbf{X}(t,x_{0}))=\rho_{\mathcal{F}}\big(\partial_{t}\mathbf{u}(t,\mathbf{X}(t,x_{0}))+\nabla_{x}\mathbf{u}(t,\mathbf{X}(t,x_{0}))\cdot\dot{\mathbf{X}}(t,x_{0})\big)

\displaystyle=-\nabla_{x}p(t,\mathbf{X}(t,x_{0})).

This is nothing else than the Euler-Lagrange equations for the Lagrangian

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(t,\mathbf{X},\dot{\mathbf{X}})=\frac{1}{2}\rho_{\mathcal{F}}|\dot{\mathbf{X}}|^{2}-p(t,\mathbf{X}).

Indeed, by least action principle, we find

\displaystyle 0=\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\dot{\mathbf{X}}}-\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial{\mathbf{X}}}=\rho_{\mathcal{F}}\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\dot{\mathbf{X}}-\frac{\partial}{\partial{\mathbf{X}}}(-p)=\rho_{\mathcal{F}}\ddot{\mathbf{X}}+\nabla p.

(8.2)

Recall also that the Lagrangian
\mathcal{L}
is physically the difference of kinetic energy (here,
\frac{1}{2}\rho_{\mathcal{F}}|\mathbf{u}|^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\rho_{\mathcal{F}}|\dot{\mathbf{X}}|^{2}
) and potential energy (here, the pressure
p
). The total energy, the so-called Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}
, is instead the
sum
of both, meaning
\mathcal{H}=\frac{1}{2}\rho_{\mathcal{F}}|\dot{\mathbf{X}}|^{2}+p=\frac{1}{2}\rho_{\mathcal{F}}|\mathbf{u}|^{2}+p
; note that this totally coincides with the energy balances derived in previous chapters. Lastly, the initial conditions read

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}(0,x_{0})=x_{0},\quad\dot{\mathbf{X}}(0,x_{0})=\mathbf{u}(0,x_{0})=\mathbf{u}_{0}(x_{0}).

(8.3)

On the other hand, given a one-parameter family
\mathbf{X}(t,x_{0})
and a function
p(t,x)
satisfying (
8.2
) and (
8.3
), we define the flow as
\mathbf{u}(t,x)=\mathbf{u}(t,\mathbf{X}(t,x_{0}))=\dot{\mathbf{X}}(t,x_{0})
. Following the lines above, we can show that the so constructed function is indeed a solution to (
8.1
). This shows that the PDE formulation and the Lagrangian formulation coincide (for a more detailed discussion and references, see
[
14
]
).

Remark 8.1
.

If the force density on the right hand side of (
8.1
)
2
is not zero, but conservative, meaning of the form
\mathbf{f}=\nabla f
for some function
f=f(t,x)
, the Lagrangian reads

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(t,\mathbf{X},\dot{\mathbf{X}})=\frac{1}{2}\rho_{\mathcal{F}}|\dot{\mathbf{X}}|^{2}-p(t,\mathbf{X})+f(t,\mathbf{X}),

which can be verified by the same arguments. In particular, for gravitational force
\mathbf{f}=-g\mathbf{e}_{3}=\nabla[x\mapsto-gx_{3}]
, we can write the Euler equations as a Lagrangian flow.

Let us come to the collision result from
[
33
, Section 5]
. The considered configuration is an infinite cylinder of radius
1
falling in half-space. By symmetry, the whole configuration reduces to a two-dimensional system, where a ball
\mathcal{S}
of radius
1
is falling in a half-plane
\Omega=\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty)
. As before, we denote by
h>0
the distance of
\mathcal{S}
to
\partial\Omega
.

For the sake of fixing notations, we set

\displaystyle\Omega=\{(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:x_{2}>0\},\quad\mathcal{S}(t)=B_{1}(\mathbf{G}(t)),\quad\mathbf{G}(t)=(0,1+h(t)),\quad\mathbf{u}|_{\mathcal{S}}=\dot{\mathbf{G}}=(0,\dot{h}).

Note that this means we consider a spherical solid moving vertically along the axis
x_{1}=0
with speed
\dot{\mathbf{G}}
and zero angular velocity. Moreover, as the fluid flow is irrotational and hence
{\rm curl}(\mathbf{u})=0
, we may find a potential
\phi
such that
\mathbf{u}|_{\mathcal{F}}=\nabla\phi
. Moreover, by the movement of the solid, this potential is linear in
\dot{h}
; in particular,
\phi=\dot{h}\varphi
, where
\varphi
solves

\displaystyle\begin{cases}-\Delta\varphi=0&\text{in }\mathcal{S}(t)\\
\nabla\varphi\cdot\mathbf{n}=1+h-x_{2}&\text{on }\partial\mathcal{S}(t),\\
\nabla\varphi\cdot\mathbf{n}=0&\text{on }\partial\Omega,\end{cases}

(8.4)

where we used that
\mathbf{n}=-r\mathbf{e}_{1}+(1+h-x_{2})\mathbf{e}_{2}
on
\partial\mathcal{S}
(see also Figure
8.2
below). In order to verify that collision may occur, we will solve system (
8.4
) explicitly by using conformal mappings and transforming the whole configuration to concentric circles; this is essentially the same idea as we used before in Chapter
7
. As it is notationally convenient for us, we will not distinguish between the complex variable
z=z_{1}+iz_{2}\in\mathbb{C}
and the vector
\mathbf{z}=(z_{1},z_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. In the same spirit, we have
\nabla_{z}f(z)=f^{\prime}(z)
for a function
f
defined on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\sim\mathbb{C}
.

We begin by transforming the whole configuration with two simple lemmata from complex analysis, the proofs of which can be found in
[
28
, §§ 5.4 and 5.6]
:

Lemma 8.2
.

Let
h>0
and

\displaystyle k(z)=\frac{z-ia}{z+ia},\quad a=\sqrt{(1+h)^{2}-1}.

Then,
k
is a conformal map from
\Omega=\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty)
to
B_{1}(0)
. Moreover, for
\mathcal{S}=B_{1}(0)
and
A_{\sigma}=B_{1}(0)\setminus\overline{B_{\sigma}(0)}
with
\sigma=(1+h+a)^{-1}
, we have the following properties:

\displaystyle k(\mathcal{S})=B_{\sigma}(0),\quad k(\Omega\setminus\mathcal{S})=A_{\sigma},\quad k(\partial\mathcal{S})=\partial B_{\sigma}(0),\quad k(\partial\Omega)=\partial B_{1}(0)\setminus\{1\}.

As for the inverse mapping, we have

\displaystyle k^{-1}(w)=ia\frac{1+w}{1-w},\quad 1+h=\frac{\sigma^{-1}+\sigma}{2},\quad a=\frac{\sigma^{-1}-\sigma}{2}.

Lemma 8.3
.

Let
U,V\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be open and
l=l_{1}+il_{2}:U\to V
be a conformal map.

1.

Let
f\in C^{2}(V)
and
F=f\circ l
. Then
F\in C^{2}(U)
and for any
w=w_{1}+iw_{2}\in U
,

\displaystyle\Delta F(w)=|l^{\prime}(w)|^{2}\Delta f(l(w)),

where
l^{\prime}(w)=\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}w}l(w)
is the complex derivative of
l
.

2.

Let
\gamma\subset U
be a
C^{1}
curve and
\mathbf{n}
be the unit normal on
\gamma
, and let
l:U\to V
be a conformal map. Similarly, let
\tilde{\gamma}=l(\gamma)\subset V
and
\tilde{\mathbf{n}}=l(\mathbf{n})
. Then, for all
w=w_{1}+iw_{2}\in U
,

\displaystyle\nabla F(w_{1},w_{2})\cdot\mathbf{n}=\bigg|\frac{\partial l}{\partial w_{1}}(w_{1},w_{2})\bigg|\ \nabla f(l(w_{1},w_{2}))\cdot\tilde{\mathbf{n}}.

As the map
k
from Lemma
8.2
is conformal, its inverse
l=k^{-1}
is as well. Thus, applying Lemma
8.3
with
U=A_{\sigma}
and
V=\Omega
to problem (
8.4
) and defining
\zeta(z)=\varphi(k^{-1}(z))
, we find

\displaystyle\nabla\zeta\cdot\mathbf{n}|_{\partial B_{\sigma}(0)}

\displaystyle=\big|\partial_{w_{1}}k^{-1}(w)\big|\nabla\varphi\cdot\mathbf{n}|_{\partial\mathcal{S}(t)}=\frac{2a}{|1-w|^{2}}(1+h-x_{2})\circ k^{-1}(w)

\displaystyle=\frac{2a(1+h-a\frac{1-\sigma^{2}}{|1-w|^{2}})}{|1-w|^{2}}=\frac{2a(2\sigma^{2}-(1+\sigma^{2})w_{1})}{\sigma(1+\sigma^{2}-2w_{1})^{2}}.

Since clearly
\nabla\zeta\cdot\mathbf{n}|_{\partial B_{1}(0)}=0
, the system for
\zeta
reads
2
2

2

We remark that in
[
33
]
, a factor
2
is missing in the boundary condition on
\partial B_{\sigma}(0)
.

\displaystyle\begin{cases}-\Delta\zeta=0&\text{in }A_{\sigma},\\
\nabla\zeta\cdot\mathbf{n}=\frac{2a(2\sigma^{2}-(1+\sigma^{2})w_{1})}{\sigma(1+\sigma^{2}-2w_{1})^{2}}&\text{for }w=w_{1}+iw_{2}\in\partial B_{\sigma}(0),\\
\nabla\zeta\cdot\mathbf{n}=0&\text{on }\partial B_{1}(0).\end{cases}

(8.5)

As
\zeta
is harmonic, there exists a harmonic complex conjugate
\xi
and the function
F=\zeta+i\xi
is holomorphic in
\Omega\setminus\mathcal{S}
and defined up to an additive constant (this is because
\zeta
solves a Neumann boundary problem). Without loss of generality, we may fix this constant to be equal to zero. The boundary conditions for
F
follow from those for
\zeta
: on
|w|=1
, we have
\mathbf{n}=w
and hence
\nabla F\cdot\mathbf{n}=F^{\prime}(w)w
. On
|w|=\sigma
, the normal is given by
\mathbf{n}=-w/\sigma
, thus
-\sigma\nabla F\cdot\mathbf{n}=F^{\prime}(w)w
. Finally, writing
w=w_{1}+iw_{2}
, (
8.5
)
2
and (
8.5
)
3
give us

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\Re[F^{\prime}(w)w]=-\frac{2a(2\sigma^{2}-(1+\sigma^{2})w_{1})}{(1+\sigma^{2}-2w_{1})^{2}}&\text{if }|w|=\sigma,\\
\Re[F^{\prime}(w)w]=0&\text{if }|w|=1,\end{cases}

where
\Re[w_{1}+iw_{2}]=w_{1}
is the real part of
w
.

Since
F
is holomorphic in
A_{\sigma}
, it admits a Laurent series expansion. More precisely, we have:

Lemma 8.4
.

For any
w\in A_{\sigma}
, the function
F
has Laurent series expansion

\displaystyle F(w)=-2a\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{\sigma^{2n}}{1-\sigma^{2n}}(w^{n}+w^{-n}).

Remark 8.5
.

Let us note that the radius of convergence of this series is
\sigma^{2}<|w|<\sigma^{-2}
, which by
\sigma<1
is a superset of
A_{\sigma}
.

Proof of Lemma
8.4
.

We start in writing

\displaystyle F(w)=\sum_{n\geq 1}(a_{n}+ib_{n})w^{n}+(c_{n}+id_{n})w^{-n}.

This gives for any
w=re^{i\theta}

\displaystyle\Re[F^{\prime}(w)w]=\sum_{n\geq 1}n[r^{n}a_{n}-r^{-n}c_{n}]\cos(n\theta)-n[r^{n}b_{n}+r^{-n}d_{n}]\sin(n\theta).

Accordingly, from the boundary conditions of
F^{\prime}(w)w
, for
r=1
we find
a_{n}=c_{n}
and
b_{n}=-d_{n}
, hence

\displaystyle\Re[F^{\prime}(w)w]=\sum_{n\geq 1}n[r^{n}-r^{-n}](a_{n}\cos(n\theta)-b_{n}\sin(n\theta)).

For
r=\sigma
, by observing
w_{1}=\sigma\cos(\theta)
we define

\displaystyle\beta(\theta)=-\frac{2a\sigma(2\sigma-(1+\sigma^{2})\cos(\theta))}{(1+\sigma^{2}-2\sigma\cos(\theta))^{2}}.

Thus,

\displaystyle\beta(\theta)=\sum_{n\geq 1}n[\sigma^{n}-\sigma^{-n}](a_{n}\cos(n\theta)-b_{n}\sin(n\theta)).

As
\beta
is an even function, we immediately have
b_{n}=0
for all
n\geq 1
. Moreover, we find

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}\beta(\theta)\cos(n\theta)\ \mathrm{d}\theta=na_{n}[\sigma^{n}-\sigma^{-n}]\int_{0}^{2\pi}\cos^{2}(n\theta)\ \mathrm{d}\theta=\pi na_{n}[\sigma^{n}-\sigma^{-n}].

Therefore,

\displaystyle a_{n}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\sigma^{n}}{n(1-\sigma^{2n})}\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\beta(\theta)\cos(n\theta)\ \mathrm{d}\theta=-\frac{\sigma^{n}}{n(1-\sigma^{2n})}\Re\bigg[\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\beta(\theta)e^{-in\theta}\ \mathrm{d}\theta\bigg]

\displaystyle=-\frac{\sigma^{n}}{n(1-\sigma^{2n})}\Re[\hat{\beta}(n)],

where
\hat{\beta}(n)
is the Fourier coefficient of
\beta
(note that the seemingly “missing” factor 2 is already included in
a_{n}
, since we consider a one-sided rather than a two-sided series expansion). It is now sufficient to calculate the Fourier coefficients of
\beta
explicitly. To this end, for
z=e^{i\theta}
, we have

\displaystyle\hat{\beta}(n)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\partial B_{1}(0)}\frac{-2a\sigma(4\sigma z-(1+\sigma^{2})(z^{2}+1))}{((1+\sigma^{2})z-\sigma(z^{2}+1))^{2}}\frac{{\rm d}z}{z^{n}}.

As we may write

\displaystyle\frac{-2a\sigma(4\sigma z-(1+\sigma^{2})(z^{2}+1))}{((1+\sigma^{2})z-\sigma(z^{2}+1))^{2}}=2a\sigma\bigg[\frac{1}{(z-\sigma)^{2}}+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\frac{1}{(z-\sigma^{-1})^{2}}\bigg],

by virtue of the residue theorem, we get

\displaystyle\hat{\beta}(n)=2an\sigma^{n}\quad\forall n\geq 1.

Plugging this expression in the one for
a_{n}
, we complete the proof.
∎

With the series expansion of
F
at hand, we can compute the kinetic energy of the fluid:

Lemma 8.6
.

The kinetic energy of the fluid inside
\Omega\setminus\mathcal{S}
is given by
E_{f}=\frac{1}{2}\rho_{\mathcal{F}}|\dot{h}|^{2}\mathcal{E}(\sigma)
, where

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\sigma)=4\pi a^{2}\sum_{n\geq 1}n\sigma^{2n}\frac{1+\sigma^{2n}}{1-\sigma^{2n}}.

Moreover,
\mathcal{E}(\sigma)
is continuous on
(0,1)
with finite strictly positive limits as
\sigma\to 0
and
\sigma\to 1
, respectively.

Proof.

We will just show the main steps of this lemma. By change of variables, we have

\displaystyle E_{f}=\frac{1}{2}\rho_{\mathcal{F}}|\dot{h}|^{2}\int_{\Omega\setminus\mathcal{S}}|\nabla\varphi|^{2}\ \mathrm{d}x=\frac{1}{2}\rho_{\mathcal{F}}|\dot{h}|^{2}\int_{A_{\sigma}}|\nabla\zeta|^{2}\ \mathrm{d}w,

where
\varphi
is the potential of the velocity, and
\zeta
is as above. Since
|\nabla\zeta|^{2}=|F^{\prime}(w)|^{2}
, we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\sigma)=\int_{A_{\sigma}}|\nabla\zeta|^{2}\ \mathrm{d}w=\int_{\sigma}^{1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}|F^{\prime}(\rho\cos\theta,\rho\sin\theta)|^{2}\rho\ \mathrm{d}\theta\ \mathrm{d}\rho.

We wish to apply Parseval’s theorem, for which we have to proof that for any
\rho\in[\sigma,1]
, the function
\theta\mapsto F^{\prime}(\rho\cos\theta,\rho\sin\theta)
is an element of
L^{2}(0,2\pi)
. Indeed, a simple calculation shows

\displaystyle|F^{\prime}(\rho\cos\theta,\rho\sin\theta)|\leq\frac{2a}{(1-\sigma)^{3}}.

Thus, Parseval’s theorem gives

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\sigma)=\pi\int_{\sigma}^{1}\sum_{n\geq 1}\bigg[\frac{2an\sigma^{2n}}{1-\sigma^{2n}}\bigg]^{2}[\rho^{2n-2}+\rho^{-2n-2}]\rho\ \mathrm{d}\rho=4\pi a^{2}\sum_{n\geq 1}n\sigma^{2n}\frac{1+\sigma^{2n}}{1-\sigma^{2n}}.

Some simple consequences from the series expansions of
(1-x)^{-1}
and
(1-x)^{-2}
, and the fact that
a=(\sigma^{-1}-\sigma)/2
then give another representation for
\mathcal{E}(\sigma)
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\sigma)=2\pi\bigg(\sum_{n\geq 1}\sigma^{2n-2}\bigg[\frac{1-\sigma^{2}}{1-\sigma^{2n}}\bigg]^{2}\bigg)-\pi.

Lebesgue’s theorem now shows that indeed
\mathcal{E}(\sigma)
is continuous on
(0,1)
with limit
\lim_{\sigma\to 0}\mathcal{E}(\sigma)=\pi
. As the summand tends to
1/n^{2}
as
\sigma\to 1
, we have also that
\mathcal{E}(\sigma)
is continuous on
[0,1]
with value
\mathcal{E}(1)=\frac{\pi^{3}}{3}-\pi
.
∎

Let us finally show how the calculations above yield the collision result. As
\sigma=\sigma(h)
, with a slight abuse of notation, the Lagrangian of our system under consideration is simply its kinetic energy, given by
3
3

3

Compared to the Lagrangian discussed previously, here we can neglect the pressure
p
since it just plays the role of a Lagrange multiplier for the incompressibility condition
\div\mathbf{u}=0
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=\frac{1}{2}m|\dot{h}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\rho_{\mathcal{F}}|\dot{h}|^{2}\mathcal{E}(h).

Hence, the least action principle yields

\displaystyle 0=\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\dot{h}}-\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial h}=\ddot{h}(m+\rho_{\mathcal{F}}\mathcal{E}(h))+\frac{1}{2}\rho_{\mathcal{F}}|\dot{h}|^{2}\frac{\partial\mathcal{E}}{\partial h}(h).

Multiplying by
2\dot{h}
gives
\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}[|\dot{h}|^{2}(m+\rho_{\mathcal{F}}\mathcal{E}(h))]=0
, this is,

\displaystyle\dot{h}=\dot{h}_{0}\sqrt{\frac{m+\rho_{\mathcal{F}}\mathcal{E}(h_{0})}{m+\rho_{\mathcal{F}}\mathcal{E}(h)}}.

(8.6)

(Note that the negative solution is indeed ruled out, since this would imply
\dot{h}(0)=-\dot{h}_{0}
.) The above equation ensures that
\dot{h}
keeps the same sign as
\dot{h}_{0}
. If we choose
\dot{h}_{0}<0
and
h_{0}>0
, then this ensures
0\leq h\leq h_{0}
. As
1+h=(\sigma^{-1}+\sigma)/2
and
\mathcal{E}(h)
is right-continuous as
h\to 0
(that is,
\sigma\to 1
), there exists
h_{0}>0
such that
\mathcal{E}(h)\leq\frac{3}{2}\mathcal{E}(0)
for all
0\leq h<h_{0}
. Hence, for any
\dot{h}_{0}<0
and any
0\leq h<h_{0}
,

\displaystyle h(t)\leq h_{0}+\dot{h}_{0}t\sqrt{\frac{m}{m+\frac{3}{2}\rho_{\mathcal{F}}\mathcal{E}(0)}}.

As always
h\geq 0
, this shows that there exists some finite
T=T(m,h_{0},\dot{h}_{0})<\infty
such that
h(t)\to 0
as
t\to T
, meaning collision happens in finite time and, additionally, with non-zero speed.

Remark 8.7
.

We remark that this collision result is in contrast to Theorem
6.1
, where collision occurs indeed with zero velocity. This shows that viscosity has some strong damping effect on the solid’s speed. Further investigation of (
8.6
) as made in
[
33
, Section 5.2]
shows that for Euler fluids, there is also a damping effect, although not that strong. As a matter of fact, for the foregoing situation such that
m=\pi\rho_{\mathcal{S}}
, even if the density ratio
\rho_{\mathcal{F}}/\rho_{\mathcal{S}}\to\infty
, the damping in an Euler fluid is not higher than
1-(\pi^{2}/3-1)^{-1/2}\approx 34\%
, meaning that the solid collides with a speed that is at least
66\%
of its initial velocity.

8.2
On a line

After investigation of inviscid fluids, let us come to a much simpler model of one-dimensional flows as described in
[
50
]
. Here, we consider
N
particles (points) on the real line having positions
h_{i}(t)
with

\displaystyle-\infty<h_{1}(t)<h_{2}(t)<...<h_{N}(t)<\infty

for some
0<t<T
. The system under consideration is the 1D Navier-Stokes system

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}u+\kappa\partial_{x}u^{2}-\partial_{xx}^{2}u=0&\text{for }x\in I_{i}(t),\ i\in\{0,...,N\},\ t>0\\
\dot{h}_{i}(t)=u(t,h_{i}(t))&\text{for }i\in\{1,...,N\},\ t>0,\\
m_{i}\ddot{h}_{i}(t)=[\partial_{x}u](t,h_{i}(t))&\text{for }i\in\{1,...,N\},\ t>0,\\
u(0,x)=u_{0}(x)&\text{for }x\in\mathbb{R},\\
h_{i}(0)=h_{i,0},\ \dot{h}_{i}(0)=\dot{h}_{i,0}&\text{for }i\in\{1,...,N\}.\end{cases}

(8.7)

Here, we denoted by
I_{i}(t)
the intervals occupied by the fluid and separated by the particles, given as

\displaystyle I_{0}(t)=(-\infty,h_{1}(t)),\quad I_{i}(t)=(h_{i}(t),h_{i+1}(t)),\ i\in\{1,...,N-1\},\quad I_{N}(t)=(h_{N}(t),\infty);

see Figure
8.1
for the main notations.

h_{1}(t)
h_{2}(t)
h_{3}(t)
h_{4}(t)
I_{0}(t)
I_{1}(t)
I_{2}(t)
I_{3}(t)
I_{4}(t)

Figure 8.1:
The configuration for
N=4
.

The mass of particle
i
is
m_{i}>0
, and the real number
\kappa
is the ratio between convection and diffusion (in processes involving heat, this is also called the Peclét number). Lastly, the jump of a function
f
is defined as

\displaystyle[f](x)=\lim_{s\to 0}[f(x+s)-f(x-s)].

As written in
[
50
, Section 1]
, “according to the transmission conditions satisfied at the point mass locations
x=h_{i}(t),\ i=1,...,N
, the velocities of the fluid and the particles coincide, and each particle is accelerated by the difference of the velocity gradient on both sides of it. Thus, the velocity gradient acts as a pressure.”

The main theorem of this section now reads:

Theorem 8.8

(
[
50
, Theorem 2.1]
)
.

Let
u_{0}\in W^{1,2}(\mathbb{R})
,
m_{i}>0
, and
h_{i,0},\dot{h}_{i,0}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\displaystyle-\infty<h_{1,0}<h_{2,0}<...<h_{N,0}<\infty,

together with the compatibility conditions
u_{0}(h_{i,0})=\dot{h}_{i,0}
for any
i\in\{1,...,N\}
. Then, there exists a unique global solution
(u,h_{1},...,h_{N})
to system (
8.7
) with

\displaystyle u\in C([0,\infty);W^{1,2}(\mathbb{R})),\quad\partial_{xx}^{2}u\in L^{2}((0,T)\times I_{i}(t)),\quad h_{i}\in C([0,\infty)),\quad\ddot{h}_{i}\in L^{2}(0,T),

for any finite time
T>0
. Moreover, for any
t>0
,

\displaystyle-\infty<h_{1}(t)<h_{2}(t)<...<h_{N}(t)<\infty,

meaning there is no collision in finite time.

For the existence proof of a strong solution, we refer to the original paper
[
50
]
. Instead, we will just show how the regularity properties of
u
imply the no-collision result. Indeed, similar to before, the dynamics of the particles are given by

\displaystyle\dot{h}_{i}(t)=u(t,h_{i}(t)),\quad i\in\{1,...,N\}.

If two points (say,
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
) collide at time
t=T>0
, then they are both solutions of the Cauchy problem

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\dot{h}_{i}(t)=u(t,h_{i}(t)),&\text{for }0\leq t<T,\ i\in\{1,2\},\\
h_{1}(T)=h_{2}(T).\end{cases}

(8.8)

Hence, to show the result, it is sufficient to prove that (
8.8
) is indeed uniquely solvable backwards in time. This in turn will follow from the fact that
u(t,h)
is Lipschitz in the second variable.

As a first step, we let the reader prove the following version of Grönwall’s inequality:

Lemma 8.9

(Grönwall’s inequality (differential form, backwards in time))
.

Let
f:[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}
be differentiable and assume there is a function
\alpha(t)\in L^{1}(0,T)
such that

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(t)\geq-\alpha(t)f(t).

Then, we have

\displaystyle f(t)\leq f(T)\exp\left(\int_{t}^{T}\alpha(s)\ \mathrm{d}s\right)

(8.9)

for almost every
t\in[0,T]
.

Next, we see

\displaystyle|\dot{h}_{1}(t)-\dot{h}_{2}(t)|=|u(t,h_{1}(t))-u(t,h_{2}(t))|\leq\|\partial_{x}u(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}(I_{1}(t))}|h_{1}(t)-h_{2}(t)|.

(8.10)

That means that if
\|\partial_{x}u(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}(I_{1}(t))}\in L^{1}(0,T)
, then an application of (
8.9
) shows that the solution to (
8.8
) is uniquely determined by the data at time
t=T
. As a matter of fact, we have even more.

Proposition 8.10
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
8.8
, it holds
\partial_{x}u\in L^{2}(0,T;L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}))
.

Proof.

As a key step in the existence proof, the authors in
[
50
, Proposition 4.1]
show that the solution
u
to (
8.7
) satisfies in the time interval
(0,T)
without collision that, for any
t\in(0,T)
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\partial_{x}u(t,x)|^{2}\ \mathrm{d}x+\sum_{i=0}^{N}\int_{0}^{T}\|\partial_{xx}^{2}u\|_{L^{2}(I_{i}(t))}^{2}\ \mathrm{d}t+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{0}^{T}m_{i}|\ddot{h}_{i}(t)|^{2}\ \mathrm{d}t\leq C,

where
C>0
depends on
T
,
\|u_{0}\|_{W^{1,2}(\mathbb{R})}
, and
\sum_{i=1}^{N}m_{i}|\dot{h}_{i,0}|^{2}
. Moreover, recall that the jump of
\partial_{x}u(t,h_{i}(t))
is precisely given by
m_{i}\ddot{h}_{i}
by (
8.7
)
3
; hence, we may decompose
\partial_{x}u
in a regular part
\partial_{x}u_{\rm reg}
and a finite number of Dirac measures located at positions
h_{i}(t)
and having amplitudes
m_{i}
. Since
\partial_{xx}^{2}u(t,\cdot)\in L^{2}(I_{i}(t))
for any
i
and any
t
, meaning
\partial_{xx}^{2}u_{\rm reg}(t,\cdot)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
for any
t\in(0,T)
, we have by Sobolev embedding (
1.4
) that
\partial_{x}u_{\rm reg}(t,\cdot)\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
. We may now impose the following interpolation inequality: for any
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
that can be decomposed in a regular part
f_{\rm reg}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and a finite number of Dirac measures with amplitudes
a_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\displaystyle\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\partial_{x}f_{\rm reg}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{\frac{1}{2}}+\sum_{i=1}^{N}|a_{i}|.

(8.11)

Choosing
f=\partial_{x}u
yields finally

\displaystyle\|\partial_{x}u\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}))}^{2}\lesssim 1+\int_{0}^{T}\|\partial_{x}u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\|\partial_{xx}^{2}u_{\rm reg}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\ \mathrm{d}t\lesssim 1.

∎

Remark 8.11
.

Inequality (
8.11
) follows by observing

\displaystyle f^{2}(x)=-\int_{-\infty}^{x}2f(t)f_{\rm reg}^{\prime}(t)\ \mathrm{d}t+\sum_{x_{i}\in(-\infty,x)}[f^{2}](x_{i}),

where
x_{i}
is the location of the Dirac measure with amplitude
a_{i}
. Moreover,

\displaystyle|[f^{2}](x_{i})|\leq 2\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}|a_{i}|.

It follows

\displaystyle\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}\leq 2\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\|f^{\prime}_{\rm reg}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}+2\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|a_{i}|.

Solving for
\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}
gives the desired.

Finally, with the help of Proposition
8.10
, we indeed find
\|\partial_{x}u\|_{L^{\infty}(I_{1}(t))}\in L^{1}(0,T)
. Hence, going back to (
8.10
), we get with Grönwall’s inequality (
8.9
)

\displaystyle|h_{1}(t)-h_{2}(t)|\lesssim|h_{1}(T)-h_{2}(T)|\ \quad\forall\,0\leq t\leq T.

As
h_{1}(T)=h_{2}(T)
, we have
h_{1}\equiv h_{2}
, thus showing that the ODE (
8.8
) is uniquely solvable; in turn, no collision can occur.

8.3
Slip-boundary conditions

Let us go back to three-dimensional flows. The paradox that a ball does not collide its boundary clearly contradicts our physical intuition and experience. To resolve this issue, one can take a closer look to the physical behavior of fluids on surfaces, or, mathematically speaking, the boundary conditions of the fluid velocity. Indeed, the common no-slip condition
\mathbf{u}|_{\partial\Omega}=0
, although very close to reality, is just an approximation to a more complex slip-condition

\displaystyle(\mathbf{u}-\dot{\mathbf{G}}(t))\cdot\mathbf{n}|_{\partial\mathcal{S}}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle(\mathbf{u}-\dot{\mathbf{G}}(t))\times\mathbf{n}|_{\partial\mathcal{S}}

\displaystyle=-\beta_{\mathcal{S}}[\mathbb{S}(\mathbf{u})\mathbf{n}]\times\mathbf{n}|_{\partial\mathcal{S}},

(8.12)

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{n}|_{\partial\Omega}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}\times\mathbf{n}|_{\partial\Omega}

\displaystyle=-\beta_{\Omega}[\mathbb{S}(\mathbf{u})\mathbf{n}]\times\mathbf{n}|_{\partial\Omega},

(8.13)

where the slip coefficients
\beta_{\mathcal{S}},\beta_{\Omega}\geq 0
represent the slip lengths on the solid’s and container’s boundary, respectively. Typically, those slip lengths are very small of order
10^{-9}-10^{-6}
m (see, e.g.,
[
7
,
48
]
), so it is convenient to simply set them equal to zero and recover the no-slip conditions. However, as we shall show in the sequel, the fact that they are
not equal
to zero allows for collision even for a ball-shaped solid. In a nutshell, our main result for slip-boundary conditions reads as follows.

Theorem 8.12
.

Let
\mathcal{S}=B(\mathbf{G}(t))\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
be the unit ball centered at
\mathbf{G}(t)
with
\dist(\mathcal{S}(0),\partial\Omega)>1
. Let
\mathbf{u}
be a solution to the incompressible Stokes equations (
4.1
) with stress tensor
\mathbb{S}=2\mu\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{u})
that complies with the slip-boundary conditions (
8.12
)–(
8.13
). Let moreover the fluid’s density
\rho_{\mathcal{F}}
be smaller than the solid’s one, meaning
\rho_{\mathcal{S}}>\rho_{\mathcal{F}}>0
.

•

If both
\beta_{\mathcal{S}}>0
and
\beta_{\Omega}>0
, collision occurs in finite time.

•

If at least one of the coefficients
\beta_{\mathcal{S}}
and/or
\beta_{\Omega}
vanishes, then the solid stays away from
\partial\Omega
for all times.

In other words, a ball can just collide a wall if
all
surfaces are slippery.

The problem of collision with slip boundary conditions was investigated in
[
23
,
24
,
25
]
, and we follow the presentation of
[
25
]
. The ideas to prove Theorem
8.12
are basically the same as for no-slip conditions, however, the test function now has to comply with the slip boundary conditions. This will complicate both its form as well as the computations to be done.

8.3.1
Construction of test function

As before, we want to construct a proper test function for the drag force. Due to the slip-conditions, the energy functional now reads

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{h}=\int_{\mathcal{F}_{h}}|\nabla\mathbf{u}|^{2}\ \mathrm{d}x+(1+\beta_{\mathcal{S}}^{-1})\int_{\partial\mathcal{S}_{h}}|(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{e}_{3})\times\mathbf{n}|^{2}\ \mathrm{d}\sigma+\beta_{\Omega}^{-1}\int_{\partial\Omega}|\mathbf{u}\times\mathbf{n}|^{2}\ \mathrm{d}\sigma,

which formally can be obtained by multiplying the momentum equation by
\mathbf{u}
and integrating by parts. Again, we search for a function of the form
\mathbf{w}_{h}=\nabla\times(\phi_{h}\mathbf{e}_{\theta})=-\partial_{3}\phi_{h}\mathbf{e}_{r}+\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}(r\phi_{h})\mathbf{e}_{3}
for some function
\phi_{h}
to be determined. As we saw in previous chapters, the convective part does not really play a role in whether or not the solid can collide with its container; hence, in the formulation of the boundary conditions, we can replace
\dot{\mathbf{G}}
simply by
\dot{h}\mathbf{e}_{3}
as in the linear case for Stokes equations. Skipping the index
h
for brevity, the Euler-Lagrange equation for
\mathcal{E}
is again
\partial_{3}^{4}\phi(r,x_{3})=0
, thus

\displaystyle\phi(r,x_{3})=a(r)+b(r)x_{3}+c(r)x_{3}^{2}+d(r)x_{3}^{3}.

To determine the boundary conditions of
\phi
, from which we will find the functions
a,b,c
, and
d
, let us first note that the normal on
\partial\Omega\cap\partial\Omega_{h,r_{0}}
is simply
\mathbf{n}=-\mathbf{e}_{3}
, and, since
\mathcal{S}
is a ball of radius one, the normal on
\partial\mathcal{S}\cap\partial\Omega_{h,r_{0}}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathbf{n}=-r\mathbf{e}_{r}+\sqrt{1-r^{2}}\mathbf{e}_{3}.

x_{3}
(r,\theta)
1
\mathbf{n}
r
\sqrt{1-r^{2}}
\mathbf{e}_{r}
\mathbf{e}_{3}
\otimes\,\mathbf{e}_{\theta}

Figure 8.2:
The normal
\mathbf{n}
on
\partial\mathcal{S}\cap\partial\Omega_{h,r_{0}}
.

With this at hand, we compute

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{w}\cdot\mathbf{n}|_{\partial\Omega}=\Big(-\partial_{3}\phi\mathbf{e}_{r}+\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}(r\phi)\mathbf{e}_{3}\Big)(r,0)\cdot\mathbf{e}_{3}=\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}(r\phi)(r,0),

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{w}-\mathbf{e}_{3})\cdot\mathbf{n}|_{\partial\mathcal{S}}=\Big(-\partial_{3}\phi\mathbf{e}_{r}+\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}(r\phi)\mathbf{e}_{3}-\mathbf{e}_{3}\Big)(r,\psi)\cdot(-r\mathbf{e}_{r}+\sqrt{1-r^{2}}\mathbf{e}_{3})

\displaystyle=r\partial_{3}\phi(r,\psi)+\frac{\sqrt{1-r^{2}}}{r}\partial_{r}(r\phi)(r,\psi)-\sqrt{1-r^{2}}.

Noting that for the ball case, where the shape function is given by
\psi=\psi_{h}(r)=1+h-\sqrt{1-r^{2}}
, we see that

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime}=\frac{r}{\sqrt{1-r^{2}}},

\displaystyle\sqrt{1+|\psi^{\prime}|^{2}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-r^{2}}}=\frac{1}{r}\psi^{\prime}.

Hence, the impermeability condition on
\partial\mathcal{S}
reduces to

\displaystyle 0=r\partial_{3}\phi(r,\psi)+\frac{\sqrt{1-r^{2}}}{r}\partial_{r}(r\phi)(r,\psi)-\sqrt{1-r^{2}}=\frac{1}{\psi^{\prime}}\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}r}\bigg[r\phi(r,\psi)-\frac{r^{2}}{2}\bigg],

that is,

\displaystyle\phi(r,\psi)=\frac{r}{2}+\frac{\kappa}{r},\quad\kappa\in\mathbb{R}.

In order to get a smooth function up to the origin, we again choose
\kappa=0
. We moreover note that from the impermeability condition on
\partial\mathcal{S}
, we find

\displaystyle 0=\partial_{3}\phi+r\partial_{r3}^{2}\phi-\frac{1}{r^{2}\sqrt{1-r^{2}}}\partial_{r}(r\phi)+\frac{\sqrt{1-r^{2}}}{r}\partial_{rr}^{2}(r\phi)+\frac{r}{\sqrt{1-r^{2}}}.

Hence, as
\phi(r,\psi)=r/2
, we see that

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{r^{2}\sqrt{1-r^{2}}}\partial_{r}(r\phi)+\frac{\sqrt{1-r^{2}}}{r}\partial_{rr}^{2}(r\phi)+\frac{r}{\sqrt{1-r^{2}}}=-\frac{r}{r^{2}\sqrt{1-r^{2}}}+\frac{\sqrt{1-r^{2}}}{r}+\frac{r}{\sqrt{1-r^{2}}}=0,

in turn,

\displaystyle\partial_{r3}^{2}\phi(r,\psi)=-\frac{1}{r}\partial_{3}\phi(r,\psi).

(8.14)

We will need this relation later on.

Similarly, the no-penetration condition on
\partial\Omega
leads to

\displaystyle\phi(r,0)=0,

giving rise to
a(r)=0
. Considering now incompressible Newtonian fluids with
\div\mathbf{u}=0
and
\mathbb{S}=\mu(\nabla\mathbf{u}+\nabla^{T}\mathbf{u})=2\mu\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{u})
, we infer from the slip boundary conditions that on
\partial\Omega

\displaystyle\mu\beta_{\Omega}\partial_{33}^{2}\phi(r,0)-\partial_{3}\phi(r,0)

\displaystyle=0,

(8.15)

see Section
8.3.3
for its derivation. This yields
b(r)=2\mu\beta_{\Omega}c(r)
, hence

\displaystyle\phi(r,x_{3})=c(r)(2\mu\beta_{\Omega}x_{3}+x_{3}^{2})+d(r)x_{3}^{3}.

As moreover
\phi(r,\psi)=r/2
, we find

\displaystyle c(r)(2\mu\beta_{\Omega}\psi+\psi^{2})+d(r)\psi^{3}=\frac{r}{2}\ \Rightarrow\ d(r)=\frac{1}{\psi^{3}}\bigg(\frac{r}{2}-c(r)(2\mu\beta_{\Omega}\psi+\psi^{2})\bigg),

in turn,

\displaystyle\phi(r,x_{3})=c(r)\bigg(2\mu\beta_{\Omega}x_{3}+x_{3}^{2}-\frac{2\mu\beta_{\Omega}+\psi}{\psi^{2}}x_{3}^{3}\bigg)+\frac{r}{2}\bigg(\frac{x_{3}}{\psi}\bigg)^{3}.

(8.16)

The missing coefficient
c(r)
will be determined from the last boundary condition on
\partial\mathcal{S}
. One finds (see Section
8.3.3
)

\displaystyle 0=\partial_{33}^{2}\phi+\bigg(2+\frac{1}{\mu\beta_{\mathcal{S}}}\bigg)\frac{\sqrt{1-r^{2}}}{1-2r^{2}}\partial_{3}\phi,

(8.17)

which yields

\displaystyle c(r)=\frac{3r}{2\psi}\frac{2\mu\beta_{\mathcal{S}}+\big(2\mu\beta_{\mathcal{S}}+1\big)\frac{\sqrt{1-r^{2}}}{1-2r^{2}}\psi}{(4\mu\beta_{\Omega}+\psi)(4\mu\beta_{\mathcal{S}}+\big(2\mu\beta_{\mathcal{S}}+1\big)\frac{\sqrt{1-r^{2}}}{1-2r^{2}}\psi)-4\mu^{2}\beta_{\Omega}\beta_{\mathcal{S}}}.

Note that in the limit
\beta_{\Omega},\beta_{\mathcal{S}}\to 0
representing no-slip conditions, we obtain

\displaystyle c(r)\to\frac{3r}{2\psi^{2}}

and hence

\displaystyle\phi(r,x_{3})\to\frac{3r}{2\psi^{2}}\bigg(x_{3}^{2}-\frac{x_{3}^{3}}{\psi}\bigg)+\frac{r}{2}\frac{x_{3}^{3}}{\psi^{3}}=\frac{r}{2}(3t^{2}-2t^{3})|_{t=\frac{x_{3}}{\psi}}

as expected. Moreover, we may represent
\phi(r,x_{3}\psi)=\frac{r}{2}[P_{1}(r)x_{3}+P_{2}(r)x_{3}^{2}+P_{3}(r)x_{3}^{3}]
with

\displaystyle P_{1}(r)=\frac{6(2+\alpha_{\mathcal{S}})}{12+4(\alpha_{\mathcal{S}}+\alpha_{\Omega})+\alpha_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha_{\Omega}},\qquad P_{2}(r)=\frac{3(2+\alpha_{\mathcal{S}})\alpha_{\Omega}}{12+4(\alpha_{\mathcal{S}}+\alpha_{\Omega})+\alpha_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha_{\Omega}},

\displaystyle P_{3}(r)=-\frac{2(\alpha_{\mathcal{S}}+\alpha_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha_{\Omega}+\alpha_{\Omega})}{12+4(\alpha_{\mathcal{S}}+\alpha_{\Omega})+\alpha_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha_{\Omega}},

\displaystyle\alpha_{\Omega}=\frac{1}{\mu\beta_{\Omega}}\psi,\qquad\alpha_{\mathcal{S}}=\bigg(2+\frac{1}{\mu\beta_{\mathcal{S}}}\bigg)\frac{\sqrt{1-r^{2}}}{1-2r^{2}}\psi.

Remark 8.13
.

Let us notice that the form of
\alpha_{\mathcal{S}}
in
[
25
]
does not match the one above, namely, the coefficient
\sqrt{1-r^{2}}/(1-2r^{2})
is missing there. This is due to the fact that for small
r
, this fraction is bounded below and above (and even close to
1
), hence the
qualitative
behavior of the function does not change and one might neglect it.

8.3.2
Uniform estimates, corresponding pressure, and proof of Theorem
8.12

As for the case of no-slip, we need some bounds of the constructed test function, as well as an additional pressure catching the singularities of
\Delta\mathbf{w}_{h}
. This is done in the following Lemmata:

Lemma 8.14
.

Let
\beta_{\Omega},\beta_{\mathcal{S}}>0
. For the function
\mathbf{w}_{h}
constructed in the previous section, there holds

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{w}_{h}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{F}_{h})}+\|\beta_{\mathcal{S}}[\mathbb{S}(\mathbf{w}_{h})\mathbf{n}]\times\mathbf{n}+(\mathbf{w}_{h}-\mathbf{e}_{3})\times\mathbf{n}\|_{L^{2}(\partial\mathcal{S}_{h})}

\displaystyle\lesssim 1,

\displaystyle\|\nabla\mathbf{w}_{h}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{F}_{h})}^{2}\lesssim|\log h|

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{w}_{h})\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{F}_{h})}^{2}.

Moreover, denoting
x=(r\cos\theta,r\sin\theta,x_{3})
,

\displaystyle\left\|\int_{0}^{\psi}\partial_{h}\mathbf{w}_{h}(r,s)\ \mathrm{d}s\right\|_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega\cap\partial\Omega_{h,r_{0}})}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim|\log h|,

\displaystyle\left\|\int_{x_{3}}^{\psi}\partial_{h}\mathbf{w}_{h}(r,s)\ \mathrm{d}s\right\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{h,r_{0}})}+\left\|\psi\nabla\int_{x_{3}}^{\psi}\partial_{h}\mathbf{w}_{h}(r,s)\ \mathrm{d}s\right\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{h,r_{0}})}

\displaystyle\lesssim 1,

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\overline{\Omega}_{h,r_{0}}}\left|\int_{x_{3}}^{\psi}\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{w}_{h})(r,s)\ \mathrm{d}s\right|+\sup_{x\in\overline{\Omega}_{h,r_{0}}}\left|\psi\nabla\int_{x_{3}}^{\psi}\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{w}_{h})(r,s)\ \mathrm{d}s\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim 1.

Lemma 8.15
.

There exists a pressure
q_{h}
satisfying

\displaystyle|\log h|\lesssim\int_{\partial\mathcal{S}_{h}}(\mathbb{S}(\mathbf{w}_{h})-q_{h}\mathbb{I})\mathbf{n}\cdot(\mathbf{e}_{3}-\mathbf{w}_{h})\ \mathrm{d}\sigma-\int_{\partial\Omega}(\mathbb{S}(\mathbf{w}_{h})-q_{h}\mathbb{I})\mathbf{n}\cdot\mathbf{w}_{h}\ \mathrm{d}\sigma\lesssim|\log h|,

and for any
\mathbf{v}\in W^{1,2}(\mathcal{F}_{h})
with
\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{n}=0
on
\partial\Omega
, it holds

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathcal{F}_{h}}(\Delta\mathbf{w}_{h}-\nabla q_{h})\cdot\mathbf{v}\ \mathrm{d}x\right|\lesssim\|\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{v})\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{F}_{h})}+\|\mathbf{v}\|_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}.

As a matter of fact, the pressure will be determined as before, meaning

\displaystyle q_{h}(r,x_{3})=\partial_{r3}^{2}\phi_{h}(r,x_{3})-\int_{0}^{r}\partial_{333}^{3}\phi_{h}(t,x_{3})\ \mathrm{d}t.

The same calculations as made for the no-slip case (but using the above constructed “slip-test-function”) yield the desired bounds; we therefore do not repeat them and refer to
[
25
, Appendix A]
for details.

Also, the proof of Theorem
8.12
follows essentially the same lines as for the no-slip case, but this time using the weaker (logarithmic) bounds obtained in Lemma
8.14
. Repeating the steps done in Chapter
6.2
, the final outcome is an inequality of the form

\displaystyle\mu\int_{h_{0}}^{h(T)}\mathcal{D}(s)\ \mathrm{d}s+\frac{4\pi}{3}(\rho_{\mathcal{S}}-\rho_{\mathcal{F}})gT\leq C_{0}(1+\sqrt{T})

(8.18)

for some generic constant
C_{0}>0
that is independent of time, where the
drag

\mathcal{D}
is the energy of the test function
\mathbf{w}_{h}
, given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(h)=\mathcal{E}_{h}(\mathbf{w}_{h})=\int_{\mathcal{F}_{h}}|\nabla\mathbf{w}_{h}|^{2}\ \mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle+(1+\beta_{\mathcal{S}})^{-1}\int_{\partial\mathcal{S}_{h}}|(\mathbf{w}_{h}-\mathbf{e}_{3})\times\mathbf{n}|^{2}\ \mathrm{d}\sigma

\displaystyle+\beta_{\Omega}^{-1}\int_{\partial\Omega}|\mathbf{w}_{h}\times\mathbf{n}|^{2}\ \mathrm{d}\sigma.

This formula is the counterpart to (
6.11
) for slip boundary conditions. Moreover, the bounds obtained for
\mathbf{w}_{h}
yield a drag force of order

\displaystyle|\mathcal{D}(h)|\lesssim|\log h|,

hence

\displaystyle\int_{h_{0}}^{h(T)}\mathcal{D}(s)\ \mathrm{d}s\gtrsim-\int_{0}^{\sup\limits_{t\in(0,T)}h(t)}|\mathcal{D}(s)|\ \mathrm{d}s\gtrsim-1.

(8.19)

As the fluid’s density is smaller than the solid’s one, (
8.18
)–(
8.19
) yield collision in finite time.

For the mixed case, we focus just on
\beta_{\mathcal{S}}=0
and
\beta_{\Omega}>0
. The reverse case follows similar lines. Rigorously, one has to re-define the test function
\mathbf{w}_{h}
, and estimate its corresponding norms accordingly; however, it turns out that this is equivalent in taking the limit
\alpha_{\mathcal{S}}\to\infty
(respectively
\beta_{\mathcal{S}}\to 0
) in the definition of
\mathbf{w}_{h}
, and hence the corresponding norms change as one shall expect. This limit procedure yields as in the no-slip case

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{w}_{h}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{F}_{h})}^{2}+h\|\nabla\mathbf{w}_{h}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{F}_{h})}^{2}\lesssim 1

\displaystyle\lesssim h\|\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{w}_{h})\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{F}_{h})}^{2},

and for the drag force, one gets

\displaystyle\int_{h_{0}}^{h(T)}\mathcal{D}(s)\ \mathrm{d}s\lesssim 1+T,

yielding

\displaystyle|\log h(T)|\lesssim 1+T

and hence collision cannot occur. More details regarding the calculations can be found in
[
25
, Section 4]
.

8.3.3
Appendix: tangential slips

Let us show how the equations (
8.15
) and (
8.17
) arise. First, we recall the gradient in cylindrical coordinates:

Lemma 8.16
.

We have

\displaystyle\nabla=\mathbf{e}_{r}\partial_{r}+\frac{1}{r}\mathbf{e}_{\theta}\partial_{\theta}+\mathbf{e}_{3}\partial_{3}

as well as

\displaystyle\nabla\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{e}_{r}\otimes\nabla(\mathbf{w}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{r})+\mathbf{e}_{\theta}\otimes\nabla(\mathbf{w}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{\theta})+\mathbf{e}_{3}\otimes\nabla(\mathbf{w}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{3})+\frac{1}{r}\big((\mathbf{w}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{r})\mathbf{e}_{\theta}-(\mathbf{w}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{\theta})\mathbf{e}_{r}\big)\otimes\mathbf{e}_{\theta}.

Recalling
\phi=\phi(r,x_{3})
is independent of the angle
\theta
, as well as
\mathbf{w}=-\partial_{3}\phi\mathbf{e}_{r}+\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}(r\phi)\mathbf{e}_{3}
, we find

\displaystyle\nabla\mathbf{w}

\displaystyle=-\partial_{r3}^{2}\phi\mathbf{e}_{r}\otimes\mathbf{e}_{r}-\partial_{33}^{2}\phi\mathbf{e}_{r}\otimes\mathbf{e}_{3}-\frac{1}{r}\partial_{3}\phi\mathbf{e}_{\theta}\otimes\mathbf{e}_{\theta}+\partial_{r}\bigg(\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}(r\phi)\bigg)\mathbf{e}_{3}\otimes\mathbf{e}_{r}+\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}(r\partial_{3}\phi)\mathbf{e}_{3}\otimes\mathbf{e}_{3}.

Hence, using also that
(\mathbf{a}\otimes\mathbf{b})\cdot\mathbf{c}=(\mathbf{b}\cdot\mathbf{c})\mathbf{a}
, we get

\displaystyle 2\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{w})\mathbf{n}

\displaystyle=-2\partial_{r3}^{2}\phi(\mathbf{e}_{r}\cdot\mathbf{n})\mathbf{e}_{r}+\bigg[\partial_{r}\bigg(\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}(r\phi)\bigg)-\partial_{33}^{2}\phi\bigg]\big((\mathbf{e}_{r}\cdot\mathbf{n})\mathbf{e}_{3}+(\mathbf{e}_{3}\cdot\mathbf{n})\mathbf{e}_{r}\big)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{2}{r}\partial_{r}(r\partial_{3}\phi)(\mathbf{e}_{3}\cdot\mathbf{n})\mathbf{e}_{3}-\frac{2}{r}\partial_{3}\phi(\mathbf{e}_{\theta}\cdot\mathbf{n})\mathbf{e}_{\theta}.

In turn, we have on
\partial\Omega
where
\mathbf{n}=-\mathbf{e}_{3}
and by the relation
\mathbf{e}_{r}\times\mathbf{e}_{3}=-\mathbf{e}_{\theta}

\displaystyle 2[\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{w})\mathbf{n}]\times\mathbf{n}|_{\partial\Omega}=\bigg[\partial_{33}^{2}\phi-\partial_{r}\bigg(\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}(r\phi)\bigg)\bigg]\mathbf{e}_{\theta}.

Taking into account that
\mathbb{S}(\mathbf{w})=2\mu\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{w})
and that on
\partial\Omega
, we have
\phi(r,0)=0
, this yields

\displaystyle 0=(\beta_{\Omega}[\mathbb{S}(\mathbf{w})\mathbf{n}]+\mathbf{w})\times\mathbf{n}|_{\partial\Omega}=[\mu\beta_{\Omega}\partial_{33}^{2}\phi-\partial_{3}\phi](r,0)\mathbf{e}_{\theta},

which is precisely (
8.15
). Similarly, on
\partial\mathcal{S}
where
\mathbf{n}=-r\mathbf{e}_{r}+\frac{r}{\psi^{\prime}}\mathbf{e}_{3}
, we find

\displaystyle(\mathbf{e}_{r}\cdot\mathbf{n})\mathbf{e}_{r}\times\mathbf{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{r^{2}}{\psi^{\prime}}\mathbf{e}_{\theta},

\displaystyle(\mathbf{e}_{r}\cdot\mathbf{n})\mathbf{e}_{3}\times\mathbf{n}

\displaystyle=r^{2}\mathbf{e}_{\theta},

\displaystyle(\mathbf{e}_{3}\cdot\mathbf{n})\mathbf{e}_{r}\times\mathbf{n}

\displaystyle=-\bigg(\frac{r}{\psi^{\prime}}\bigg)^{2}\mathbf{e}_{\theta},

\displaystyle(\mathbf{e}_{3}\cdot\mathbf{n})\mathbf{e}_{3}\times\mathbf{n}

\displaystyle=-\frac{r^{2}}{\psi^{\prime}}\mathbf{e}_{\theta}.

Hence,

\displaystyle 2[\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{w})\mathbf{n}]\times\mathbf{n}|_{\partial\mathcal{S}}=\bigg[-2\partial_{r3}^{2}\phi\frac{r^{2}}{\psi^{\prime}}+\bigg[\partial_{r}\bigg(\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}(r\phi)\bigg)-\partial_{33}^{2}\phi\bigg]\bigg(r^{2}-\frac{r^{2}}{|\psi^{\prime}|^{2}}\bigg)-2\partial_{r}(r\partial_{3}\phi)\frac{r}{\psi^{\prime}}\bigg]\mathbf{e}_{\theta}.

Moreover,

\displaystyle(\mathbf{w}-\mathbf{e}_{3})\times\mathbf{n}=\bigg[\partial_{3}\phi\frac{r}{\psi^{\prime}}-(\partial_{r}(r\phi)-r)\bigg]\mathbf{e}_{\theta}.

Using the specific form
\phi(r,\psi)=r/2
on
\partial\mathcal{S}
, this yields

\displaystyle(\mathbf{w}-\mathbf{e}_{3})\times\mathbf{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{r}{\psi^{\prime}}\partial_{3}\phi\mathbf{e}_{\theta},

\displaystyle 2[\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{w})\mathbf{n}]\times\mathbf{n}

\displaystyle=-\bigg[2\partial_{r3}^{2}\phi\frac{r^{2}}{\psi^{\prime}}+\partial_{33}^{2}\phi\bigg(r^{2}-\frac{r^{2}}{|\psi^{\prime}|^{2}}\bigg)+2\partial_{r}(r\partial_{3}\phi)\frac{r}{\psi^{\prime}}\bigg]\mathbf{e}_{\theta},

in turn,

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=[(\mathbf{w}-\mathbf{e}_{3})\times\mathbf{n}+2\mu\beta_{\mathcal{S}}[\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{w})\mathbf{n}]\times\mathbf{n}]\cdot\mathbf{e}_{\theta}

\displaystyle=\frac{r}{\psi^{\prime}}\partial_{3}\phi-\mu\beta_{\mathcal{S}}\bigg[2\partial_{r3}^{2}\phi\frac{r^{2}}{\psi^{\prime}}+\partial_{33}^{2}\phi\bigg(r^{2}-\frac{r^{2}}{|\psi^{\prime}|^{2}}\bigg)+2\partial_{r}(r\partial_{3}\phi)\frac{r}{\psi^{\prime}}\bigg]

\displaystyle=-\partial_{33}^{2}\phi\mu\beta_{\mathcal{S}}\bigg(r^{2}-\frac{r^{2}}{|\psi^{\prime}|^{2}}\bigg)+\frac{r}{\psi^{\prime}}\bigg(\partial_{3}\phi(1-2\mu\beta_{\mathcal{S}})-4r\mu\beta_{\mathcal{S}}\partial_{r3}^{2}\phi\bigg).

Using (
8.14
), we get for the last term

\displaystyle\partial_{3}\phi(1-2\mu\beta_{\mathcal{S}})-4r\mu\beta_{\mathcal{S}}\partial_{r3}^{2}\phi=\partial_{3}\phi(1-2\mu\beta_{\mathcal{S}})+4\mu\beta_{\mathcal{S}}\partial_{3}\phi=\partial_{3}\phi(1+2\mu\beta_{\mathcal{S}}).

By (
8.16
), we find

\displaystyle\partial_{3}\phi(r,\psi)

\displaystyle=-c(4\mu\beta_{\Omega}+\psi)+\frac{3r}{2\psi},

\displaystyle\partial_{33}^{2}\phi(r,\psi)

\displaystyle=-4c\frac{3\mu\beta_{\Omega}+\psi}{\psi}+\frac{3r}{\psi^{2}}.

Hence, recalling
\psi=1+h-\sqrt{1-r^{2}}
, we are left with

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=-\mu\beta_{\mathcal{S}}\partial_{33}^{2}\phi\bigg(r^{2}-\frac{r^{2}}{|\psi^{\prime}|^{2}}\bigg)+(1+2\mu\beta_{\mathcal{S}})\frac{r}{\psi^{\prime}}\partial_{3}\phi

\displaystyle=\mu\beta_{\mathcal{S}}(1-2r^{2})\left[\partial_{33}^{2}\phi+\bigg(2+\frac{1}{\mu\beta_{\mathcal{S}}}\bigg)\frac{\sqrt{1-r^{2}}}{1-2r^{2}}\partial_{3}\phi\right]

and consequently

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=-4c\frac{3\mu\beta_{\Omega}+\psi}{\psi}+\frac{3r}{\psi^{2}}+\bigg(2+\frac{1}{\mu\beta_{\mathcal{S}}}\bigg)\frac{\sqrt{1-r^{2}}}{1-2r^{2}}\bigg(-c(4\mu\beta_{\Omega}+\psi)+\frac{3r}{2\psi}\bigg)

\displaystyle=-c\bigg(4\frac{3\mu\beta_{\Omega}+\psi}{\psi}+\bigg(2+\frac{1}{\mu\beta_{\mathcal{S}}}\bigg)\frac{\sqrt{1-r^{2}}}{1-2r^{2}}(4\mu\beta_{\Omega}+\psi)\bigg)+\frac{3r}{\psi^{2}}+\bigg(2+\frac{1}{\mu\beta_{\mathcal{S}}}\bigg)\frac{\sqrt{1-r^{2}}}{1-2r^{2}}\frac{3r}{2\psi},

so finally

\displaystyle c

\displaystyle=\frac{\frac{3r}{\psi^{2}}+\big(2+\frac{1}{\mu\beta_{\mathcal{S}}}\big)\frac{\sqrt{1-r^{2}}}{1-2r^{2}}\frac{3r}{2\psi}}{4\frac{3\mu\beta_{\Omega}+\psi}{\psi}+\big(2+\frac{1}{\mu\beta_{\mathcal{S}}}\big)\frac{\sqrt{1-r^{2}}}{1-2r^{2}}(4\mu\beta_{\Omega}+\psi)}

\displaystyle=\frac{3r}{2\psi}\frac{2\mu\beta_{\mathcal{S}}+\big(2\mu\beta_{\mathcal{S}}+1\big)\frac{\sqrt{1-r^{2}}}{1-2r^{2}}\psi}{(4\mu\beta_{\Omega}+\psi)(4\mu\beta_{\mathcal{S}}+\big(2\mu\beta_{\mathcal{S}}+1\big)\frac{\sqrt{1-r^{2}}}{1-2r^{2}}\psi)-4\mu^{2}\beta_{\Omega}\beta_{\mathcal{S}}}.

8.4
Tresca’s boundary condition

Letting the fluid slip on the boundary makes collision happen, however, this model is not realistic in the sense that the fluid slips regardless of the boundary’s shear. Another model to handle this issue are the so-called Tresca
4
4

4

after Henri Tresca (1814–1885)
boundary conditions as a version of slip boundary conditions, but dependent on the rate of shear exerted by the fluid. More precisely, as stated in
[
32
]
, “[in] these boundary conditions, the fluid sticks to the interface up to a shear-rate threshold that the fluid is prevented to exceed by allowing slip on the interface. The boundaries of the fluid domain split then in a zone of small shear rates where Dirichlet boundary conditions are imposed and high shear rates where a type of Navier boundary conditions are imposed (but with an unknown slip length which encodes that the shear rate cannot
exceed the threshold value).” We shall show that under such modified slip conditions, collision can still occur, provided the body is close enough to its container’s wall and its mass is large enough.

To precise the presentation, we focus on the following setting: let
\Omega=(-L,L)\times(0,L^{\prime})\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
L>1,\ L^{\prime}>2
, and let the solid
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}_{h}=B_{1}((h+1)\mathbf{e}_{2})
be the unit disk centred as
(h+1)\mathbf{e}_{2}
. Denote as usual
\mathcal{F}=\Omega\setminus\mathcal{S}
. The equations governing the fluid’s and solid’s motion are given by

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\rho_{\mathcal{F}}(\partial_{t}\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{u}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{u})-\div\mathbb{S}+\nabla p=0&\text{in }\mathcal{F},\\
\div\mathbf{u}=0&\text{in }\mathcal{F},\\
m\ddot{h}=-\int_{\partial\mathcal{S}}(\mathbb{S}(\nabla\mathbf{u})-p\mathbb{I})\mathbf{n}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{2}\ \mathrm{d}\sigma-(m-\pi\rho_{\mathcal{F}})g,\\
h(0)=h_{0},\ \dot{h}(0)=\dot{h}_{0},\ \mathbf{u}(0,\cdot)=\mathbf{u}_{0}&\text{in }\mathcal{F}(0),\end{cases}

where
\mathbb{S}=2\mu\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{u})=\mu(\nabla\mathbf{u}+\nabla^{T}\mathbf{u})
, and
m=\pi\rho_{\mathcal{S}}
is the solid’s mass. The no-penetration boundary conditions are as before

\displaystyle(\mathbf{u}-\dot{h}\mathbf{e}_{2})\cdot\mathbf{n}|_{\partial\mathcal{S}}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{n}|_{\partial\Omega}

\displaystyle=0.

For the tangential parts, this time we have on
\partial\mathcal{S}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}(\mathbf{u}-\dot{h}\mathbf{e}_{2})\times\mathbf{n}=0&\text{if }|[\mathbb{S}\mathbf{n}]\times\mathbf{n}|<\varsigma^{\ast},\\
\exists\beta\geq 0:\ (\mathbf{u}-\dot{h}\mathbf{e}_{2})\times\mathbf{n}=-\beta[\mathbb{S}\mathbf{n}]\times\mathbf{n}&\text{if }|[\mathbb{S}\mathbf{n}]\times\mathbf{n}|\geq\varsigma^{\ast},\end{cases}

and similar on
\partial\Omega

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\mathbf{u}\times\mathbf{n}=0&\text{if }|[\mathbb{S}\mathbf{n}]\times\mathbf{n}|<\varsigma^{\ast},\\
\exists\beta\geq 0:\ \mathbf{u}\times\mathbf{n}=-\beta[\mathbb{S}\mathbf{n}]\times\mathbf{n}&\text{if }|[\mathbb{S}\mathbf{n}]\times\mathbf{n}|\geq\varsigma^{\ast}.\end{cases}

Here, the
shear threshold

\varsigma^{\ast}>0
, and without loss of generality, we may set
\varsigma^{\ast}=1
in the sequel.

Introducing the function

\displaystyle\mathbf{w}^{\ast}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{e}_{2}&\text{on }\partial\mathcal{S},\\
0&\text{on }\partial\Omega,\end{cases}

these boundary conditions can be shortly written as

\displaystyle\begin{cases}(\mathbf{u}-\dot{h}\mathbf{w}^{\ast})\cdot\mathbf{n}|_{\partial\mathcal{F}}=0,&\\
(\mathbf{u}-\dot{h}\mathbf{w}^{\ast})\times\mathbf{n}|_{\partial\mathcal{F}}=0&\text{if }|[\mathbb{S}\mathbf{n}]\times\mathbf{n}|<1,\\
\exists\beta\geq 0:\ (\mathbf{u}-\dot{h}\mathbf{w}^{\ast})\times\mathbf{n}|_{\partial\mathcal{F}}=-\beta[\mathbb{S}\mathbf{n}]\times\mathbf{n}|_{\partial\mathcal{F}}&\text{if }|[\mathbb{S}\mathbf{n}]\times\mathbf{n}|\geq 1.\end{cases}

(8.20)

The proof that contact occurs relies on several estimates including an adapted Korn inequality and bounds obtained from the energy inequality. These bounds then enable the authors in
[
32
]
to prove the following two statements:

Lemma 8.17

(
[
32
, Lemma 2.5]
)
.

Let
\dot{h}_{0}=0
,
\mathbf{u}_{0}\in L^{2}(\mathcal{F}(0))
with
\div\mathbf{u}_{0}=0
, and
\rho_{\mathcal{F}}>0
be given. As long as
h\leq 1
, there holds

\displaystyle\dot{h}(t)\leq-\frac{gt}{2}+C^{\sharp}gh_{0}+\frac{C^{\flat}}{m}\int_{0}^{t}|\log h(s)|\ \mathrm{d}s,

where the constants
C^{\sharp},C^{\flat}>0
are independent of
m
and
h_{0}
.

Lemma 8.18

(
[
32
, Lemma 2.6]
)
.

Let
\sigma\in(0,1/2)
and define a sequence of times
(t_{n})_{n\geq 0}
via

\displaystyle\begin{cases}t_{0}=\frac{1}{4}\sqrt{\frac{h_{0}}{g}},\\
t_{n+1}=t_{n}+\sigma\frac{h(t_{n})}{2\sqrt{gh_{0}}}.\end{cases}

This sequence is well defined for
h_{0}
sufficiently small and
mh_{0}
sufficiently large. Moreover, there holds

\displaystyle\frac{h_{0}}{2}(1-\sigma)^{n}\leq h(t_{n})\leq\frac{3}{2}h_{0}\Big(1-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{32}\Big)^{n}.

(8.21)

Similar to the chapters before, the proof of Lemma
8.17
relies on the construction of a suitable test function
\mathbf{w}_{h}
for the momentum equation satisfying

\displaystyle\div\mathbf{w}_{h}=0\ \text{ in }\ \mathcal{F},\qquad(\mathbf{w}_{h}-\mathbf{w}^{\ast})\cdot\mathbf{n}|_{\partial\mathcal{F}}=0,\qquad[\mathbb{S}(\nabla\mathbf{w}_{h})\mathbf{n}]\times\mathbf{n}|_{\partial\Omega}=0,

and a corresponding pressure
q_{h}
(see
[
32
, Section 3]
for details). The proof of Lemma
8.18
is given by induction and an application of Lemma
8.17
. The terms “sufficiently small” and “sufficiently large” are interpreted as

\displaystyle h_{0}<\max\left\{\frac{2}{3(1+\sigma)},\frac{1}{(32C^{\sharp})^{2}g}\right\}

and

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{32}\sqrt{gh_{0}}+\frac{3C^{\flat}\sigma}{4m}\sqrt{\frac{h_{0}}{g}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\Big(1-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{32}\Big)^{k}\Big|\log\Big[(1-\sigma)^{k+1}\frac{h_{0}}{2}\Big]\Big|\leq-\frac{\sigma}{16}\sqrt{gh_{0}},

which ensure that
h(t)\in(0,1)
for all
t\in[0,t_{n}]
and all
n\geq 0
, and also that
h(t)
satisfies (
8.21
). The final step is now to see that the definition of
t_{n}
, together with (
8.21
), ensures that the sequence
(t_{n+1}-t_{n})_{n\geq 0}
is bounded from above by a convergent geometric series. In turn, we have
\lim_{n\to\infty}t_{n}=T_{\ast}<\infty
, and additionally
\lim_{n\to\infty}h(t_{n})=0
by (
8.21
). Since
h
is continuous, this shows
h(T_{\ast})=0
, meaning collision happens, and the maximal existence time of the solution is in this case finite.

Remark 8.19
.

Note carefully that the assumptions of a large mass and zero initial speed fit, to some extend, the assumptions of Theorem
2.2
for the compressible setting with no-slip boundary conditions. This might be interpreted in such a way that for compressible fluids, the non-constant density plays the role of a kind of “pseudo-slip”, hence allowing for collision.

Some bibliographical remarks

Among the literature cited in this work, there is a bunch of works dealing with different situations, although most of them are focussed on incompressible fluids. We will give here some literature, and refer the interested reader to the works cited in there. First, let us mention the article
[
29
]
, where the author summarizes the up-to-date known results of collisions for incompressible Newtonian fluids with no-slip boundary conditions. In
[
5
]
, the authors show existence for incompressible fluids even in the presence of contacts, where no-slip boundary conditions are imposed on the container’s wall, and Navier slip boundary conditions are imposed on the solid, extending the work of
[
9
,
24
]
, where existence was shown
up to collision
. Existence of strong solutions for incompressible two-dimensional fluid-solid interaction with no-slip boundary conditions and a solid of class
C^{2}
was proven in
[
47
]
, where either the maximal existence time
T_{\ast}=\infty
, or collision occurs in finite time. For a deformable structure in two dimensions, global existence of strong solution was shown in
[
26
]
. Short time existence for elastic structures has been shown in
[
2
]
, and in
[
8
]
for quite high regularity assumptions on the container’s and solid’s boundary.

Besides the examples of roughness given in previous chapters, the authors in
[
23
, Section 4]
investigated the case of a corrugated container’s wall, that is, for some given smooth
1
-periodic function
\Gamma:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to(-\infty,0]
with
\max_{(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Gamma(x_{1},x_{2})=\Gamma(0,0)=0
and some given
\varepsilon>0
, we have

\displaystyle\partial\Omega\cap(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(-\infty,0])=\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:x_{3}=\varepsilon\Gamma\left(\frac{x_{1}}{\varepsilon},\frac{x_{2}}{\varepsilon}\right)\right\}.

Focussing just on the case
\varepsilon\ll h\ll 1
, the authors “quote that this is the only regime for which this modelling of the roughness is relevant. Indeed, when
h
becomes comparable to
\varepsilon
, a rescaling in space by a factor
1/\varepsilon
brings back to the classical situation of smooth boundaries. From this point of view, the model we consider in this section is peculiar: it does not allow to conclude anything about the possibility of collisions for a given small roughness size
\varepsilon
.”

Concerning the other parameters of the configuration, they assumed
\mathcal{S}
to be a ball of unit radius, and no-slip boundary conditions on every part of the boundary. The outcome of their computations is a drag force of order

\displaystyle\frac{6\pi}{h+\lambda\varepsilon}+\mathcal{O}(|\log(h+\lambda\varepsilon)|)\leq\mathcal{D}_{h}\leq\frac{6\pi}{h}+\mathcal{O}(|\log h|),\qquad\lambda=-\min_{(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Gamma>0,

which matches the experimental results found in
[
35
,
51
]
. In this context, let us also cite
[
38
]
, where the authors investigate the drag force of a sphere falling over a corrugated wall. Moreover, the references in there show that for a flat bottom, we have
\mathcal{D}_{h}\sim h^{-1}
, hence giving the experimental verification of our foregoing calculations.
